# Sūrah al-Anʿām (Chapter 6) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**The Manifesto of Pure Monotheism and Cosmic Sovereignty**

Al-Anʿām, "The Cattle," is the sixth chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 165 verses, and it represents the first major Makkan sūrah encountered in the codex arrangement. It takes its name from verses 136–144, which systematically dismantle the arbitrary pagan taboos and superstitions about livestock—superstitions by which the polytheists consecrated certain animals to their idols and forbade others to women. The commentators, including Ibn ʿAbbās, recorded that Sūrah al-Anʿām was revealed all at once in Makkah during a single night, accompanied by an escort of seventy thousand angels glorifying Allah, so immense was its religious gravity. Where the preceding Medinan sūrahs (al-Baqarah through al-Mā'idah) elaborated communal legislation, civil statutes, and covenantal relations with the People of the Book, al-Anʿām returns with volcanic power to the bedrock of faith: the absolute unicity of God (*tawḥīd*), the reality of resurrection (*baʿth*), the repudiation of polytheism (*shirk*), and the defense of prophetic revelation against obstinate skepticism.

**The Shape of the Sūrah**

The movement of al-Anʿām unfolds as a relentless, rhythmic dialectic between cosmic evidence and divine interrogations. The opening movement (1–32) establishes God as the sole Creator of darkness and light, warns against taking divine signs lightly, exposes the psychology of denial, and depicts the sorrowful regret of the deniers when stood before the Fire. The second movement (33–73) consoles the Prophet, asserting that his opponents do not reject him personally but deny the signs of Allah, while presenting the cosmic panorama of divine omniscience: *“With Him are the keys of the unseen; none knows them except Him. And He knows what is on the land and in the sea; not a leaf falls but that He knows it”* (6:59). The third movement (74–90) introduces the intellectual and spiritual climax of the sūrah: the dramatic narrative of Abraham using celestial observation—the setting of the star, the moon. The sun—to show the absurdity of worshipping ephemeral, declining phenomena, declaring: *“I have turned my face toward Him who created the heavens and the earth, truly devout, and I am not of the polytheists”* (6:79), followed by the grand roll-call of eighteen prophets united in a single line of guidance. The fourth movement (91–140) attacks the Quraysh's failure to estimate Allah with His true measure (*qadr*), refutes their concocted dietary taboos and infant sacrifice, and contrasts their fabricated customs against the true lawful provisions granted by the Creator. The fifth movement (141–153) provides the supreme moral charter of Islam: the Ten Universal Commandments of verses 151–153, which prohibit association of partners with God, mandate honor to parents, forbid infanticide and indecency, protect orphan wealth, and demand absolute justice in weighing and speech. The epilogue (154–165) contrasts the sectarian fractures of human religions against the pure, undivided faith of Abraham, culminating in the triumphant personal manifesto of verse 162: *“Say, 'Indeed, my prayer, my rites of sacrifice, my living and my dying are for Allah, Lord of the worlds.'”*

The Quraysh were demanding physical miracles—that an angel descend visibly, or that a parchment inscribed by God fall from the sky into their hands (6:7–8).

---

## Verse 6:1

> All praise is for Allah Who created the heavens and the earth and made darkness and light. Yet the disbelievers set up equals to their Lord ˹in worship˺

**Praise Opens a Case It Does Not Yet Argue**

Praise is given to God before the dispute is named, and that placement is already an argument. The sūrah opens with the formula *al-ḥamdu li-llāh*, and four other sūrahs open the same way, each introducing the claim it will spend itself defending: the Lord of all worlds (1:2), the One Who revealed the Book to His servant (18:1), the Owner of all that is in the heavens and the earth (34:1). The Originator who made angels messengers with wings (35:1). Praise in these openings is not decoration set above an argument; it establishes jurisdiction. Whoever made the heavens holds the title deed to them, and no court higher than the Maker exists.

**Darkness in the Plural, Light in the Singular**

Arabic says *ẓulumāt* for darkness, a plural, and *nūr* for light, a singular. The Book keeps both forms: believers are brought out of darkness into light, and those who follow false gods are led out of light into darkness (2:257); guidance brings people out of darkness into light by God's will (5:16); and the question is put plainly, can darkness and light be equal (13:16)? God is named the Light of the heavens and the earth (24:35), and never the darkness of anything.

**A Charge They Had Already Answered Against Themselves**

The verse closes on the disbelievers setting up equals to their Lord, and the Book treats that charge as self-inflicted. Ask them who created the heavens and the earth and they answer, "Allah" (29:61); the same answer is given in 31:25, and in 43:9 they credit the Almighty, All-Knowing. Commentators report that many Makkans acknowledged a supreme Creator while keeping their idols as intermediaries, which is why the argument keeps returning to creation: the confession is already on their lips, and the practice contradicts it.

An equal shares the rank, not necessarily the workshop. The Quraysh did not claim their stones made the rain; they claimed the rain needed a recommendation. The verse answers that borrowed authority with its own premise: the One who made both the rain and the light did not appoint a partner to receive the request.

**The Sūrah Reported to Have Come Down Whole**

Ibn ʿAbbās, as reported by al-Ṭabarānī in *al-Muʿjam al-Kabīr* (12/215) and by al-Ḥākim (2/314), said the entire sūrah was revealed in Makkah at night, accompanied by seventy thousand angels raising their voices in glorification; the version in al-Bayhaqī (1/365) says the angels filled the horizon. The chains have been disputed — several variations are weak, and some scholars judge the report ḥasan — while the unity of the sūrah's argument is accepted by most who study its sequence.

That report is worth the pause because it says something about the verse in front of us. The opening is not a fragment that later grew a context; it stands at the head of one continuous case, argued from creation to worship, from Abraham to the dietary rules, without a change of subject.

Early commentators noticed that this opening resembles the first lines of the Torah, where God creates the heavens and the earth and then separates light from darkness (Genesis 1:1–5).

---

## Verse 6:2

> He is the One Who created you from clay, then appointed a term ˹for your death˺ and another known only to Him ˹for your resurrection˺—yet you continue to doubt

**Two Terms Standing in One Sentence**

The verse distinguishes two appointments. The first is decreed and named as such; the second is said to rest with God alone, known to Him. Most commentators read the first as a person's earthly lifespan and the second as the moment of gathering and reckoning, though some take the second as a further description of the first. Cross-references settle the pattern: God calls back souls by night and revives their owners daily "to complete your appointed term" (6:60); the term for a community cannot be moved forward or delayed by anyone (7:34); and no soul dies except by God's will at the time written for it (3:145).

Read as two terms, the verse maps a life in two directions at once: it ends, and afterwards it is answered for. Its audience had no quarrel with the first claim. Every family buried its dead. Adam is described as formed from clay more than a dozen times in the Book, and the material is named in several ways: sounding clay moulded from black mud (15:26), clay shaped like pottery (55:14), an extract of clay (23:12). Whatever the images change, the substance stays lowly and comes from the ground under the audience's feet. The verse puts that dust and a schedule in one breath.

**The Right to Keep a Date Secret**

"Another known only to Him" is not a gap in divine knowledge but a decision about human access. The hour of death is hidden from the person carrying it; the day of return is hidden from the whole generation. The Book returns to that concealment often, and treats it as a structure rather than a cruelty: if a person knew the date, the years before it would turn into accounting, and virtue into arithmetic. A merchant told his bankruptcy day would behave differently on the last month.

Cross-references place the hidden date beside the unavoidable one. Term after term is said to be written, immovable, and unannounced: for each community there is an appointed term, and when it comes nobody can delay it a moment (10:49); God never delays a soul when its appointed time arrives (63:11). The certainty of the first term is what makes the secrecy of the second a test instead of a riddle.

**The Certain Term and the Hour That Refuses to Be Read**

Two facts about death are stated together in later passages and hold the verse's structure in place. Death will overcome a person wherever he is, "even if you were in fortified towers" (4:78), so the first term is not negotiable and no wall deflects it. The knowledge of the Hour, by contrast, is reserved: God knows it, and no soul knows what it will earn tomorrow or in what land it will die (31:34). That pairing — a guaranteed end with an unreadable date — is the working assumption of a serious life, and every soul will taste death (3:185) without being told the morning.

People build substitutes for the hidden date: a healthy body, a strong clan, a full granary. The verse refuses every substitute by naming the Maker of the body and the appointer of the term in one clause. The only calendar a person truly owns is the present, which is why the sūrah moves next to what people do with the signs they can already read.

---

## Verse 6:3

> He is the Only True God in the heavens and the earth. He knows whatever you conceal and whatever you reveal, and knows whatever you do

**The One Place God Is Named God of Both Realms**

This sentence has no exact twin in the Book: nowhere else is God described as the God in the heavens and the earth. The phrase "heavens and the earth" runs through the sūrah like a refrain (6:1, 3, 12, 13, 14, 73, 75, 79, 95, 101), but here the two halves of creation are named as the two halves of one jurisdiction. Some earlier commentators, uneasy that naming God "in the earth" might compromise His transcendence, explained the phrase as meaning that He is called God and worshipped as God in both realms. Others left the wording as it stands: God in the heavens and the God on the earth (43:84).

Either reading answers Makkan practice. Idols divided the world into districts — a god for the sanctuary, a god for the rain, a star for the season, a jinn for the valley. The verse collapses the map. A deity with a local office is a deity with neighbours, and the verse grants the neighbours nothing.

**Knowledge That Reaches the Hidden**

The second half of the verse tightens the sentence into a searchlight. Whatever you conceal and whatever you reveal are known; so is whatever you do. That is not a general remark about God's omniscience but a specific answer to the postures this sūrah is about to describe: arguments made in private, doubts kept behind a courteous face, mockery rehearsed among friends and withheld from the Prophet. Whether you hide what is in your hearts or disclose it, it is known to God (3:29); and God will call people to account for what they hid or revealed (2:284).

**Knowledge That Counts Deeds**

The Arabic word for what a person "does" throughout the Book carries the sense of earning, of acquiring a result. The verse therefore joins knowledge to a ledger rather than to a mood: God knows not only what was done but what it purchased. Whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it (99:7), and the same rule runs the other way. There is no unnoticed act in the Book, and no act without a receipt.

**The Same Claim in Other Words**

Two other passages state the knowledge clause so closely that they read like annotations. In one sūrah the sentence is almost bare: "And Allah knows what you conceal and what you reveal" (16:19). In another the contrast is drawn between speech that is secret and speech that is public, with the heart placed underneath both: whether you speak secretly or openly, He knows best what is hidden in the heart (67:13). The wording varies, the extent does not.

Later in the sūrah the same claim is argued from the roof of the created order rather than asserted (6:75–79), where Abraham watches the stars, the moon, and the sun rise and set and refuses each of them on the grounds that a thing which disappears is not a lord.

---

## Verse 6:4

> Whenever a sign comes to them from their Lord, they turn away from it

**Turning Away Is a Decision, Not a Failure to Notice**

The Arabic verb behind "turn away" is *iʿrāḍ*, the act of turning one's side toward someone — a deliberate social gesture. The Book never treats it as inattention, and it never treats it as an accident. Who does more wrong than the one who is reminded of Allah's revelations and then turns away from them, and who is met with punishment for it (32:22)? The question assumes that both halves of the act are the person's own: the reminder came, and the turning was chosen.

**Signs Already Standing in Their Own Landscape**

What had reached the Makkans already? The Book answers with the world they walked through. The dawn is caused to break and the night is made for rest, the sun and moon travelling with precision (6:96). The stars are set as guides through the darkness of land and sea (6:97); and the alternation of day and night, the rain that revives dead ground, and the winds that shift across the sky are all counted among the signs for people of understanding (2:164).

**The Demand for Proof as Postponement**

Alongside the signs already granted sits a standing request for something more impressive. The disbelievers ask why no sign has been sent down from his Lord (13:7), and they are told that signs belong to God and the warner has only his warning (29:50). Other demands are recorded in the same register, and not one of them is a request for information: each asks for a spectacle whose acceptance or rejection would settle the question in public.

The verse quietly explains the demand. A person who has resolved to turn away needs a reason that arrives after the refusal rather than before it, and an absent miracle is exactly that: a reason that cannot be examined. The sign already delivered has one disadvantage for the refuser — it can be believed today, and today is inconvenient. A request for evidence that keeps being renewed is therefore not a question awaiting an answer. It is a way of holding the question open, and the Book names the tactic plainly when it says the demanders have no knowledge and that their predecessors asked the same thing because their hearts are alike (2:118).

**When the Turning Becomes a Policy**

The Book records the refusal in its organised form, and the record is blunt. A group of them advised one another not to listen to the recitation of the Qur'an, but to drown it out with noise so that they might prevail (41:26). That is not a man shrugging off an argument; that is a meeting deciding that the argument must not be heard at all. Once the decision is made collectively, the sign loses its chance before it is delivered, and the listeners are protected from the very thing the verse says kept arriving.

Those with no knowledge ask for a sign to be sent to them, or for God to speak to them directly (2:118), and the Book notes that earlier generations said the same thing, since hearts resemble one another.

The same construction appears where the Book reports that whatever new reminder comes from the Most Compassionate, they always turn away from it (26:5), and where it says the messengers were mocked before this Prophet and the mockers were then overtaken by the thing they had mocked (21:41).

---

## Verse 6:5

> They have indeed rejected the truth when it came to them, so they will soon face the consequences of their ridicule

**"What They Used to Mock" Comes Back as Its Own Answer**

A formula runs through the Book: the mockers are overwhelmed by the very thing they used to mock. It appears when punishment is expected to be delayed (11:8), it appears as a consolation to the mocked Prophet (21:41), and it appears twice in the account of what happens when the consequences of deeds unfold before their eyes (39:48; 45:33). The repetition is not rhetorical padding; it is a doctrine about the relationship between contempt and consequence.

**What They Were Mocking Was Resurrection**

The specific joke in Makkah is recorded elsewhere in the Book. They ask, once we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected (23:82)? They say mockingly that bones and ashes will be raised as a new creation (17:49), and they argue about who will give life to decayed bones (36:78). The tone in each report is the tone of people who think the claim is not only unlikely but absurd, and the subject of the joke is the one event that would end it.

**Mockery Was the Standing Response to Every Messenger**

The Prophet is not being handed an unique hardship. A line repeated with small variations across the Book says that no messenger came to a people without being mocked (15:11; 36:30; 43:7). The sūrah of the Heights puts the complaint in the mouths of the mockers themselves, taunting whether this is the one who speaks ill of their gods (21:36), and the earliest Makkan reaction to revelation was to call the messenger a clear magician (10:2). Mockery is the oldest costume in the audience's wardrobe, and every generation has found that it fits.

The repetition serves two purposes here. It supplies evidence for the charge of rejection, since the treatment received by this Prophet matches the treatment recorded of messengers before him, and it locates the fault in the response rather than in the message. The Book says as much in its own voice: it is not your honesty that is being questioned, but the signs of God that the wrongdoers deny (6:33). The joke is aimed at the messenger and lands on the message, which is why the next clause of the verse treats ridicule as a debt with a due date.

**Two Readings of the Punishment**

Not everyone restricted the tidings to a battlefield. Some early commentators held that the punishment in view is an earthly one, since the line of the verse concerns the affairs of its hearers, while others read it as covering either this world or the next, since nothing in the wording limits where the account is settled. The second position rests on the repeated use of the same phrase for the reversal that unfolds in the Hereafter (39:48; 45:33).

Addressing an audience that denies the very assembly the Hereafter assumes, the sūrah reaches for a proof they can still witness, and the reports of Badr show that the Quraysh themselves came to read that defeat as something more than a commercial setback.

When the messengers came with clear proofs, the ones who had been proud of their own knowledge were the ones destroyed (40:83), and the Book gives the same sequence twice over when it reports people turning from every new reminder and then being overtaken by what they mocked (26:5–6).

---

## Verse 6:6

> Have they not seen how many ˹disbelieving˺ peoples We destroyed before them? We had made them more established in the land than you. We sent down abundant rain for them and made rivers flow at their feet. Then We destroyed them for their sins and replaced them with other peoples

**A Question Put to People Who Had Seen the Ruins**

The sentence begins as an appeal to sight. "Have they not seen" asks the Quraysh to notice what was already lying along their roads: societies destroyed for persisting in wrongdoing, "leaving them in total ruin" with abandoned wells and lofty palaces (22:45). The Book repeats the invitation because this audience did travel. Similar situations came to pass before you, so travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers (3:137); the earlier peoples were superior in might and cultivated the land more than these Meccans ever have (30:9).

**The Loan That Looked Like a Deed**

Rain and rivers feel like permanent property to those who inherit them. The Book treats them as monthly instalments on an account that will be closed. Those who disbelieve should not imagine that a longer life is good for them; they are only given more time, and a punishment follows (3:178). God draws such people on gradually in ways they cannot understand, delaying their end while His own planning stays flawless (68:44–45).

Pharaoh read the streams flowing at his feet as proof of his sovereignty (43:51) and was answered by the sea. Water is the last asset a tyrant thinks can be repossessed, and the verse makes the repossessing part of the record. The owners of the irrigation were removed; the irrigation was not.

**The Word Behind "Peoples"**

The Arabic term translated as peoples is *qarn*, a word that names a generation, a stretch of decades, and also a horn — the animal's weapon. The Book uses the term for the peoples destroyed after Noah (17:17), for the luxurious generations wiped out before this one (19:74), and for the societies of wrongdoers replaced by others (21:11).

A *qarn* is not a tribe on a map and not a nation-state with borders; it is a run of human beings bound together by a shared period, defined by the time they occupied rather than by anything they chose.

**Sin Treated as a Public Debt**

The verse says they were destroyed "for their sins," and it says it about a people rather than about individuals. The Book often works this way when it speaks of communities: many are the societies destroyed for persisting in wrongdoing, left in total ruin (22:45); whole societies far superior in might to the Prophet's were destroyed with none to help them (47:13). The wrong is corporate because the policy was corporate, made in a council and enforced by a majority that found the practices convenient.

This is not a replacement for personal responsibility, which the sūrah asserts elsewhere in the sharpest terms. It is a statement that a civilisation can be a decision rather than an accident. The audience is told that Allah would never wrong such peoples, but that they wronged themselves (30:9) — the ruin is the return of their own choice, arriving in its own time.

Another generation was raised after them (23:31), and those who abandon faith are answered with a people whom God will love and who will love Him (5:54).

Even the generations who inherited the Scripture and repeated their predecessors' offences were allowed to continue for a while (7:169), which shows that the mechanism in this verse is not a single episode of punishment but a standing order of things.

---

## Verse 6:7

> Had We sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺ a revelation in writing and they were to touch it with their own hands, the disbelievers would still have said, “This is nothing but pure magic!”

**A Condition Settled Before It Happens**

The verse grants the demand of the doubters and then cancels the result in advance. Let the revelation come as a physical book, let it be placed in their hands, let the fingers confirm the paper: the verdict is already prepared. This is a counterfactual aimed at a specific complaint. The disbelievers asked why the Qur'an had not been sent down all at once (25:32), why the Prophet had not been given the like of what Moses received (28:48), and they set belief on a book they could read with their own eyes (17:93).

By answering the demand hypothetically, the verse exposes what the demand was doing. A request that can be satisfied in principle and dismissed in practice was never a test of the evidence; it was a way of keeping the question open while the answer was postponed.

**The Verdict That Was Waiting: Sorcery**

What the disbelievers would have said is recorded elsewhere in almost the same words. A group of them say of the revelation that it is nothing but pure magic (37:15). When Moses came with clear signs, they called it conjured tricks and denied that their forefathers had heard of such a thing (28:36). Pharaoh accused Moses of coming to drive them out of their land with magic (20:57); and the first Makkan reaction to revelation was to call the messenger a clear magician (10:2).

Sorcery is the perfect refuse bin for inconvenient evidence. It requires no engagement with content, no refutation, and no counter-argument, and it carries a social penalty for anyone who takes the message seriously. Once the category exists, any proof can be dropped into it.

**The Book Keeps Company With Other Impossible Requests**

The written scripture is one item in a cluster of demands, and the cluster is revealing. Alongside "let him bring us a book we can read" sits the wish for a treasure to be sent down with the messenger or an angel to accompany him as a co-warner (11:12), and the complaint that the messenger eats food and walks in the markets like everyone else (25:7). Different requests, same structure: each is satisfied by changing the messenger or the medium, and none enquires into what the message actually says.

Read together, they describe an audience shopping for a proof it has already decided to reject. That is why the answer in this verse can be granted hypothetically without cost to the argument. The Book is not defending an inadequate sign; it is showing that the objectors would have found the same sentence for a better one, and that the sentence was prepared before the sign arrived.

**The Line That Splits Two Kinds of Hearers**

Read carefully, the verse is addressed as much to the person who says he would believe with better evidence. If a written book delivered to the hand would not have converted the Quraysh, then the modern complaint that revelation is too old, or too mediated, or too unlike anything drawn from a laboratory, is the same complaint in newer clothing. The Book states the principle plainly: the hearts are all alike (2:118).

---

## Verse 6:8

> They say, “Why has no ˹visible˺ angel come with him?” Had We sent down an angel, the matter would have certainly been settled ˹at once˺, and they would have never been given more time ˹to repent˺

**A Demand That Sounds Like Devotion**

The request for an angel is one of the oldest objections in the Book, and it comes in several shapes. Some ask why an angel is not sent down with the warner rather than a man (11:12); others say that if only angels had been sent to them they would have believed. One passage reports the full boast, that they would not believe until a stair into heaven arrived, a house of gold, or a book they could read (17:93); another records the impatient demand to see God and the angels face to face (25:21). Angels, in all of these, are treated as the missing credential.

What the demand quietly assumes is that the messenger's authority is public property, requiring certification by a witness the audience has chosen. The verse treats the assumption as the real subject.

**"The Matter Would Have Been Settled"**

The reply contains a phrase the Book uses for the end of history. When the angel descends, the matter is decided — the root is the same as the Day of Regret, when all matters will be settled while people remain heedless (19:39). A sign of that order does not open a debate; it closes one. There would be no further inquiry, no further excuse, and no interval in which to reconsider.

That is why the answer says they would have been granted no more time to repent. The visible angel was never on offer as a piece of evidence for a trial period. It arrives with the verdict attached, and the verdict arrives in the same instant.

**The Mercy They Were Standing Inside**

There is a harder reading of the verse, and it follows from what the sūrah has already said. God has taken upon Himself to be Merciful, forgiving those who commit evil in ignorance and then repent (6:54). The absence of the angel was the space that mercy required. A generation that complained about the missing angel was complaining about the very interval in which it was still able to change.

That is the bitterness of the verse. What the Makkans counted as an insult — no angel sent to honour them — was the condition of their continued survival. The sign they demanded would have removed the chance they were using to demand it.

**The Awful Thing They Were Asking For**

The request carries an implication its speakers had not thought through. A sign that settles the matter at once is a verdict, and the verdict is not one they wanted read out in their own marketplace. The Prophet in the same sūrah is instructed to say that he truly fears the torment of a tremendous Day if he disobeys his Lord (6:15) — a man who fears the judgment is being mocked by people who are asking to be judged.

Hypocrisy is the wrong word for it, since the demanders were sincere in their unbelief.

The Book elsewhere describes the modes by which revelation reaches a human being: inspiration, speech from behind a veil, or a messenger-angel sent with God's permission (42:51).

---

## Verse 6:9

> And if We had sent an angel, We would have certainly made it ˹assume the form of˺ a man—leaving them more confused than they already are

**An Angel Would Have Needed a Human Face**

The reply to the demand continues with a condition that cancels it. Suppose an angel had been dispatched; he would have arrived wearing the shape of a man. Commentators explain why: the unveiled angelic form is not something human beings can stand before, and its appearance would overwhelm or destroy those who saw it. Mercy therefore dictates the costume. The Book records one such descent in detail, when the angel was sent to Mary and appeared before her as a man, perfectly formed (19:17).

**Guests Who Arrived as Men**

Angels do visit human beings in the Book, and they arrive as people. The story of Abraham's honoured guests is introduced as an event the Prophet is asked about (51:24), and the same visitors are called guests in the sūrah of al-Ḥijr (15:51). They came to a household, sat with a host, and were offered food; nothing in the scene required a witness to see anything but travellers.

That consistency matters for this verse. The rule the Makkans were complaining about is the rule the Book applies everywhere: when an angelic messenger comes to human beings in this world, he comes in human shape. The visible angel of their demand was a being they had never met, in a form no one had ever seen.

**The Objection Cancels Itself**

If the angel looks like a man, the Makkans' own leaders could say what they had said about the Prophet: that this too was only a human being. The complaint about the messenger's species survives every version of the sign, because the species of the messenger was never the difficulty.

The Book elsewhere reports this exact dismissal of human messengers — a message delivered by someone who eats food and walks in the markets for a living (25:7).

**The Angel Who Did Come, Dressed as a Man**

The hypothetical is not the whole story, and the Book supplies the rest. The angel Gabriel brought the revelation down to the Prophet's heart by God's will (2:97); the trustworthy spirit carried it down (26:193); the holy spirit brought it from God with the truth to reassure the believers (16:102). The angelic messenger did come, and he came to a human being in the manner this verse describes. The most detailed account of such an arrival is the report in which Jibrīl sat among the Companions as a man no one recognised, with clothes too white and hair too dark for a traveller, and questioned the Prophet about submission, faith, and worshipping God as though seeing Him. Afterwards the Prophet told them that this was Jibrīl, who had come to teach them their religion (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 8).

The contrast is exact. What the Makkans wanted was an angel as a public spectacle they could set terms for. What the Book describes is an angel as a teacher in human clothing, sent to a particular man with a particular message, and known to be an angel only after he had left.

Later in the Book the principle is stated in one line: had there been angels walking the earth, well settled, God would have sent down from heaven an angel as a messenger for them (17:95).

---

## Verse 6:10

> ˹Other˺ messengers had already been ridiculed before you ˹O Prophet˺, but those who mocked them were overtaken by what they used to ridicule

**A Consolation Older Than the Grief**

The Prophet is told that the mockery reaching him is a treatment with precedents. The Book repeats the fact with small changes: no messenger came to a people without being mocked (15:11); the same is recorded of the prophets generally (43:7); and the disbelievers, on seeing the Prophet, only make fun of him (21:36). Earlier in this sūrah God states that He knows how their talk grieves him, and that it is not his honesty being questioned but God's signs that the wrongdoers deny (6:33).

The comfort in the verse is therefore not that mockery is harmless. It is that mockery is expected. It belongs to the work rather than to the worker, and a man who knew that in advance would not treat each jeer as a fresh verdict on himself.

**The Interval Between the Joke and the Answer**

The second half of the verse has a schedule in it. Mocking is answered, but not on the spot. Other messengers were ridiculed before you, and I delayed the disbelievers for a while and then seized them, and how terrible was My punishment (13:32); the end of the wicked is deferred while God's own planning remains flawless (68:45). The delay is what makes mockery feel safe to the mocker.

That interval is also what makes the verse a warning rather than merely a balm.

**Overtaken by the Thing They Mocked**

The phrase is exact: they were overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule (16:34). Not something similar, not something symbolically related — the mocked thing itself becomes the instrument of the reversal. The same formula is used when the consequences of deeds unfold before their eyes, and when the nations that mocked their messengers meet their end (39:48; 45:33). The warning about a Day becomes the Day.

This is the hardest part of the verse to hear and the easiest to state. A joke about the resurrection is a joke about an appointment that has already been fixed, and the appointment does not adjust itself to the mood of the audience. The mocker's confidence borrows time from the very event he is laughing at.

**Why the Mockers Are Not Silenced**

The verse also explains what God declines to do about the mockery, and the restraint is deliberate. The Prophet is told that his duty is only to remind (88:21), that he is not there to compel belief (50:45), and that if God had willed it every person on earth would have believed, so no one is asked to force the outcome (10:99). Enemies were appointed for every prophet, devilish humans and jinn, whispering to one another elaborate deceptions (6:112).

That structure is the reason the pattern in this verse keeps repeating. If mockery were answered immediately, faith would be a calculation about consequences rather than a choice about truth. Silence is the price of a free test, and God carries the mockery for decades to leave the door open. The mockers are not being ignored; they are being allowed to hold a position that only a limited lifetime can defend.

Similar situations came to pass before you, it says, so travel throughout the land and look at how the deniers ended (3:137); earlier societies were superior in might and were annihilated (47:10).

---

## Verse 6:11

> Say, “Travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers.”

**A Command That Sends the Deniers to Look**

The instruction is addressed to the Prophet, but its effect is directional: the people who are denying the message are told to go and inspect the evidence. This is not a rhetorical flourish in the Book. The same itinerary is prescribed with the same confidence in several passages — to see the fate of the wicked (27:69), to see the end of those destroyed before them (35:44), to go out so that hearts may reason and ears may listen (22:46). Religion is usually argued in the abstract; here it is handed a map and a pair of walking shoes.

The order has a practical bite in a trading city. The Quraysh already travelled; the routes north and south were their livelihood. The verse takes the journey they were making anyway and tells them to look up from the caravan.

**A Landscape That Was Already a Lesson**

The addresses are pointed directly at the Meccans elsewhere: the ruins of ʿĀd and Thamūd were said to be clear to them, visible in the settlements they passed (29:38). Those two nations sit in a longer list that includes the people of Noah (22:42), and the Book treats the remains as public property, not hidden knowledge. Wells were left open and palaces stood empty (22:45), all of it within reach of a merchant's eyes.

The argument is therefore not that God once destroyed people somewhere far away. It is that the destruction is still legible in the landscape the audience crosses, and that a man who sleeps beside a ruin has already received the warning in physical form. One verse presses exactly this distinction: the travel is commanded so that hearts may reason and ears may listen, and then the reader is told that it is not the eyes which go blind but the hearts inside the chests (22:46). Seeing the ruins does something a sermon cannot do, because the ruined town delivers its own verdict without a preacher.

**The Word for "Fate"**

The term translated as fate is *ʿāqibah*, the end that follows a course of action — the outcome trailing behind the deed rather than an accident landing on it. That is why the Book keeps asking not what happened to a people but how they ended. The question is about consequences, and consequences are how scripture argues with people who think the present arrangement will last.

The same word governs the reports of ruined towns and superior peoples who were annihilated (35:44; 22:45). Every one of those passages is about an ending, and every one of them is addressed to an audience that had not yet reached its own.

**Why the Book Trusts Stones More Than Arguments**

There is a method in the instruction. Most claims in religion rest on testimony: someone reports, someone else believes. The ruined settlement requires no chain of witnesses at all. It can be visited, dated, and inspected by the very people who are contesting the message, and it will say the same thing to a believer and a sceptic standing next to each other. Few pieces of evidence in any argument are that even-handed.

Later passages continue the practice by pointing to the ruins as facts the Meccans could confirm without the Prophet's help (29:38), which is precisely what makes the warning in this verse hard to dismiss as rhetoric.

---

## Verse 6:12

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “To whom belongs everything in the heavens and the earth?” Say, “To Allah!” He has taken upon Himself to be Merciful. He will certainly gather ˹all of˺ you together for the Day of Judgment—about which there is no doubt. But those who have ruined themselves will never believe

**A Question That Puts the Answer in Their Mouths**

The verse has the Prophet ask and then answer on the audience's behalf, and it is a device the Book uses because the audience's own position supplies the premise. Ask them to whom the earth and those on it belong (23:84), and the exchange proceeds until they concede the point (23:89). Ask who created the heavens and the earth and subjected the sun and the moon, and they will certainly say, "Allah" (29:61). The same concession is recorded in 31:25 and 43:9.

What the question extracts is not new information but an admission. The Quraysh were not being asked to learn that God owns creation; they were being asked to notice that they already said so, in the marketplace, without drawing the conclusion.

**The Obligation God Placed on Himself**

Between the ownership clause and the announcement of the gathering sits a sentence that changes the tone of the whole verse: God has taken upon Himself to be Merciful. The Book expands it — Your Lord has taken upon Himself to be Merciful, forgiving those who sin in ignorance and then repent (6:54) — and elsewhere speaks of mercy that encompasses everything (7:156). No other attribute in the Qur'an is described as prescribed for God by God.

That construction carries a subtle point. Mercy is not simply a mood that arises in God when creatures deserve it; it is a commitment He announced, recorded, and bound Himself to honor. If mercy were merely temperamental, the respite the Makkans were enjoying would be unpredictable. Because it is a self-imposed obligation, delay has a reason and a limit.

**The Day About Which There Is No Doubt**

The Book attaches that formula to the Hour repeatedly: the Hour is coming and there is no doubt about it (40:59; 22:7); God will gather all people on the Day of Judgment, about which there is no doubt, and no word is more truthful than His (4:87). The same pairing of the gathering with an oath of certainty appears when God gives life, causes death, and then gathers everyone for a Day on which no one doubts (45:26).

The phrase is a statement about the reliability of the speaker rather than about a probability estimate. Doubt about the Hour is therefore not intellectual caution; it is a refusal of testimony. The verse treats it as the last item in a series of denials that have already been described in the sūrah — of the signs, of the messengers, and of the meeting with their Lord.

**When the Denial Was Put in Plain Words**

The Book preserves the flat version of the Makkan position, and the reply to it uses an oath. They say the Hour will never come to them. They are told to the contrary by the Lord of the unseen, that it will certainly come, and that not even an atom's weight in the heavens or the earth escapes Him, nor anything smaller or larger, but it is written in a perfect Record (34:3). The denial in that passage is not a request for evidence. It is an announcement that the subject is closed.

Set beside that report, the present verse reads as an exercise in patience.

---

## Verse 6:13

> To Him belongs whatever exists in the day and night. And He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**Ownership Measured in Hours Rather Than Miles**

The sūrah's first verse named the heavens and the earth, the widest claim in space. Here the same ownership is restated in time: whatever exists in the day and whatever exists in the night. The two clauses cover creatures that move at noon and creatures that appear only after dark, which means the claim has no shift in it, no interval in which the property is unsupervised. Elsewhere the same thought is joined to the alternation itself — to Him belongs the alternation of the day and night (23:80).

An owner is described by what he holds at every hour rather than by what he holds on the best day of the year.

**The Practice the Verse Makes Possible**

These two attributes are what make worship in the dark a reasonable act rather than a performance. The Book directs prayer into the hours when no audience is available: the dawn recitation is witnessed by angels (17:78); worship in the night is more impactful and better suited to recitation (73:6); the Prophet is told to rise in the last part of the night as an extra devotion (17:79). The sincere are described as people who abandon their beds, calling on their Lord with hope and fear (32:16).

**A Lord Near Enough to Answer When Called**

Ownership of the night would be cold comfort if it were only ownership. The Book supplies the other half: when My servants ask you about Me, I am truly near, and I respond when they call upon Me (2:186). The same nearness is attached to the hours this verse owns. The Prophet is told to stay patiently with those who call upon their Lord morning and evening, seeking His pleasure, and not to let his eyes wander past them toward the luxuries of the world (18:28).

Morning and evening, day and night — the vocabulary keeps circling the same two halves of the clock. What the verse provides is the reason those hours can hold a conversation with God at all. A claim over time that ended at ownership would leave prayer as a petition posted to an absent office. The two names at the end turn the claim into a promise that someone is on the other side of it.

**Two Words That Carry the Sūrah's Whole Case**

The sūrah assembles its portrait of God clause by clause, and each attribute arrives where an objection needs answering. Creation opens the case (6:1); the terms of life and death follow (6:2). Knowledge of the hidden is stated when concealment is the problem (6:3); mercy is named when the question is why judgment has not yet fallen (6:12); and here hearing and knowing return to close the loop around words and intentions alike.

The Book states the combination in a sentence that matches this verse exactly: whether you speak openly or not, He certainly knows what is secret and what is even more hidden (20:7).

If three people converse privately, God is their fourth; if five, He is their sixth; whether fewer or more, He is with them wherever they may be, and on the Day of Judgment He will inform them of what they did (58:7).

---

## Verse 6:14
> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Will I take any guardian other than Allah, the Originator of the heavens and the earth, Who provides for all and is not in need of provision?” Say, “I have been commanded to be the first to submit and not be one of the polytheists.”

**Fāṭir: The Name That Means "Splitter"**

The translation renders the title Originator, and the Arabic behind it is *fāṭir*, from a root meaning to split or cleave. In the Qur'an the word appears inside one standing phrase, Originator of the heavens and the earth.

It is applied to God in the prayers of Joseph (12:101), in the preaching of the earlier messengers (14:10), in the opening of the sūrah of the angels (35:1), in a supplication (39:46), and in a declaration of God's uniqueness (42:11).

The verbal form of the same root is used for the forming of human beings, and its noun gives the word *fiṭrah*, the natural disposition God instilled in people and which the verse on upright faith says must not be altered (30:30).

The name therefore carries a picture rather than a title: the heavens and the earth were opened out from something closed, in the way a seed splits and a bud separates. Whoever did that does not need a partner for maintenance.

**The God Who Feeds and Is Not Fed**

The second clause is economic, and it is aimed straight at the cult of Makkah. God provides for all and needs no provision from anyone. The Book states it as a first-person claim: I seek no provision from them, nor do I need them to feed Me (51:57). That single sentence removes the entire commercial logic of sacrifice, in which the deity is sustained by what is brought to the shrine and the worshipper expects the arrangement to be honoured in return.

The Messiah and his mother both ate food, the Book observes, as a sign that neither could have been a god (5:75).

**"The First to Submit"**

Having refused the compromise, the Prophet is told to declare his own position: he has been commanded to be the first of those who submit. Interpreters read the word in three ways — first among the people of his own time to submit, first in rank among the submitters, or the one uniquely singled out to receive revelation. Two other passages carry the same claim in his mouth: he is commanded to be the first of those who submit (39:12), and he is the first to submit (6:163).

Each reading keeps the sense of priority in a sequence rather than superiority in a hierarchy. Abraham, raised among idolaters, is the model of the same submission (16:120), and the faith being claimed here is presented as the faith of Abraham, the upright, who was not a polytheist (6:161).

**The Command Not to Be Among the Polytheists**

The verse closes with a warning addressed directly to the Prophet: and not be one of the polytheists. That the Book warns its own messenger is not a slight; it is the clearest possible statement that the prohibition is universal and that no station is exempt from it. The same phrasing recurs across the sūrah, attached to Abraham (6:79), to the Prophet's declaration of faith (6:161), and to his final profession (6:163).

---
## Verse 6:15

> Say, “I truly fear—if I were to disobey my Lord—the torment of a tremendous Day.”

**The Same Line Repeated in Another Sūrah**

A nearly identical sentence appears in the sūrah of the Crowds: I truly fear, if I were to disobey my Lord, the torment of a tremendous Day (39:13). The repetition matters for how the verse should be read. This is not a momentary remark the Prophet improvised under pressure; it is part of the confession he was commanded to deliver, and the Book records it twice in the same form.

Repetition in the Qur'an usually signals that a statement carries structural weight. What is being established is the shape of prophethood: a man who carries a warning he believes, addressed first to himself, and then to everyone else. The Book does not permit its messenger to be the one figure exempt from what he announces. Elsewhere the same Prophet is told exactly what he does not have: he does not say he holds God's treasuries, or knows the unseen, or is an angel; he only follows what is revealed to him (6:50). The verse in front of us continues that self-description. He is not a shareholder in the divine power and not a priest with privileged immunity; he is a servant with an instruction and a fear.

**The Day and Its Adjective**

An earlier passage uses the same register when it warns that if the addressed turn away, they are feared for the torment of a formidable Day (11:3). The vocabulary is not there to frighten for its own sake. It is there because the audience was being asked to trade a comfortable present for a claim about the future, and the Book refuses to make the trade look small.

**Fear That Lives Beside Hope**

The verse does not describe the Prophet as despairing, and the Book never puts fear in opposition to hope. The sincere are described as those who abandon their beds, calling on their Lord with hope and fear (32:16). The two travel together because each corrects the other: hope alone drifts into presumption, and fear alone curdles into a portrait of God that no one could love.

What the Prophet's confession supplies is a standard. In Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī 20, when the Companions told him that God had already forgiven his past and future sins and that they were not like him, he became angry and said that he was the most God-fearing and the most knowledgeable of God among them. The point is not that he feared more than others as an achievement. It is that knowledge of God and fear of God increase together, and the man who knows most has the least reason to feel safe.

---

## Verse 6:16

> Whoever is spared the torment of that Day will have certainly been shown Allah’s mercy. And that is the absolute triumph

**Safety Described as Mercy, Not as Wages**

Notice the grammar of the escape. The one who is spared has been shown mercy; he has not been described as having earned his exemption. That is a significant choice of words in a Book that insists deed counts for something, and it reflects the Book's consistent position that the accounting is exact while the outcome is a gift. Even a person whose good deeds outweigh his bad ones arrives at the Day carrying a debt he cannot settle on his own.

The same framing appears when the righteous are described as praying for protection from the consequences of their evil deeds, since whoever is shielded from the evil of his actions on that Day has been shown mercy (40:9). The prayer of the best of people is therefore a request for mercy rather than a request for a fair wage, and this verse states the outcome of that request.

That wording also explains why the Book never lets its readers treat the Day as a negotiation.

**"That Is the Absolute Triumph"**

In the four words at the end of the verse, the Book redefines winning. Escape from the Fire is not a rescue from a misfortune; it is an attainment, and the phrase is used elsewhere for a reward measured in Gardens and forgiveness of sins (61:12), and for the ultimate triumph of the saved (3:185). The scale of the language shows how the Book weighs the stakes it is describing.

For an audience that measured success in livestock, lineage, caravan profits, and victories in tribal skirmishes, the claim is a direct challenge. The Quraysh would have understood triumph as a matter of reputation and acquisitions in this world. The verse assigns the word to an outcome that happens after death, when none of those assets can be carried, and it does so without conceding any comparison between the two.

No instruction follows the promise, and no list of deeds is attached. The verse is a statement about how the Day will be settled, and its absence of a to-do list is intentional in this place in the sūrah, where the audience has been arguing rather than asking for guidance. What it offers instead is a description of the only outcome worth arranging one's life around.

The arrangement itself has been described in the surrounding verses: submit, and do not join others with God (6:14); fear the Day if you disobey (6:15); and know that escape from it will come as mercy rather than as a settlement (6:16). The verse's quiet generosity depends on the reader having taken the earlier sentences seriously — mercy is offered, but only to those who needed to be spared.

---

## Verse 6:17

> If Allah touches you with harm, none can undo it except Him. And if He touches you with a blessing, He is Most Capable of everything

**The Same Sentence Elsewhere in the Book**

The wording of this verse recurs almost identically in the sūrah of Jonah: if Allah touches you with harm, none can undo it except Him; and if He intends good for you, none can withhold His bounty, granting it to whom He wills of His servants (10:107). The Book repeats it because the audience kept needing it, and the repetition is itself the argument — a claim this central is stated in more than one sūrah in nearly the same words.

A second passage makes the same point from the opposite direction: whatever mercy God opens up for people, none can withhold it, and whatever He withholds, none but Him can release it (35:2). Two sūrahs, the same doctrine, and both place every gain and every loss inside a single decision that no intercessor can move.

**The Objects That Could Not Respond**

The idols of Makkah were credited with the power to hurt and to help, and the Book dismisses the claim from several angles. How can you worship, besides Allah, those who can neither harm nor benefit you (5:76)? Of the calf that the Israelites fashioned, the question is put with heavy irony: did they not see that it could not respond to them, nor protect or benefit them (20:89)? And the lords taken besides God are described as unable to benefit or protect even themselves (13:16).

Set beside that catalogue, this verse is compact and decisive. The complaint against the idols is not that they are weak in comparison with a stronger god. It is that the category of harm and benefit — the two levers by which a person's life is actually steered — is closed to them completely, and belongs to one power alone.

**The Messenger Does Not Own the Power He Announces**

An obvious suspicion follows from such a claim, and the Book addresses it in the Prophet's own voice. He is told to say that he has no power to benefit or protect himself except by God's will, that he is only a warner and a bringer of good news to believers (7:188). He is told to say that he does not claim the treasuries of God, or knowledge of the unseen, or angelic status, and that he only follows what is revealed to him (6:50).

The two verses belong together. The messenger announces that God alone can harm and help, and then explicitly removes himself from that power. Arabia was used to men who claimed a share of the sacred; the Prophet's testimony is designed to be unusable as a claim of that kind. He is the announcer, not the dispenser.

**Even Sorcery Sits Under the Same Claim**

Makkan anxiety about magic was real, and the Book does not simply ridicule it. It places sorcery inside the teaching about this verse: the practitioners of magic in the story of Babylon could not harm anyone except by Allah's will, and the harm they caused was ultimately to themselves (2:102). The verse's claim about harm therefore extends over the very practice the audience feared most.

That inclusion is a quiet mercy. People who live in fear of curses and charms are usually offered a stronger charm in exchange.

---

## Verse 6:18

> He reigns supreme over His creation. And He is the All-Wise, All-Aware

**The Summit of the Argument So Far**

The sūrah has been climbing through its first verses, and this short sentence states the result. Ownership of the heavens and the earth (6:12), control of harm and benefit (6:17), and now unqualified dominance: He reigns supreme over His creation. The Arabic root behind the phrase carries the sense of overpowering and subduing, the way a thing is held down by something stronger than itself. Nothing in the created order stands outside that grip.

The word for creation here is the word for servants, which links the claim to loyalty rather than to raw force. A maker has authority over what he made; the verse states that authority in the language of ownership over those who serve, and the two senses run together deliberately. The sūrah is arguing with a city that had assigned shares of divine authority to other beings, and the sentence leaves no gap in which such a share could be lodged.

**The Same Claim Beside the Recording Angels**

Five verses later the phrase returns with an addition that shows what kind of dominance is meant. He reigns supreme over all of His creation, and sends recording-angels watching over you, and when death comes the angels take the soul without neglecting the duty (6:61). The next sentence reports that people are returned to Allah, their True Master, to whom judgment belongs alone, and that He is the swiftest reckoner (6:62).

Dominance in the Book is therefore never blind force. It arrives with a ledger, and the powerful hand that holds the creature down is the same hand that keeps a careful account of the creature's days. The rulers of Makkah understood power as something exerted in public; the verse attaches it to an administration that never sleeps and never loses a file. Wisdom in the Qur'anic vocabulary means more than cleverness; it means everything set where it belongs, so that no arrangement in the creation is an accident. The suffering of the believers and the long prosperity of their tormentors are therefore not evidence against God's government, because the purpose behind both is known to him who set them in place.

The Quraysh had asked why no angel came (6:8), why the punishment had not fallen, why the message had not been sent as a single book.

**What This Rule Requires of the Creature**

A being who is dominated, watched, and dealt with wisely has only one reasonable posture, and the Book names it elsewhere: if Allah helps you, none can defeat you, and if He denies you help, who else could help you, so in Allah let the believers put their trust (3:160). The verse has argued away every other refuge — the stones and the stars cannot harm or help, the ancestors cannot negotiate, and the messenger claims no share of the power.

---

## Verse 6:19

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Who is the best witness?” Say, “Allah is! He is a Witness between me and you. And this Quran has been revealed to me so that, with it, I may warn you and whoever it reaches. Do you ˹pagans˺ testify that there are other gods besides Allah?” ˹Then˺ say, “I will never testify ˹to this˺!” ˹And˺ say, “There is only One God. And I totally reject whatever ˹idols˺ you associate with Him.”

**The Best Witness**

The verse tells the Prophet to ask his people a question, and then to answer it himself: who is the best witness? God, he is told to say, is witness between me and you. The Book uses the title of witness where a dispute needs to be closed, and it gives the title to God in the passage about the men who deny the messenger, where the Prophet is told that God is enough as a witness between him and them (17:96 states it). The same title belongs to the day when the witnesses stand, and the sūrah of the Pilgrimage names God as witness over the differences between the communities (22:17 states it). The audience in Makkah had been asking for a witness of their own choosing, and the verse answers the demand by naming the one witness nobody can cross-examine.

**Why This Answer Works in That City**

The men the verse is addressing lived in a place where a case was won by producing the right witnesses, and testimony could be bought. The verse names a witness who was present at the giving of the message, present at the intention of the man who received it, and present at the private reasoning of the men who denied it. The sūrah had already told the Prophet that no one could change the words of God, and it had told him that the men who rejected the message would not believe even if a miracle came, because they were not arguing about evidence. The verse in front of the reader closes the argument with the answer only God could give.

**The Warning That Reaches Everyone**

The verse then says why the Book was sent: so that the Prophet may warn with it the men of his own city and every person the message reaches. The wording places every later reader beside the first audience, which the sūrah reinforces when it says that the Book is a reminder for all the worlds. The teachers of the community cited this phrase as the reason the message is universal rather than local, and the Book states the same universality in the sūrah of the Prophets, where the Prophet is described as sent as a mercy to all the worlds.

**The Rejection at the End**

The verse closes with a question and an answer: do you testify that there are other gods besides God? And the Prophet is told to say that he will not testify to that, that there is one God, and that he rejects whatever they join with Him. The declaration matches the words of the prophets before him: Abraham told his people that he rejected what they joined with God, and Hūd called God and the people to witness that he rejected them too (6:78 reports Abraham's words and 11:54 reports Hūd's). The verse in front of the reader leaves the reader with the same choice, and the sūrah goes on to say that the men who were given the Book know that the message is the truth from their Lord. The prophet Hūd made the same declaration to his people when he called God and the people to witness that he rejected what they joined with Him (11:54 reports his words).

---

## Verse 6:20

> Those to whom We gave the Scripture recognize him ˹to be a true prophet˺ as they recognize their own children. Those who have ruined themselves will never believe

**Those Who Ruined Themselves Will Never Believe**

The sentence closes with the refrain the sūrah has already used once: those who have ruined themselves will not believe (6:12). Placed after the recognition of the People of the Book, the clause reads as an explanation of how recognition can fail to become faith. Something in the person is spent, and the loss is his own — the ruin comes first, the unbelief follows, and both are described as the same event seen from two angles.

**Recognition, Not Information**

The verb in this sentence is not the one used for intellectual knowledge in general; it is the one for recognizing something already known. The People of the Book are not described as encountering a new claim and being persuaded by it, but as seeing someone they had been expecting and identifying him. Their scriptures had described a messenger to come, and the sūrah reports that the identification is not difficult for them.

A covenant is on record elsewhere in which God took a pledge from the prophets that if a messenger came confirming what they had, they would believe in him and support him (3:81). Whether the audience of this verse were rabbis, monks, or traders who had picked up the language of the earlier scriptures, the Book's claim is that the tools for recognition were already in their hands.

**The Same Claim With an Uncomfortable Addition**

In the sūrah of the Cow the statement is repeated with a second sentence that this verse omits: a group of them hides the truth knowingly (2:146). Recognition and concealment appear side by side in the same report, and the pairing defines the accusation. It is not that the learned among the People of the Book failed to notice the signs; it is that some of them noticed and chose silence.

---

## Verse 6:21

> Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His signs? Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed

**A Question That Ranks Wrongdoing**

The sentence is put as a question with no answer available, and the form appears across the Book whenever a particular offence is being placed at the top of the list. Who does more wrong than the one who fabricates lies against Allah (11:18; 18:15; 61:7)? Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah or deny His revelations (7:37; 10:17)? The same interrogative is used of those who bar God's name from being mentioned in His places of worship (2:114) and of those who hide testimony they received from God (2:140).

Ranking wrongs is not the Book's usual habit, and the device is a reminder that the two crimes named here are not moral slips. They are attacks on the source of the whole moral order, and they are committed by people who know the stakes, since the audience hearing this verse had already heard the message of the First Sūrah and the one who was named there as the "Lord of all worlds" (1:2).

**Fabricating a Lie Means Claiming Divine Guidance**

The Arabic phrase is specific. To fabricate against God is to invent a religious practice or belief and present it as God's command, which is why the Book attaches the charge to the ritual customs of the Makkans. When they commit a shameful deed they say, "We found our forefathers doing it and Allah has commanded us to do it," and the reply cuts them off: God never commands what is shameful (7:28). The same charge is laid against the invented cattle categories, the baḥīrah, sā'ibah, waṣīlah, and ḥām, which the sūrah itself treats at length: God never ordained them, but the disbelievers fabricate lies about Him (5:103).

An invention is not a harmless opinion, because a person who adds a rule to the religion has appointed himself a legislator beside God. He is not only mistaken about the law; he is claiming the authority to make one.

**The Other Crime: Denial of the Signs**

The second half of the verse names the opposite operation. Denial does not add to God; it removes what He has already shown, refusing the evidence that the sky, the harvest, and the record of ruined nations were said to supply. Both are named together in 7:37 and 10:17, and the pairing is deliberate, because the two offences describe the same act of rearranging authority: one party edits the message, the other deletes it.

Denial also has a working form in the audience's own city. Signs were not met with silence; they were reassigned. The recitation was called magic, the messenger a poet or a soothsayer, and the warnings of the earlier scriptures were treated as material that had been borrowed and polished.

Believers are described as successful because they believe in the unseen, keep the prayer, and spend from what they are given (2:5); the people of the Gardens are described with the same word.

Commentators explain that the wrongdoers will not find what they seek in this world or the next, which is why the same verb is used where the wrongdoers are seen to have failed after their apparent prosperity was stripped away (28:82).

---

## Verse 6:22

> ˹Consider˺ the Day We will gather them all together then ask those who associated others ˹with Allah in worship˺, “Where are those gods you used to claim?”

**An Interrogation Rather Than an Announcement**

The scene opens with a question addressed to the polytheists, and the question is not about what they did. It asks where the gods are. In a trial, that kind of question is not a try to get information, since the one asking knows the answer. It is the device a judge uses to make the accused confront the object of his own claim, and it works by requiring him to look.

The Book returns to this question with a frequency that suggests it was the most effective thing that could be said to a people who had built their lives around shrines. On the Day of Judgment He will humiliate them and say, "Where are My associate-gods for whose sake you used to oppose the believers?" (16:27). The same challenge is reported in the sūrah of Stories twice over (28:62; 28:74), and again in the sūrah of the Believer (40:73), and once in Fussilat (41:47).

**The Gathering That Comes Before the Question**

All of humanity is assembled first: We will gather them all together. The Book insists on the totality of that assembly because the Makkan argument had rested on a small society of important people. In this world a shrine belongs to the strong, who can prevent questions from being asked and shut down the ones who ask them. At the gathering, the audience is every human being who has ever lived, and the tribe can no longer determine who speaks or when.

That is why the verse says "all together" rather than naming the guilty group.

**Where the Absent Are**

The answer is silence. In the parallel passages the accused cry out that their gods have failed them and confess that they had invoked nothing real (40:74); elsewhere the associates are said to fail their worshippers and the worshippers realise there is no escape (41:48). The question therefore works as a demonstration rather than a rebuke. Everyone present sees, in the same instant, that the whole apparatus was empty.

Nothing is reported about their gods arriving to defend them, and nothing is reported about their intercession, which is exactly the point the verse wants to press. The Book allows the idols every kind of dignity except presence at the moment when presence would matter.

**The Question Left Standing for the Hearer**

The verse places the reader in the position of the judge's assistant: he watches the interrogation and hears the question before the accused does. That arrangement is deliberate, and it is the reason the sūrah says earlier that God knows what is concealed and what is revealed (6:3).

It is also worth noticing what the verse does not ask. There is no request for an explanation, no demand for repentance, and no negotiation about terms. The question concerns the location of a set of objects that the accused had treated as divine. The only honest answer available to them is the one the Book records in the parallel passages, where the gods are said to have failed them and the worshippers admit they had invoked nothing real (40:74).

---

## Verse 6:23

> Their only argument will be: “By Allah, our Lord! We were never polytheists.”

**Swearing by the God They Denied**

There is a bitter irony in the formula they choose. Their oath is taken in the name of Allah, and they attach the word "our Lord" to it, which is an acknowledgement of the lordship they had refused to live by. The Book notes elsewhere that the Makkans already knew who created the heavens and the earth and would say so if asked (31:25; 43:9). In the moment of judgment they say it again, with the name they had avoided, to deny the practice they had maintained.

**The Defence That Convicts Its Speaker**

Facing a question they cannot answer, the accused produce one sentence, and the sentence is a lie sworn in the name of the God they spent their lives ignoring. They do not plead that the idols were powerful, or that they had misunderstood the message, or that their ancestors misled them. They deny the charge completely, and they do it under oath, in the presence of the One they are swearing by.

That confession is wrung out of them and used against them. They have just testified to the authority of the God in whose presence they are standing, which makes the denial that follows harder to file as an error of judgement.

Swearing in God's name was not, for this audience, an unusual thing to do in a tight corner. The Book records that they swore their most solemn oaths that a warner would make them better guided than any other community. That when the warner came it only drove them further away (35:42); they swore that God would never raise the dead (16:38); they swore that a sign would bring them to belief (6:109). The oath of 6:23 is the last of that series. The sūrah of the Argument reports the same behaviour on the Day of Resurrection, when people will falsely swear to God as they used to swear to their leaders, thinking they have something to stand on, and are named in the report as the total liars (58:18).

**The Disclosure That Comes First**

The Book describes a moment before the lying begins: what they used to hide becomes clear to them (6:28). The verse at hand sits inside that disclosure. They are not being asked to remember an event from long ago; they are standing in front of a record that has been produced, and the denial is spoken against what they themselves now see.

That is the terrible shape of the scene. A person who has been shown the truth of his own case and still denies it has crossed out of the category of the mistaken and into the category of the liar, and the Book names the result plainly: see how they lie about themselves (6:24).

**The Gods Who Refuse to Back Them Up**

Elsewhere the Book lets the false deities speak. When the polytheists call on their associate-gods, the gods throw the accusation back at them and say that they are definitely liars (16:86), and on that Day they offer full submission to Allah while everything they had fabricated falls away (16:87). Even their allies from the unseen do not defend them. Satan himself announces at the end that he had no authority over them, that he only called and they answered, and that they should blame themselves (14:22).

---

## Verse 6:24

> See how they will lie about themselves and how those ˹gods˺ they fabricated will fail them

**An Order to Look at a Specific Moment**

The verse begins with a command to observe: see how they will lie about themselves. It is the same instruction the sūrah gives later when it describes those arraigned before the Fire (6:27) and when it reports what the disbelievers will say when they stand before their Lord (6:30). The reader is not asked to form an opinion about the Hereafter in general. He is placed at a precise point in a scene and told which detail to notice.

**The Fabrications That Walk Away**

The second half of the verse states the consequence: the gods they fabricated fail them. The Book uses the same expression repeatedly at the end of scenes like this one. Coming back to God all alone as He created the first time, a person finds that his intercessors have vanished, his ties broken, and his claims brought to nothing (6:94); every soul faces what it has done, and whatever gods they fabricated fail them (10:30).

The failure is not described as an act of punishment. It is described as absence. The systems that were built to carry a person through the judgment simply do not arrive, and the worshippers who had spent their lives maintaining them are left carrying nothing. The sūrah of the Heights uses the identical closing formula in the same setting: those who ignored the warning ask for intercessors or for a second term, and the report ends by noting that they have ruined themselves and that whatever gods they fabricated have failed them (7:53).

What disappears with the gods is the entire chain of dependence that had been arranged around them. No patron steps forward, no offering is remembered as a credit, and no lineage carries weight. The verse that reports the collapse at the beginning of the Hereafter, when people come back alone as they were created the first time and every tie is broken (6:94), describes the same vacuum from a different angle.

**Idolatry's Ordinary Disappointments in This Life**

The Makkans did not need to wait for the Hereafter to test the reliability of their gods. The Book points out that the objects of their worship neither respond nor protect nor benefit (20:89), and it asks how anyone can worship, besides God, what can neither harm nor benefit him (5:76). The complaint is not that the idols are weaker than another deity. It is that they are not participants in the transaction at all.

**What the Reader Is Meant to Take Away**

The instruction to look is not aimed at the men in the scene. It is aimed at the person holding the text, who is still alive, still able to choose, and still in a position to examine what he relies on. The sūrah will go on to describe people who listen without hearing (6:25) and leaders who block the way (6:26), and this verse stands before them as the preview of where that road ends.

Two things are being shown: an accusation that convicts by its own utterance, and a support that stops supporting.

The men in the scene have understood their position completely, and their understanding produces nothing but a denial.

---

## Verse 6:25

> There are some of them who ˹pretend to˺ listen to your recitation ˹of the Quran˺, but We have cast veils over their hearts—leaving them unable to comprehend it—and deafness in their ears. Even if they were to see every sign, they still would not believe in them. The disbelievers would ˹even˺ come to argue with you, saying, “This ˹Quran˺ is nothing but ancient fables!”

**Listeners Who Do Not Hear**

The verse opens with attendance. Some of them do listen to the recitation, and their bodies are present in the gathering, which makes their condition the more revealing. What reaches them is not a rumour but the text itself, recited in their own language. Nothing arrives; the sentence reports the veil over their hearts and the deafness in their ears, and it does so without suggesting that either came from a shortage of recitation on the Prophet's side.

The Book uses the same language for the same condition elsewhere. Their hearts are sealed along with their hearing, and their sight is covered (2:7). Veils are cast over the hearts and deafness placed in the ears, and when the Lord alone is mentioned they turn their backs in aversion (17:46); they are described as those who turned a blind eye to the reminder and could not stand listening to it (18:101).

**The Question of Who Closed the Shutters**

Since God is the one said to have placed the covering and the deafness, the verse raises the question every reader of the Book eventually asks.
