Others object that this would make the call to believe meaningless for those so described, and would give the disbelievers an argument against God, since no soul is tasked beyond its capacity (2:286).

The Book states the sequence where it says that God leaves to stray those who are already in a state of disbelief and wrongdoing, and where it describes a person who takes his own desire as a god and is then left to stray knowingly (45:23). The hardening is the consequence of a decision, not the cause of it, and the same chapter that reports the veil reports the deliberate turning away in the verse immediately before (18:57).

**Signs Would Not Change the Outcome**

Having described the impediment, the verse removes the last comfort available to the sceptic: even if they were to see every sign, they still would not believe in them. The Book repeats this in several places with a firmness that leaves no room for negotiation (7:146; 10:96). The clearest case is recorded in the sūrah of the Moon, where the splitting of the moon, granted in answer to a demand for a sign, produced the reply that this was the same old magic (54:1–2).

**The Debate That Follows the Recitation**

The verse's last clause records what happens after the listening. The disbelievers come to argue, and the argument they bring is that the Qur'an is nothing but ancient fables. The same charge appears in a series of reports — the recitation is familiar, they could produce something like it. The material is old (8:31); it is what the Lord has revealed, they are told, and they answer that it is ancient fables (16:24; 68:15); and others allege that it was written down and rehearsed morning and evening (25:5).

The charge is not a counter-argument, since it says nothing about whether the material is true. It is a social verdict dressed as a literary one, and the Book quotes it because it shows what the arguing was for. People who have decided not to accept a message do not need to refute it; they need a phrase that makes it uninteresting to their neighbours.

---

## Verse 6:26

> They turn others away from the Prophet and distance themselves as well. They ruin none but themselves, yet they fail to perceive it

**Two Refusals in One Sentence**

The verse describes a double movement: turning others away from the Prophet, and standing back from him. The leaders of Makkah did not simply decline the message; they made sure the people around them did not get near it, and then kept their own distance so that no association with the Prophet could be held against them.

The Book records the operational version of this policy in another sūrah: the disbelievers advised one another not to listen to the Qur'an, but to drown it out with noise so that they might prevail (41:26).

**Why Blocking the Road Was a Business Decision**

Makkan hostility to the message was not detached religious dislike. The standing of the city rested on the sanctuary, the pilgrimage seasons, and the network of shrines and offerings that made Quraysh a broker between tribes. A message that made the idols functionally worthless threatened revenue, prestige, and the regional arrangement in which the clan's authority was grounded. The Book describes the pattern in general terms when it speaks of those who hinder others from the way of Allah, striving to make it appear crooked (14:3), which is deliberate distortion rather than passive unbelief.

**The Reading That Points to Abū Ṭālib**

Some early exegetes read the first clause differently, taking the thing forbidden to be harm to the Prophet rather than the message itself, and applying the verse to Abū Ṭālib, the Prophet's uncle and early protector, who shielded him from the notables while not accepting the message. The interpretation is transmitted from several commentators.

Its weakness is the sentence that follows. The verse censures those it names as people who ruin none but themselves, and shielding the Prophet was a service, not a ruin. The commentators who report the Abū Ṭālib reading also note that it sits awkwardly in a passage aimed throughout at the leaders who persecuted the Prophet, and the Shia tradition rejects the attribution altogether, holding that Abū Ṭālib died a Muslim. The obstructionist reading is the stronger one, because it gives both halves of the verse a single hostile meaning and keeps the censure where the passage has placed it.

**"They Ruin None but Themselves"**

The verse then turns the policy back on those who designed it. Whatever they believed they were protecting by keeping the message away from their people, the Book reports the loss as their own. The followers they turned away are not excused, and the leaders are told elsewhere that they will carry their burdens in full on the Day of Judgment along with some of the burdens of those they misled without knowledge (16:25).

A principle stated repeatedly in the Book holds the two truths together: no soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another (6:164; 17:15; 35:18). The led answer for their own choices and the leaders answer for theirs, which include the choices they cultivated by blocking the road. What no leader can do is hand his loss to the people he obstructed.

The Book describes the same trap elsewhere in the story of those who planned their strategies and found that God's own planning was ahead of it (86:16).

---

## Verse 6:27

> If only you could see when they will be detained before the Fire! They will cry, “Oh! If only we could be sent back, we would never deny the signs of our Lord and we would ˹surely˺ be of the believers.”

**A Scene Shown to the Prophet**

The Book several times asks the Prophet to picture a moment he cannot witness, so that its weight enters his argument. If you could see when the wrongdoers are in the throes of death while the angels stretch out their hands (6:93). If only you could see when they will be detained before their Lord (6:30); if only you could see the wicked hanging their heads in shame before their Lord, crying that they have now seen and heard and asking to be sent back (32:12). The device takes a claim about the future and gives it the force of a scene already in progress.

Its placement here matters. The verse before it described leaders who turned people away from the Prophet and kept their distance while ruining themselves, and this scene shows where that road ends. Nobody in the picture is debating theology, and nobody is guarding a shrine.

**The Request Every Generation Makes**

Begging for a return is a fixed feature of these scenes, and the Book repeats it because the request never becomes less common. The wrongdoers plead, as they face the torment, to be shown any way back (42:44). People scream to their Lord to take them out so they may do good rather than what they used to do (35:37). The wicked ask to be sent back, promising good conduct and claiming sure faith (32:12); and the misled followers, seeing the fate of the leaders they obeyed, cry out that with a second chance they would disown them as they were disowned (2:167).

**Why the Wish Cannot Be Honoured**

Nothing in the scene is arbitrary. The life being asked for was already given, and the answer to a direct request for return is recorded: were you not given lives long enough for whoever wished to be mindful to be mindful, and did the warner not come to you (35:37)? A second term would add no information to the case, because the information had been delivered. What it would add is a different motive, and a virtue produced by the sight of the Fire is not the virtue the test was designed to produce.

**The Voice of Panic, Not of Faith**

Commentators observe that the promise is made out of fear of the Fire rather than from any real desire for belief, which is why it carries no benefit. That judgement sounds harsh until the words are examined.

The Book has already recorded what happens to a promise of belief made at the sight of punishment, in the case of Pharaoh as the sea closed over him. He declared his faith, and was told that it came too late, after a lifetime of disobedience and corruption (10:90–91). The verse in front of us is that moment, multiplied into a crowd, and spoken before the reader has to reach it.

There is one further difference worth noticing between the two scenes. Pharaoh spoke when the water arrived and there was still a heartbeat in which to speak, while the men in this verse are detained in a place where the ability to act has been withdrawn completely.

---

## Verse 6:28

> But no! ˹They only say this˺ because the truth they used to hide will become all too clear to them. Even if they were to be sent back, they would certainly revert to what they were forbidden. Indeed they are liars

**What Was Hidden Becomes Plain**

The reason given is knowledge rather than honesty: the truth they used to hide will become all too clear to them. What was concealed in life, whether the realities of the resurrection and the judgment that the leaders dismissed in public, or the deeds that were kept out of sight, is displayed on the day the secrets are tested (86:9). Their promise is therefore not a change of heart. It is what a person says when he can no longer say anything else.

Commentators offer several identifications of what was hidden: the disbelief that hypocrites concealed, the truth about the Prophet that the People of the Book suppressed, or the evil deeds that the wrongdoers kept from view. The Makkan context favours the last two readings in combination, since the sūrah's opponents had both concealed their own conduct and worked to keep the message from circulating.

**The Counterfactual the Book States With Confidence**

The verse then makes a claim about a world that did not happen. Even if they were sent back, they would certainly revert to what they were forbidden. This is not a prediction about individuals whose future is unknown, since the Book elsewhere records that a return to the world would have produced the same conduct (32:12). It reports the pattern of a generation that swore the most solemn oaths to believe if a warner came, and then was driven further away when he did (35:42).

**What the Book Does With Its Hypotheticals**

That certainty is itself a claim about human nature. Appetite, habit, and social position do not change when a person is given a second trip through the same conditions, and the Book states elsewhere that the hearts are alike across generations, so that the demands of one age are the demands of another (2:118). Knowing this, God still grants the span of a life, and the granting is mercy rather than oversight. The men in the scene mistake the delay for an invitation to negotiate, and the verse states in advance why the negotiation would have gone nowhere.

---

## Verse 6:29

> They insisted, “There is nothing beyond this worldly life and we will never be resurrected.”

**The Claim That Explains All the Others**

The verse names the position underneath every other objection in the sūrah. Requests for signs, arguments about the ancestors, mockery of the warnings, and the obstruction described a few verses earlier all rest on this one conclusion: there is nothing beyond this life, and no return to a judgment. The Book reports the same position in the mouths of other audiences, and it repeats it because it is the foundation on which a whole approach to life can be built.

Elsewhere the denial appears with the same edge: they say that once they are dead and reduced to dust and bones, they will not be resurrected (23:82). They ask whether, when they die and become dust and bones, they will really be judged (37:53); and the disbelievers claim the Hour will never come to them (34:3), a claim answered with an oath by the Lord of the unseen.

**A Sentence About the Boundaries of Reality**

The Book treats the sentence as a boast rather than a finding. The verse says they insisted, which suggests repetition and reinforcement, the way a position is held by being restated among people who agree with one another.

**The Book's Name for This Denial**

Note the vocabulary the Book prefers. Rather than arguing about the mechanics of resurrection, it describes the position as denying the meeting with God (6:31); elsewhere as those who do not expect to meet their Lord (25:21). The choice of words places the issue where scripture wants it: not an argument about biology and dust, but a refusal that the account will ever be settled face to face.

That reframing explains why the Book answers this denial with evidence the audience already accepted. It points to the earth being revived after its death and says that people will be brought out in the same way (30:19); it points to the God who causes seeds to sprout and brings the living out of the dead (6:95). The audience had no quarrel with any of those observations. The quarrel was about where they led.

**The Claim Tested Against What the Audience Already Knew**

The Book does not treat the denial as an unanswerable position, and it does not ask the Makkans to accept a new principle to reject it. It asks them about the things they already granted. They accepted that God created the heavens and the earth; they accepted the rain, the harvest, and the span of a human life as coming from a power beyond the shrines. Against that backdrop, the sentence in this verse is not a cautious conclusion drawn from the evidence, and the Book says as much where it reports those who deny the meeting with God asking who will give life to decayed bones while forgetting that they were created in the first place (36:78).

**First Creation as the Standing Proof**

The sūrah has already made the argument in its opening verses. The One who created human beings from clay appointed a term, and another term rests with Him, and the audience is asked why they still doubt (6:2). The reasoning runs from the creation the Makkans could see in their own bodies to the appointment they could not see, and it uses the visible fact as the guarantee of the invisible one.

---

## Verse 6:30

> But if only you could see when they will be detained before their Lord! He will ask ˹them˺, “Is this ˹Hereafter˺ not the truth?” They will cry, “Absolutely, by our Lord!” He will say, “Then taste the punishment for your disbelief.”

**A Question the Answer Cannot Help**

Is this Hereafter not the truth? The question is not an examination, since the reality of what they see is not in dispute. It is the last opportunity to say out loud what their whole life was spent rejecting, and they take it. They say, by our Lord, that it is true.

The same exchange is reported in the sūrah of the Sand-Dunes with almost identical words: the disbelievers are exposed to the Fire and asked whether this is not the truth, they swear by their Lord that it is, and they are told to taste the punishment for their disbelief (46:34).

**The Confession That Comes Too Late**

The Book draws a firm line between believing because the evidence has been weighed and confessing because the evidence has become unavoidable. When Pharaoh's faith arrived as he was drowning, he was told that it came after a lifetime of disobedience and corruption (10:90–91). The same rule is stated for the disbelievers who cry out at the sight of the punishment that they now believe in God alone: their faith was of no benefit when they saw the torment, and that has always been the way God deals with His wicked servants (40:84–85).

Nothing about this is arbitrary. Faith at the sight of the punishment is not a decision about God; it is an adjustment to circumstances. The test in this world was designed for people who had to choose without seeing, and a confession wrung out of a person by force is not the thing the test was measuring.

**Tasting as a Form of Instruction**

The command to taste the punishment is used throughout the Book, and the verb is chosen for what it conveys about knowledge. Tasting is how a person learns what a thing is by experience, and the Book applies it to the consequences of deeds in more than two dozen places. Earlier in the same sūrah, the wrongdoers at the point of death are told they will be repaid with the torment of disgrace for telling lies about God and for being arrogant toward His signs (6:93).

The irony is deliberate. These were people who demanded signs, disputed evidence, and treated the warnings as fables, and their final lesson is delivered by experience rather than by argument. What they refused to know by report they are made to know by contact. The sūrah of the Women states the same law from the other side when it declares that repentance is not accepted from those who persist until they begin to die and then cry that they repent now (4:18).

**What the Question Asks the Reader to Admit**

A person reading the passage in a market or a courtyard in Makkah had not yet reached the point where the truth could not be denied, which is exactly why the passage was revealed then. The question in the verse, addressed to a crowd that can no longer benefit from answering it, is aimed over their heads at everyone who still can. The Book elsewhere states the same choice plainly when it reports the exchange between the residents of Paradise and the residents of the Fire, where both sides confirm that their Lord's promise proved true and the wrongdoers are then declared cursed (7:44).

---

## Verse 6:31

> Losers indeed are those who deny the meeting with Allah until the Hour takes them by surprise, then they will cry, “Woe to us for having ignored this!” They will bear ˹the burden of˺ their sins on their backs. Evil indeed is their burden

**Loss Declared Before the Loss Happens**

The sentence passes the verdict in the present tense and then shows the scene that will produce it. Those who deny the meeting with their Lord are already described as the losers, and the description does not wait for the surprise that will make the loss visible. The Book makes the same move in its own compact way when it opens the sūrah of the Passing of Time: humanity is in grave loss (103:2). The verdict is announced in the language of a business account that has already been closed.

Successful indeed is the one who purifies their soul, and doomed is the one who corrupts it (91:9–10).

**"The Meeting With Allah" Is the Book's Name for It**

The denial is described as denying the meeting with Allah, not as denying a biological event, and the choice of words shapes the whole passage. The Book uses this expression consistently for the people it is describing: those who do not expect to meet Us, pleased with this worldly life and heedless of the signs (10:7). Those who disbelieve in Allah's signs and the meeting with Him, who will have no hope in His mercy (29:23); and those who reject the signs and the meeting with their Lord, whose deeds are rendered void so that they will not be given any weight on Judgment Day (18:105).

The phrase places the issue where scripture wants it, which is the face-to-face accounting rather than the mechanics of revival. Its opposite is stated with the same brevity in the sūrah of the Cow, where the description of the guided includes those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and that to Him they will return (2:46). The difference between the two groups is not information about the afterlife. It is whether the possibility was allowed to govern how they lived.

**The Hour That Arrives Without an Appointment**

The surprise is the detail that turns a denial into a personal emergency. The Book records it with the same verb in several places. The Hour will take them by surprise, leaving them stunned, unable to avert it or delay it (21:40). Do they feel secure that an overwhelming torment will not overtake them, or that the Hour will not take them by surprise when they least expect it (12:107); are they waiting for the Hour to come upon them suddenly when they least expect it (43:66).

The Prophet himself is told to say that he has no power over his own benefit or protection except by God's will, and that if he knew the unseen he would have used the knowledge to his advantage (7:188). The concealment of the date is a deliberate arrangement, and it is precisely what the Makkan denial interpreted as evidence that no date existed.

**Burdens Carried on the Back**

The verse ends with an image of physical transport: they will bear their sins on their backs. The Book uses the same picture for the burden of a warning refused, where the reminder becomes a load in the Hereafter for those who ignored it (20:100–101). For the leaders who carry their own burdens in full as well as some of the burdens of those they misled without knowledge (16:25). The legal principle that no soul bears the burden of another (6:164) is untouched by the image, since what is being described is a load the person acquired through his own choices.

The Book records similar cries in the later passages that describe the same discovery, including the plea of those who see the torment and ask whether there is any way back (42:44), and the man who wishes he had sent something forward for his true life (89:24).

---

## Verse 6:32

> This worldly life is no more than play and amusement, but far better is the ˹eternal˺ Home of the Hereafter for those mindful ˹of Allah˺. Will you not then understand

**A Description of Attachment, Not of Creation**

The sentence is often misread as a condemnation of the world itself, and the Book itself closes that misreading. God did not create the heavens and the earth and everything in between without purpose (38:27), and the same claim is repeated where the creation is said to have been made for a purpose and an appointed term (30:8). The earth, the harvest, work, marriage, and play are not the problem. The problem is a life that reaches no further than they do and treats them as the whole account.

The sūrah of Iron lists the stages in a single verse — play, amusement, glitter, mutual boasting, and vying for increase in property and children (57:20) — which shows that the Book is describing a progression rather than a single activity. What begins as amusement matures into competition for standing, and that competition is what consumes a life.

**Why Play Is the Right Comparison**

The same judgement is repeated in other sūrahs, where this worldly life is described as no more than play and amusement, while the Hereafter is called the real life (29:64), and where the world's attractiveness is set against the Home of settlement (40:39). The vocabulary is stable because the doctrine is: what perishes cannot be the point of a creature who does not.

**The Comparison Stated Without a Formula**

The verse then states the alternative in the plainest terms: the Home of the Hereafter is far better for those mindful of Allah. The Book repeats this comparison across several sūrahs, sometimes with the added reassurance that the eternal Home is an accommodation from God and that what is with Him is best for the virtuous (3:198). Sometimes with a warning aimed at those who trade the Hereafter for the present life so that their punishment is neither reduced nor averted (2:86).

The qualifier is what keeps the sentence from being a slogan. The Hereafter is better for the mindful, not automatically for everyone who acknowledges its existence. The Book does not offer the next world as a consolation prize for a life spent ignoring it, and the sūrah's own argument has been that the denial of the meeting is what removes hope (29:23).

**The Question That Closes the Verse**

Will you not then understand? The verse ends by appealing to the faculty it has been addressing, and the appeal is the same one the Book uses when it points to the alternation of night and day, the revived earth, and the record kept of every deed. Understanding, in this vocabulary, is not the ability to grasp an idea but the willingness to act on one.

The Book repeats the appeal in other settings — will you not then be mindful, after describing a man who took his own desires as his god (45:23), and will you not then understand, after pointing to the earth and the sky as signs (12:109).

The Prophet expressed the same relationship in a sentence reported in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2956: this world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever.

---

## Verse 6:33

> We certainly know that what they say grieves you ˹O Prophet˺. It is not your honesty they question—it is Allah’s signs that the wrongdoers deny

**The Grief Is Acknowledged Before It Is Answered**

The verse begins by naming a private experience and confirming it from the highest authority. God knows that what they say grieves the Prophet, and the knowledge is stated before any instruction or consolation is offered. The Book does the same elsewhere: We certainly know that your heart is truly distressed by what they say (15:97). Do not let their words grieve you, for all honour and power belongs to Allah (10:65); and be patient, for your patience is only with Allah's help, and do not grieve over those who disbelieve (16:127).

**The Distinction That Makes the Verse Famous**

The sentence then draws a line the Prophet could not have drawn for himself: it is not you they are denying, but the signs of Allah that the wrongdoers deny. The denial is real, and it is aimed at the message rather than at the messenger's credibility, because the audience had already settled the question of his credibility years before. The man they were disputing with was the one they had named the trustworthy, and the Book itself points to his record when it has him say that he had lived a whole lifetime among them before the revelation came (10:16).

The distinction appears in other forms where the Book ties the treatment of the messenger to the treatment of the One who sent him. Those who pledge allegiance to you are actually pledging allegiance to Allah (48:10), and the Prophet is told to say that if they sincerely love Allah they should follow him (3:31). The message and its bearer can be separated in analysis, and the Book separates them so that the rejection is correctly located.

**What It Means to Reject a Sign**

The thing being denied is not an idea the audience found improbable. The sūrah uses the word signs for the created order as well as for the recited verses, and the Makkans had been living inside both. Their caravans navigated by the stars the Book describes as guidance through the darkness of land and sea (6:97), and they were addressed in a language they loved by a man they respected. To reject the signs is to refuse the route those things point to, not to dispute that the things exist.

The Book states the consequence of that refusal in a form that fits this passage: when a people is arrogant in the land, God turns them away from the signs, so that even if they see every sign they will not believe in it (7:146). The denial is so an activity of the will rather than a failure of the mind, which is why the Prophet's honesty is not really in question.

**The Comfort Given to Everyone Who Speaks After Him**

The line has outlived its first application. Any person who carries a message and is mocked for it can be tempted to hear the mockery as a verdict on himself, and the verse keeps that from happening by separating the bearer from the message. The Book goes further in the same direction where it tells the Prophet not to grieve himself to death over their denial (18:6; 26:3), since he is not responsible for an outcome he cannot control.

---

## Verse 6:34

> Indeed, messengers before you were rejected but patiently endured rejection and persecution until Our help came to them. And Allah’s promise ˹to help˺ is never broken. And you have already received some of the narratives of these messengers

**Rejection as the Standing Condition of the Work**

The verse hands the Prophet a fact about his own office: the messengers before him were rejected. The Book states it flatly in several places, naming the messengers generally rather than complaining about any single people. If you are rejected by them, so too were messengers before you who came with clear proofs, divine Books, and enlightening Scriptures (3:184); if you are rejected by them, so too were messengers before you, and to Allah all matters will be returned (35:4).

Those earlier messengers are named by community in other passages, where the people of Noah, ʿĀd. Pharaoh of the mighty structures are listed among those who denied the truth (38:12), and where every community is described as having plotted against its prophet to seize him and argued in falsehood to discredit the truth (40:5). The pattern is not a series of unfortunate incidents. It is the ordinary reception of a message by the society it challenges.

**Patience Until the Help Arrived**

The second clause defines the shape of that patience with a time limit: they endured until Our help came to them. The Book insists on that order in every account. Our help came to the messengers at the moment when they and their followers had reached the end of their resources (12:110). We certainly help Our messengers and the believers both in this worldly life and on the Day the witnesses will stand forth (40:51). And the believers are told that they will not enter Paradise without being tested as those before them were, until they ask when Allah's help will come, and are told that it is always near (2:214).

The wording keeps the Prophet from measuring his position by the middle of the story. A messenger standing in the middle of his mission can see only the rejection, and the verse tells him where the account is going, in the same way the Book tells the whole community that its help is guaranteed in the end.

**The Promise That Cannot Be Broken**

Then the verse states the guarantee without any conditionality: Allah's promise to help is never broken. The Book records the promise itself in the sūrah of the Ranged Ranks, where God's word has gone forth to His servants, the messengers, that they would surely be helped and that His forces would prevail (37:171–173). Again in the sūrah of the Pleading Woman, where God has decreed that He and His messengers will certainly prevail (58:21).

The same guarantee is given about God's word in general, where the Word of the Lord is perfected in truth and justice and none can change His words (6:115). Where the Book tells its readers that there is no change in the promise of Allah and that this is the ultimate triumph (10:64). What the verse offers the Prophet is therefore not encouragement but information. The outcome was announced before the dispute began.

**What the Help Looked Like**

In the Makkan period a substantial body of those accounts had been revealed. The Book states their function plainly when it says that the stories of the messengers are related to reassure the heart, and that in these stories there is the truth, a warning for the disbelievers, and a reminder for the believers (11:120; 12:111).

---

## Verse 6:35

> If you find their denial unbearable, then build—if you can—a tunnel through the earth or stairs to the sky to bring them a ˹more compelling˺ sign. Had Allah so willed, He could have guided them all. So do not be one of those ignorant ˹of this fact˺

**A Challenge Built to Be Impossible**

The verse offers the Prophet a way out of his distress and then closes it in the same sentence. If the denial is unbearable, he may go underground and dig his way out, or climb a stairway into the sky, to bring them a sign they cannot dismiss. The conditional "if you can" leaves no doubt that he cannot, and the rhetorical shape of the instruction is the argument: no effort available to a human being can produce the outcome he wants.

The Book uses the same device elsewhere with the same intention. Ask them whether, if God made the night perpetual for them until the Day of Judgment, any god besides Allah could bring them sunlight (28:71), and the same question is repeated about perpetual day and the gift of night to rest in (28:72). The impossibility is not stated as a limitation on God. It is stated as a limitation on the audience's demand.

**The Sign That Was Already in Their Hands**

The demand for a more compelling sign had already been answered in a way the Makkans could examine. The Book challenges them directly: if they are in doubt about what was revealed to the servant of God, let them produce a sūrah like it and call their helpers besides Allah, if what they say is true (2:23). Let them produce ten fabricated sūrahs like it (11:13); and if all humans and jinn were to combine to produce the equivalent of this Qur'an, they could not produce its equal, no matter how they supported one another (17:88).

So the tunnel and the stairway are not the only routes to a sign. The sign was reciting in the streets and marketplaces of Makkah in their own language, and the challenge to match it stood open for anyone who thought the message was human work.

**"Had Allah Willed, He Could Have Guided Them All"**

The middle of the verse states the fact that settles the question: had God willed, He could have guided them all. The same claim appears in several places with variations. Had Allah willed, He could have easily made all humanity into a single community of believers. He admits into His mercy whoever He wills (42:8). Had He willed, He could have made the whole earth believe, so no one is asked to force people into belief (10:99); and had He willed, they would not have associated partners with Him, and the Prophet is told that he has not been appointed as their keeper or their maintainer (6:107).

Commentators drew opposite conclusions from such verses.

---

## Verse 6:36

> Only the attentive will respond ˹to your call˺. As for the dead, Allah will raise them up, then to Him they will ˹all˺ be returned

**The Division Drawn by the Answer Rather Than by the Messenger**

The verse states a limit that the previous passage has already prepared. After the impossible tunnel and stairway, and after the reminder that God could have guided everyone had He willed, the sūrah now says who actually responds: the attentive.

Those who respond to the call of their Lord will have the finest reward. Those who do not respond would offer everything in the world twice over to ransom themselves (13:18); and in the sūrah of Yā-Sīn, the Prophet is told that he can only warn those who follow the reminder and stand in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him (36:11).

**Hearing With Something Other Than the Ears**

The Book's use of hearing for spiritual comprehension is consistent enough to be called a doctrine. The sūrah has already described listeners with veils on their hearts and deafness in their ears, who hear the recitation and leave it unchanged (6:25). Elsewhere the imagery is the same: those who deny the signs are wilfully deaf and dumb, lost in darkness (6:39); the worst of all beings in the sight of God are the wilfully deaf and dumb who do not understand (8:22).

**Who the Dead Are in This Sentence**

The second half of the verse turns to the dead, and commentators read the word in two ways that are not mutually exclusive. Some understand it as a reference to the disbelievers, meaning that they will be raised along with everyone else and judged for their disbelief. Others read it as a description of spiritual death, since a person who has cut himself off from revelation is, in the Book's vocabulary, dead while still walking.

The two readings meet in the Qur'anic image where the Prophet is told that he cannot make the dead hear the truth, nor make the deaf hear the call when they turn their backs and walk away (27:80; 30:52), and where the dead and the living are explicitly said not to be equal, with the added explanation that God alone makes whoever He wills hear (35:22). The verse in front of us uses the same pairing of attention and death, and then attaches the resurrection to it.

**Resurrection Stated as a Fact About Both Groups**

The clause about resurrection is not a threat aimed only at the deaf. To Him they will all be returned, and the Book puts the guarantee of that gathering in ownership: the One who raises the dead is the One who holds the property rights over everyone who dies. The sūrah has already asked its audience who created them from clay and appointed terms for their death, and it noted the second term that rests with God alone (6:2).

Placing the resurrection here serves a purpose in the argument. The people being addressed had denied the meeting with their Lord (6:31), and the answer to their denial is not an argument from nature or from the ruins of earlier nations. It is the statement that the gathering is happening, together with the observation that the same authority governs both who hears in this life and who comes back in the next.

---

## Verse 6:37
> They ask, “Why has no ˹other˺ sign been sent down to him from his Lord?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah certainly has the power to send down a sign”—though most of them do not know

**A Demand That Keeps Coming Back**

The request is recorded in the Book more often than any other objection to the Prophet's mission, and it always arrives in the same form: if only a sign could be sent down to him from his Lord (13:7). The same demand appears where people with no knowledge ask for God to speak to them or for a sign to come to them (2:118), where they ask why no other sign has been sent down (10:20), where they demand a sign from his Lord as though the previous ones did not exist (20:133).

Where they say that if signs had been sent down, they would certainly have believed (29:50).

The Book also records the oaths the demanders attached to their requests: they swore their most solemn oaths that if a sign came they would believe in it. The answer they received was that signs belong to God, and that even the sight of one would not produce belief in them (6:109). The demand in this verse therefore arrives with a history attached, which is why the response is so brief.

**The Precedent That Makes a Sign Dangerous**

The sūrah has already treated this subject in its discussion of the demand for an angel. Had an angel been sent down, the matter would have been settled at once and no further time for repentance would have been granted (6:8). The table sent down to the disciples of Jesus carries the same warning in a different register: whoever denies it afterwards will be subjected to a torment never inflicted on anyone else in creation (5:114–115).

So the sign is not simply evidence. It is an increase of accountability, and the Mercy that withholds it is the same Mercy the sūrah has already said God prescribed for Himself (6:12). A people who demanded a proof they had already decided to reject were asking for something that would remove the interval in which they were still able to change.

**The Sign That Was Standing in the Room**

That challenge was addressed to people whose whole culture was built on the value of words, which is why the Book treats their inability to meet it as decisive. The demand for a different sign asks for something other than the evidence already supplied, and the sūrah has shown earlier what the audience did with the evidence in its hands.

**The Consistency of the Sūrah's Answers**

Every demand that has been raised in this passage receives the same kind of answer. The request for an angel was answered with the fact that the angel's arrival would end the opportunity to repent (6:8). The request for a written book was answered with the audience's verdict on it before it arrived (6:7).

The pattern is deliberate, and the Book names it in another sūrah where the Prophet is told to say that he only follows what is revealed to him and that the recitation itself is insight, guidance, and mercy for those who believe (7:203).

Allah certainly has the power to send down a sign, and elsewhere the Book states that if He willed, He could send down upon the people a compelling sign from the heavens, leaving their necks bent in submission to it (26:4).

---
## Verse 6:38

> All living beings roaming the earth and winged birds soaring in the sky are communities like yourselves. We have left nothing out of the Record. Then to their Lord they will be gathered all together

The word translated as communities is *ummah*, a term the Book normally uses for religious communities formed by a shared relationship to God — the believing community addressed as the best community brought forth for humanity (3:110), and every community having a messenger sent to it (10:47). Applying the word to birds and crawling things widens its range in a way that has no parallel in the sūrah, and the choice of vocabulary is the claim.

**What the Rest of the Book Says About Them**

That reading is not an inference from a single word. The Book reports that God revealed to the bees how to build their hives and produce their honey. It calls the product a healing for people (16:68–69); it states that everything in the heavens and the earth glorifies God, and that human beings do not understand their glorification (17:44); and it records that the mountains and the birds joined David in praise (38:18–19). The same claim appears where all creatures in the heavens and the earth are said to declare His praise (24:41).

**Gathered to Their Lord**

Then to their Lord they will be gathered all together. The verb is the one used for the resurrection of human beings, and it is also used of the Day when the wild beasts will be gathered (81:5). Commentators differed over what the gathering of animals means, with some holding that it refers to their resurrection and judgment and others that it means they will be gathered to their deaths. The reports that are authentic on the subject support the first reading in substance.

The most cited is transmitted from Abū Hurayrah in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim 2582, where the Prophet said that rights will certainly be restored to those entitled to them on the Day of Resurrection, to the point that even the hornless sheep will lay claim upon the horned one. The same report appears in Jāmiʿ al-Tirmidhī 2420, where Abū ʿĪsā grades it ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ. What the hadith establishes is that the divine reckoning extends to wrongs that no human court would ever consider.

**What the Verse Requires of Human Beings**

If all creatures are communities with a relationship to their Creator, then the permission human beings have to use animals for food, transport, and labour is a delegation with terms rather than an unrestricted title. The Book does say that the earth and its contents have been subjected to human beings (14:32; 16:14; 22:65), and the same Book says that every creature's provision rests with God (11:6) and that there is no creature on earth whose provision God does not guarantee (29:60).

Those two sets of verses were not treated as contradictory by the scholars who read them together; they were treated as the terms of a trust. The report about the hornless sheep makes the same point from the other direction, since cruelty and waste in the use of creatures are named in the Book among the things that will be answered for.

---

## Verse 6:39

> Those who deny Our signs are ˹wilfully˺ deaf and dumb—lost in darkness. Allah leaves whoever He wills to stray and guides whoever He wills to the Straight Way

**Three Conditions in One Sentence**

The verse describes the deniers with three images that cover the whole of a person: hearing, speech, and sight. They are deaf, so the message does not enter; dumb, so no truthful word comes out; and in darkness, so nothing around them can be seen. The Book uses the same combination elsewhere, where those who are wilfully deaf, dumb, and blind will never return to the right path (2:18), and where the example of the disbelievers is a flock that understands nothing of the shepherd's calls and cries, deaf, dumb, and blind with no understanding (2:171).

**Darkness in the Plural**

The sūrah opened by saying that God made darkness and light (6:1), and the Book consistently renders darkness in the plural while light stays singular. The disbelievers are led out of light into darkness, and their false gods are the ones doing the leading (2:257); the Book was revealed to bring people out of darkness into light (14:1); God showers His blessings so that He may bring the believers out of darkness and into light (33:43).

**The Wilful Element in the Translation**

The rendering supplies the word wilfully, and it is not an embellishment. The Book elsewhere explains the condition as the result of what the person did: those whose hearts are sealed with their hearing. Whose sight is covered, are the ones who suffer a tremendous punishment (2:7). When they are reminded of the revelations and turn away, forgetting what their own hands did, veils are cast over the hearing and the hearts (18:57). And those who take their own desires as a god are left to stray knowingly, with their hearing and hearts sealed and a cover placed on their sight (45:23).

The same sequence appears in the story of the punishment that did not produce humility: instead of humbling themselves when suffering came, their hearts hardened and Satan made their misdeeds appealing to them (6:43). The covering in these passages is described as following on a decision, which is why the translation can fairly say that the deafness is chosen.

**The Deeper Question the Verse Raises**

Since God is said to leave some to stray, the sentence opens a question the scholars of the Book took seriously. The Muʿtazilites argued that God's leading astray cannot be a direct act of misguidance, since that would make the punishment of the misguided unjust, and they read it as the withholding of a grace that would have made the person receptive. The Ashʿarites held that God creates all acts, including human choices, and that guidance and misguidance are determined by His will.

The verses that settle the question most cleanly are the ones that connect the straying to what the people were already doing: the ones who break the covenant and spread corruption are left to stray (2:26). The wrongdoers are left to stray after they took the wrong course (14:27); and Joseph's people were left to stray after they continued doubting what he had brought (40:34). The Book attaches the leaving to a prior condition in every case, and it never describes a person as misled before he chose.

---

## Verse 6:40

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Imagine if you were overwhelmed by Allah’s torment or the Hour—would you call upon any other than Allah ˹for help˺? ˹Answer me˺ if your claims are true

**A Question That Uses the Audience's Own Experience**

The verse takes the Makkans to a situation every traveller in Arabia knew: a moment of overwhelming danger. Facing the punishment of God or the arrival of the Hour, the question asks whether they would call on anything besides Allah. It is not a question about their theology. It is a question about their conduct in the one situation where conduct becomes involuntary, and the answer is demanded with an oath-like challenge: answer me, if your claims are true.

**The Sea That Made Them Monotheists**

The Book records the answer in several places. When they are touched with hardship at sea, they forget every deity they normally invoke, except Him, and when He brings them safely to shore, they turn away (17:67). When a gale overwhelms a ship and the passengers assume they are doomed, they cry out to Allah alone in sincere devotion, promising gratitude in exchange for rescue (10:22). Waves like mountains produce the same result: they call on Him alone, and once delivered, only some remain grateful (31:32). The same pattern is reported in the sūrah of the Spider, where after rescue they associate others with Him once again (29:65).

The sūrah of al-Anʿām itself has already asked the question in a similar form: who rescues you from the darkest times on land and at sea, the One you call upon in humility, openly and secretly, saying that if He rescues you, you will be grateful (6:63). The answer supplied there is that only Allah rescues from this and any other distress, and yet they associate others with Him (6:64).

**Why Emergency Produces Sincerity**

The phenomenon the verse relies on has a simple explanation. A person in a storm has run out of intermediaries. The stone in its niche cannot still the wind, the tribal patron cannot reach the ship, and the star cannot alter the course of the water. What remains is the One the Makkans had never denied as the Creator, and the instinct that reaches for Him under those conditions is the plainest evidence that the deeper knowledge was never actually lost.

That is why the verse can be called an argument from the audience's own nature. The Book elsewhere notes that even the idolaters, when asked who created the heavens and the earth, answer that Allah did (31:25; 43:9). The crisis on the water simply dismantles the layer of practice that had been built over that confession.

**"If Your Claims Are True"**

The challenge at the end of the verse places the burden of proof on the people making the claims. The Makkans claimed that their deities had standing and could be approached for help. The verse puts the claim into the only test the claimants had never had to face, and asks them to supply the evidence from their own experience.

There is a pointed restraint in the demand. The Book does not ask them to abandon the idols on the basis of a revelation they did not accept. It asks them to notice what they themselves do when revelation is the last thing on their mind, and to draw the conclusion from the one moment when their practice and their belief were forced to agree.

---

## Verse 6:41

> No! He is the only One you would call. And if He willed, He could remove the affliction that made you invoke Him. Only then will you forget whatever you associate with Him ˹in worship˺.”

**The Answer Supplied Before the Audience Speaks**

The verse opens with a refusal that is also the answer to the previous question. No, they would not call on anyone else; He is the only One you would call. The Book frequently supplies the answer on behalf of the people it questions, and it does so because the answer is not in dispute. Everyone in the audience knew what happens to a man in a storm, and the verse simply records it as a fact about them.

What makes the sentence forceful is its second-person address. The Prophet is told to say "you," not "they." Each person listening is described to himself in the moment of his own greatest need, which is a more difficult position to argue from than a general statement about idolaters in the abstract.

**The Removal That Depends on His Will**

The next clause preserves God's freedom in the answer: if He willed, He could remove the affliction that made you invoke Him. The Book states elsewhere that God responds to those who call on Him (2:186), and that He has proclaimed that His servants should call on Him and that He will respond (40:60), and it also refuses to make the response a mechanical consequence of the request.

Commentators drew different conclusions from this.

**The Forgetting That Follows Rescue**

The verse closes on a diagnosis of the rescued: only then will you forget whatever you associate with Him. The Book documents this pattern in the same detail in several places. When hardship touches people they cry out to their Lord, turning to Him alone, and as soon as He gives them a taste of His mercy, a group of them associate others with their Lord again (30:33). The gratitude promised at sea is not delivered on land, as the sūrah itself has stated a few verses earlier (6:64).

The word "forget" is exact. The idols were not refuted in the storm; they simply were not there, and once the danger passes the memory of their absence fades with it. The Book treats that forgetting as the real problem, because a person who has seen the truth and lost it is in a different position from a person who never saw it.

**What the Confession Proves About the Idols**

The verse functions as a demonstration of the idols' standing, and the demonstration uses the audience's own conduct rather than an argument about the stones. The Book presses the point elsewhere in a challenge addressed to the same people: those you invoke besides Allah are created beings like yourselves, so call upon them and see if they will answer you, if your claims are true (7:194). It then asks whether those objects have feet to walk with, hands to hold with, eyes to see with, or ears to hear with (7:195).

The Man of the Whale cried out in the veils of darkness that there is no god worthy of worship except God, and that he had certainly done wrong, and the answer came: So We answered his prayer and rescued him from anguish, and so do We save the true believers (21:87–88).

---

## Verse 6:42

> Indeed, We have sent messengers before you ˹O Prophet˺ to other people who We put through suffering and adversity ˹for their denial˺, so perhaps they would be humbled

**Suffering and Adversity as Instruments**

The verse names two forms of hardship, and commentators distinguish between them: the first involving loss of wealth and straitened circumstances, and the second illness and physical suffering. Others read the pair as covering the whole range of affliction that befalls a community, from famine and drought to defeat and disease. The sūrah of the Heights describes the same programme in almost identical words: whenever a prophet was sent to a society, its people were afflicted with suffering and adversity so that they might be humbled (7:94).

Both forms are described in the Book as tests rather than as punishments, and the same two experiences are named among the marks of the righteous, who are said to be patient in times of suffering, adversity, and battle (2:177). That dual use of the vocabulary matters. The same event can be a purification for one person and a summons for another, and the verse is concerned with how a people responds to being shown its own helplessness.

**Why Hardship Was Thought to Produce Humility**

A community that has grown comfortable tends to treat its prosperity as a verdict on its conduct. When the rain fails, the caravan is lost, and the children fall ill, the arrangement that supported that confidence has been interrupted, and the interruption is meant to be noticed. The Book says elsewhere that God tested the dispersed tribes with prosperity and adversity so that they might return to the right path (7:168), and that testing with good and evil is the standing condition of human life (21:35).

**The Consolation for the Messenger**

For the Prophet, the verse carries the same message as the passage about rejected messengers a few lines earlier. The difficulties of the mission are not unique events; they are the ordinary shape of a summons that a society does not want to hear. The Book consoles its messenger by showing him the pattern and telling him that the outcome of every previous case is known.

That pattern also removes a burden he might have carried. If earlier communities had suffered as his own people were suffering, then the suffering was not evidence that he had failed or that God had withdrawn from him. The verse adds the Makkans to a list that already included communities whose stories the Prophet had been reciting, and the next sentence asks why the list ends the way it does.

The universality of the pattern is stated elsewhere in the Book in a form that leaves no community outside it. Every community has had a warner (35:24), a messenger was sent to every community with the call to worship God and shun false gods (16:36), and once a community's messenger has come, judgment is passed on it in fairness and it is not wronged (10:47). The Makkans were therefore not being singled out for hardship. They were being treated at the point in the sequence where their own messenger had arrived.

The Book states the same claim in other sūrahs, where messengers were sent to communities before and Satan made their misdeeds appealing to them (16:63).

---

## Verse 6:43

> Why did they not humble themselves when We made them suffer? Instead, their hearts were hardened, and Satan made their misdeeds appealing to them

**Hardening as a Response to the Wrong Kind of Relief**

Hardness of heart is described in the Book as a condition that can develop in anyone who receives a great deal of something without being changed by it. The sūrah of Iron warns the believers not to become like those who were given the Scripture before them, who were spoiled for so long that their hearts became hard (57:16). The children of Israel are said to have hardened after the covenant was broken (5:13), and their hearts are compared to rock, or harder than rock (2:74).

**How Misdeeds Come to Look Beautiful**

The second half of the verse names the mechanism by which the hardening is rewarded: Satan made their misdeeds appealing to them. The phrase recurs in the Book with remarkable frequency, and it is used for the entire range of conduct that a society persuades itself is normal. Satan made their deeds appealing to them and hindered them from the way (27:24); he made their deeds appealing to them in the battle of Badr and then abandoned them (8:48); evil associates were set on the wrongdoers and made their past and future misdeeds appealing to them (41:25).

The same phrase is used of Pharaoh, whose evil deeds were made so appealing that he was hindered from the right way (40:37), and of the pagans who were made to see the killing of their own children as acceptable (6:137). In each case the misdeed is not denied. It is renamed, decorated, and made into a practice with reasons attached.

**The Partnership Between the People and Their Tempter**

The verse names Satan as the agent, and it does not by this excuse the people, since the question in the first half was directed at them. The Book treats the relationship as one in which both parties are active: Satan tempts (15:39), and the person who follows him has chosen to follow. The Book also states that the delusion is a general tendency of human beings who prefer the immediate (2:212), where the life of this world has been made appealing to the disbelievers, and where desires of every kind have been made attractive to people (3:14).

That combination is what gives the verse its weight. The people wanted what they were being shown, and they wanted the reasons that made it acceptable. The tempter supplied the vocabulary; the appetite was already in place.

**What the Verse Leaves the Reader Holding**

When famine and crop failure struck them (7:130), their response was to divide the events by their own convenience: in prosperity they said that what they had was what they deserved. In adversity they blamed it on Moses and those with him, while the Book notes that all of it was destined by Allah and that most of them did not know (7:131).

The Book records the same reproach in the sūrah of the Believers, where God says that the people were seized with torment and yet never humbled themselves to their Lord, nor appealed to Him submissively (23:76).

---

## Verse 6:44

> When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise, then they instantly fell into despair

**Seized at the Moment of Exultation**

Then the seizure comes: just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise. The timing is precise. It is not during the hardship, when the people were still asking, but at the peak of the recovered confidence, when the lesson had been fully forgotten and the prosperity had been fully credited to their own merit.

**The Word "Became Oblivious" and What It Covers**

The verse describes a people who forgot what they had been reminded of, and the reminder in view is the lesson written into their own suffering. Commentators read the phrase in two ways: either they forgot the message the prophet had brought them, or they forgot the lesson their own hardship should have taught them about their complete dependence on God.

The Book states the same charge elsewhere when it describes people who call on God while lying on their side, sitting, or standing, and who then return to their old ways as soon as the hardship is lifted, as if they had never cried for help at all (10:12).

The Book uses the same sequence in several accounts of destroyed nations. The people of the Heights argued that their forefathers had seen adversity and prosperity, and then they were seized while they were unaware (7:95); earlier societies were destroyed while they were settled in their comfortable living (28:58). The seizure by surprise is the standard ending, and it is chosen at the point where the warning would have had the least chance of being believed.

**The Test That Runs in Both Directions**

The overall shape of the passage is a test that ran twice, once through adversity and once through ease, and the people failed both halves. The Book names that double testing as the standing condition of humanity, since they are tried with good and evil alike and then returned to their Lord (21:35). What makes the passage so uncomfortable is the observation that the second half of the test is the more dangerous, because it feels like a reward.

---

## Verse 6:45

> So the wrongdoers were utterly uprooted. And all praise is for Allah—Lord of all worlds

**A Sentence of Removal, Not of Battle**

The verse reports the end of the peoples described in the preceding passage with a verb of cutting off. The image is agricultural as much as military: a people is described as uprooted, removed from the ground completely, so that nothing of it continues. The Book uses the same verb for the destruction of those who denied the signs in the time of Hūd (7:72), for the disbelievers at Badr (8:7), and for the decree given to Lot about the sinners of his city, that they would be uprooted in the morning (15:66).

The choice matters for how the audience is meant to read the passage. These communities were not defeated in a war; they were removed from the record as communities, and the uprooting is described as a consequence rather than an event. The Book describes the wind that destroyed ʿĀd in the same register, leaving its people like trunks of uprooted palm trees (69:6–7).

**Why Praise Follows Judgment**

The most striking feature of the verse is its second half: and all praise is for Allah, Lord of all worlds. The formula is the one that opens the Fātiḥah and the one the sūrah itself uses at its own opening (6:1). Placing it directly after a report of destruction is a deliberate religious statement, and the commentators explain it as praise for God's justice and for the patience He showed before the punishment fell.

Read that way, the praise is not a celebration of the destruction.

**The Two Vocabularies Held Together**

The sentence joins mercy and severity in a single breath, and the sūrah has been doing that from the beginning. It opened with praise for the Creator of the heavens and the earth and of darkness and light (6:1); it stated that God prescribed mercy for Himself while announcing the gathering for judgment (6:12); it described the wrongdoers as people who ruin only themselves while their Lord could have guided everyone (6:35). The final clause of this verse is the formula that holds the two together.

The Book returns to the pairing in other places where nations are destroyed and praise is given to God for His judgement, and it does so because the alternative is worse. A universe in which the oppressor is never removed is not an universe in which mercy means anything, and the praise is the recognition that the removal is part of the mercy.

**What the Verse Does for the Prophet**

The Prophet had been consoled a few verses earlier with the fact that earlier messengers were rejected and endured until help came (6:34), and with the impossibility of forcing his people to believe (6:35). The passage about the hardened communities supplies the other half of the picture: what happens when a people continues past the point at which the warning has been exhausted.

For a messenger in the middle of a hostile city, that is a difficult and necessary piece of information. It tells him what the end of the story is for a community that refuses, and it tells him that the ending is not arbitrary. The verse does not invite him to look forward to it. It tells him that the matter is in hands that handle it correctly.

---

## Verse 6:46

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Imagine if Allah were to take away your hearing or sight, or seal your hearts—who else other than Allah could restore it?” See ˹O Prophet˺ how We vary the signs, yet they still turn away

**The Faculties Named One by One**

The verse takes three gifts the audience used every hour and asks who could return them if they were withdrawn. Hearing, sight, and the heart are named because they are the whole apparatus of understanding: the ear receives, the eye observes, and the heart is where a person decides what the information means. The Book elsewhere treats the heart as the seat of comprehension rather than of emotion, and the sealing of hearts appears in several places as the end of that comprehension (2:7; 16:108; 45:23).

The question is put as a hypothesis rather than as a threat. Nobody is being told that the faculties have been removed; they are being asked to imagine the situation and to name a candidate who could undo it. The Book has already answered that question about harm and benefit in general a few verses earlier, where it said that none can undo harm except Him (6:17), and it repeats the answer elsewhere with the same firmness (10:107).

**The Question the Sūrah Has Asked Before**

This is the third time in the sūrah that an appeal of this shape has been made, and the repetition builds a case rather than padding one. Earlier, the audience was asked who rescues them from the darkest times on land and at sea (6:63), and a few verses before this one they were asked about being overwhelmed by the punishment (6:40). Each question uses something the audience already knows, and each one strips away another layer of the claim that the idols had standing.

**The "Varying" of the Signs**

The second half of the verse turns to the method of revelation itself: see how We vary the signs, yet they still turn away. The Book uses this vocabulary repeatedly, and the sūrah names the practice in more than one place. To vary the signs means to bring the same truth through different arguments, images, and parables, so that an audience cannot claim it was offered only one kind of proof, or that the message depended on a single demonstration.

The idea appears where God says that every kind of example has been set out for people in this recitation, though most of them persist in disbelief (17:89), and where the sūrah says the parables and warnings are presented in various ways (18:54; 20:113). The verse therefore presents the diversity of the message as an act of care by God, and the audience's turning away as a response to a generosity they have no ground to complain about.

**The Emphasis on Sight at the End**

The verse closes with the Prophet as the observer of the turning away: see how they still turn away. The same instruction was given earlier in the sūrah, where the command to travel through the land and look at the fate of the deniers was followed by the observation that it is not the eyes that go blind but the hearts inside the chests (22:46). Here the object of sight is not a ruin but a living refusal that the Prophet encounters daily.

That emphasis carries a message for him.

---

## Verse 6:47

> Ask, “Imagine if Allah’s punishment were to overwhelm you with or without warning—who would be destroyed other than the wrongdoers?”

**Two Ways for a Punishment to Arrive**

The verse names the two possibilities an audience would have feared: a punishment that comes without warning, and one that comes openly. Commentators explain the pair as covering the night and the day, or as covering a coming that is felt before it strikes and a coming that arrives unannounced. The Book uses both patterns in its accounts of destroyed peoples, and it records towns in the Qur'an as having been destroyed at night while their inhabitants slept, or in the morning while they were playing (7:4; 7:98).

**Why the Timetable Was Left With God**

The sentence is addressed to a people who were pressing the Prophet to hasten something, and the answer removes the question from his hands completely. The Book records their demand in another sūrah where they called for stones to rain down from the sky if the message was true (8:32), and it reports people asking why the torment is not sent down (29:53). The verse treats the demand as a symptom of unbelief rather than as a request for information.

The Book states the underlying reason plainly: the postponement is not a sign that nothing is coming but a period in which the wrongdoer is given time, and the Book elsewhere describes the delay as a rope rather than as an acquittal (3:178). The verse in front of us supplies the warning side of that arrangement, and the next one describes the messenger's only role.

**The Two Groups Named in the Future**

The verse ends by naming the wrongdoers as the ones who will be destroyed, and the term is not accidental. The sūrah has been describing conduct rather than a label: obstructing others from the message (6:26), disputing after listening (6:25), demanding signs while rejecting the signs already given (6:37), and hardening after suffering (6:43). The wrongdoers in this sentence are those whose record contains those items, and the verse leaves each hearer to check the list against himself.

The next sentence describes the mission that had been given while that check is still possible. Messengers come as bearers of news and warnings, and the Book's habit of stating the messenger's role immediately after a threat is its way of keeping the door visible.

The demand behind this question is recorded elsewhere in a form that shows how the Makkans actually phrased it. They prayed that if the message was truly from God, He should rain down stones on them from the sky or bring a painful punishment upon them (8:32). The Book also notes that the wrongdoers have a share of torment like that of their predecessors, with an instruction not to ask for it to be hastened (51:59). The verse in front of us answers the tone of such demands rather than their content. A person asking for the punishment to come early is not requesting justice; he is testing whether anyone is listening.

---

## Verse 6:48

> We have sent messengers only as deliverers of good news and as warners. Whoever believes and does good, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve

**Two Functions and Nothing Besides Them**

The verse defines the office of the messenger in two words, and the construction is restrictive: messengers were sent only to bring good news and to warn. The Book repeats the pairing across many sūrahs, describing the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warners (2:213; 4:165; 18:56; 25:56; 33:45; 34:28; 35:24). The Prophet himself is told to say that he is only a warner and a bringer of good news to people who believe (7:188).

**Why Both Words Are Necessary**

The pairing is carefully balanced. Good news without warning would produce a religion of comfortable expectations, in which the mercy of God is separated from the seriousness of the accounting. Warning without good news would produce despair, and the Book states its own refusal of that result when it describes people who call upon God with hope and fear at once (32:16), and where it says that God has taken upon Himself to be merciful (6:12).

The two words therefore hold the messenger's task in a single direction.

**What It Means to Have No Fear or Grief**

The two words cover the two directions of human anxiety. Fear concerns what is coming, and grief concerns what has gone; the Book's promise removes both, which is why the formula recurs so often in descriptions of the Hereafter (7:35; 10:62; 46:13). Those who are promised it are told that the arrival of the judgment holds nothing for them to dread, and that the losses of their earthly lives are not the end of the account.

The Book also treats the promise as a present possession in some measure, since the people described as mindful are those who have no fear about their future and no regret over their past dealings with God. That is the practical form of the good news the messenger brings, and it is delivered in the same sentence as the warning to the people who reject him.

**The Messenger's Freedom From the Outcome**

Read together with the verses around it, this sentence also relieves the Prophet of a burden. He had been told that the punishment is not in his hands (6:47), and now he is told that his task is the delivery of news rather than the production of results. The Book repeats the point when it tells him that his duty is only to remind (88:21), and when it refuses to make him a keeper over the people (6:107).

That arrangement protects the message as well as the messenger. A prophet who could compel acceptance would destroy the moral content of the decision, and a prophet who carried the responsibility for other people's choices would be crushed by them. The verse states the boundary, and the next sentence states what happens on the other side of it.

Elsewhere it states the same pairing where it speaks of those who believe in God, the Last Day, and do righteous deeds, and says that they will have their reward with their Lord, with no fear for them and no grief (2:62; 2:262).

---

## Verse 6:49

> But those who deny Our signs will be afflicted with punishment for their rebelliousness

**Denial and Conduct Held Together**

The verse names two things about the people it describes: they deny the signs, and they have crossed the boundary. The Book's habit is to join belief with righteous action and denial with transgression, and it does so because the two belong together in its understanding of a human being. Faith that has no effect on conduct is not the faith the verse is describing, and misconduct that has no connection to belief is not the fault it is naming either.

**The Word Behind "Rebelliousness"**

The Arabic term describes the conduct of someone who steps outside a boundary he was meant to keep, and the Book uses it for people who break covenants and corrupt what they touch. The same root appears where the Book describes those who spread corruption in the land and violate their pledges (2:27), and where the people who cross the limits set for them are described as the ones who ruin themselves (2:229). The word indicates conduct rather than an inherited condition.

That view is stated plainly in the passage where the Book describes the righteous as those who keep their pledges, are patient in hardship. Give from what they have (2:177), and where it describes those who break the covenant, cut the ties God ordered to be maintained, and spread corruption as the true losers (2:27). The pairing runs through the whole Book, and this verse is a compact instance of it.

**Why the Punishment Is Attached Rather Than Described**

The verse does not describe what the punishment consists of, and this restraint is typical of the Makkan passages. Earlier in the sūrah the description was more explicit, with the wrongdoers at the point of death surrounded by angels stretching out their hands (6:93), and later the sūrah will describe the scene where the wrongdoers are held before the fire (6:27). Here the sentence ends at the fact.

Elsewhere the same sūrah fills in what is left out here. The wrongdoers are told at the point of death that they will be repaid with the torment of disgrace for lying about God and for being arrogant toward His signs (6:93). Those who take their faith as play and amusement are described as facing a boiling drink and a painful punishment for their disbelief (6:70). The absence of such detail in this verse is a matter of placement rather than of information. The sūrah states the two roads first and describes the destinations later, once the audience has been brought through the argument that makes the description bearable to hear.

**What the Verse Requires the Reader to Decide**

Both sentences in this pair are about groups, and neither one asks the reader to place himself in a group. The sūrah treats the reading itself as the point at which a person begins to decide, and the verses that follow turn immediately to the practical shape of the community being addressed, including the Prophet's treatment of the poor among his followers.

In the sūrah of the Heights, the children of Adam are told that whoever shuns evil and mends his ways will have no fear and no grief when messengers recite God's revelations to them (7:35). In the sūrah of the Cow the promise is attached to belief, good deeds, prayer, and alms (2:277).

---

## Verse 6:50

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I do not say to you that I possess Allah’s treasuries or know the unseen, nor do I claim to be an angel. I only follow what is revealed to me.” Say, “Are those blind ˹to the truth˺ equal to those who can see? Will you not then reflect?”

The verse lists three things the Prophet does not claim: the treasuries of God, knowledge of the unseen, and angelic status. Each one corresponds to a specific demand recorded elsewhere in the Book. The disbelievers asked why a treasure had not been sent down with him (11:12); they asked to see an angel, or for the messenger himself to be an angel (15:7; 17:94–95); and they demanded to know about the Hour (7:187; 43:53). The verse removes all three of the goods they were asking him to supply.

The order is deliberate. The first two would have made him a dispenser of power, and the third would have made him something other than a human being. What remains after the three are removed is a man with a message, which is exactly the position the sūrah has been defending since the demand for an angel was first raised (6:8). The wording of the denial is not unique to this Prophet, and the Book records it almost identically on the lips of Noah when his people accused him of seeking wealth and status through his message. He told them that he did not ask them for money, that his reward was with God, and that he was not going to drive away the believers (11:29; 11:31).

**The Unseen Belongs to God**

The Book states the principle behind the second denial in several places. With God are the keys of the unseen, and no one knows them except Him (6:59); He alone knows the unseen and reveals it to no one except the messengers of His choice (72:26–27); and the unseen is known only to God (10:20; 27:65). The revelation given to the Prophet contains news of the unseen, as when the Book tells him stories he could not have known, such as the account of Mary's guardianship (3:44) and the story of Joseph's brothers (12:102).

**The Challenge About Blindness and Sight**

The verse ends with a question that draws the line between two kinds of people rather than two kinds of faculty: are those blind equal to those who can see? The Book uses the same question in several places, including where it contrasts the one who stands on clear proof from his Lord with the one whose evil deeds have been made appealing to him (47:14). Where it asks whether the blind and the seeing can be equal, or the darkness and the light (13:16).
