The imagery refers to spiritual perception, and the Book makes that explicit when it says that it is not the eyes which go blind but the hearts inside the chests (22:46). The question is therefore not about the faculties a person happens to have. It is about what he does with them, which is the same argument the sūrah has made in its earlier discussion of veiled hearts and deafened ears (6:25).

The Book uses the same formula elsewhere when it has him say that he is nothing new among the messengers and that he does not know what will be done with him or with his people, and that he only follows what is revealed to him (46:9).

---

## Verse 6:51

> Warn with this ˹Quran˺ those who are awed by the prospect of being gathered before their Lord—when they will have no protector or intercessor besides Him—so perhaps they will be mindful ˹of Him˺

**The Fear That Makes a Person Reachable**

The Book treats fear of the gathering as the beginning of usefulness to the message, and it states the same principle elsewhere when it tells the Prophet that he can only warn those who follow the reminder and stand in awe of the Most Compassionate without seeing Him (36:11). The phrase "without seeing Him" matters, because it describes a fear that operates in the absence of evidence at hand, and it is the form of fear the Book commends.

That is why the verse's instruction is directed at this group and not at the men who kept demanding signs. A person who has already granted that he will be summoned to account has given the message something to work with, and the Book's method with such people is to increase their awareness rather than to argue them into an admission.

**No Protector and No Intercessor**

The verse describes the scene those listeners fear, and it describes it with two denials: apart from Him there is no protector and no intercessor. The Book makes the same statement in several places, including the passage where God says that there is no protector and no intercessor besides Him (32:4), and the one where the Prophet is told to warn a people who have no one to protect them from God (42:7).

Intercession is treated carefully in the Book rather than dismissed outright. The majority position among the scholars is that intercession exists by God's permission and for those He permits, which is why the verse's denial is expressed as a denial of any intercessor besides Him rather than of intercession as such. What the sentence removes is the assumption that led the Makkans to build their religious practice around intermediaries.

**Awaiting a Meeting to Prepare for It**

The phrase the translation renders as being gathered before their Lord is literally a meeting, and the Book uses that vocabulary deliberately both for the saved and for the lost. Those who are certain that they will meet their Lord and that to Him they will return are described among the guided (2:46), while those who do not expect the meeting are described as pleased with the present life and heedless of the signs (10:7).

So the difference between the two groups is not information about the event. It is expectation, and expectation is what produces preparation. A man who is expecting an appointment arranges his affairs accordingly, and the verse assumes that the listener who expects the meeting will act on the warning he receives.

**The Closing Purpose Clause**

The verse ends with the same formula the sūrah has used for the sending of messengers: so perhaps they will be mindful of Him. The word describes guarding oneself against something, and the Book attaches it to conduct rather than to sentiment.

The Book repeats the purpose clause in the passage about the Prophet's own instruction, where he is told to remind because his duty is only to remind (88:21), and it uses the same word when it tells him who can be warned at all. The identical phrase appears in the sūrah of the Cave, where the Prophet is told that he can warn only those who follow the reminder and fear their Lord unseen, and that he should give them news of forgiveness and a generous reward (36:11).

---

## Verse 6:52

> ˹O Prophet!˺ Do not dismiss those ˹poor believers˺ who invoke their Lord morning and evening, seeking His pleasure. You are not accountable for them whatsoever, nor are they accountable for you. So do not dismiss them, or you will be one of the wrongdoers

**"Seeking His Pleasure"**

The phrase translated as seeking His pleasure refers to a motive rather than an outward act, and the Book uses it repeatedly to describe sincerity. Those who give their wealth are described as doing so seeking God's face (2:272; 30:38–39), and the same phrase appears where the Prophet is told to stay with the poor rather than to court the powerful. Commentators read it as sincerity of intention, and some note that the literal sense concerns the hope of seeing God, which is what a honest worshipper's desire ultimately reaches toward.

The criterion that emerges is worth stating plainly. What qualified these people to be near the Prophet was not their lineage, their wealth, or their standing in the market. It was the direction of their intention, which is precisely the thing the notables could not see and were trying to assess anyway.

**The Line About Accountability**

The verse then removes a practical worry from the Prophet's shoulders: you are not accountable for them, nor are they accountable for you. The Book states the general principle elsewhere, that no soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another (6:164; 17:15), and it applies it here to a specific social anxiety. If the poor believers turned out to have mixed motives, that was God's assessment to make, and if their poverty offended the Makkans, their own accounts would be examined without reference to it.

Commentators read the sentence in two ways, with some taking the reckoning to concern the poor believers' inner sincerity, which the Prophet is not required to audit. Others taking it to concern the notables, whose acceptance of the message the Prophet should not pursue at the cost of the community he already had. Both readings converge on the same instruction: the door of the gathering is not his to narrow.

**What the Passage Does for the Rest of the Sūrah**

The instruction is placed in the middle of a long argument about idolatry, and its placement is not incidental. The sūrah has been exposing the fabricated hierarchies by which the Makkans divided their world, including the shares they assigned to their gods and to their own clans. The verse applies the same critique to the community the Prophet was building, where a distinction between first-class and second-class believers had been proposed to him.

The same scene is recorded from the other side in the story of Noah. His people argued that they could not believe in a man followed only by the lowest among them (26:111), and Noah answered that their judgment rested with his Lord and that he was not going to expel the believers (26:113–114). The disbelieving chiefs made the same complaint to him that the Makkan notables were making in this sūrah, and Noah asked his people who would protect him from God if he were to dismiss them (11:27; 11:30). The repetition of this argument across the sūrahs shows that the pressure being applied to the Prophet was not a local peculiarity; it was the standard demand made against a messenger by the powerful of every community.

---

## Verse 6:53

> In this way We have tested some by means of others, so those ˹disbelievers˺ may say, “Has Allah favoured these ˹poor believers˺ out of all of us?” Does Allah not best recognize the grateful

**Inequality as a Test Rather Than an Accident**

The verse states that the differences between people are arranged as a trial, and it states it in the first person: We have tested some by means of others. The Book treats disparities of wealth, strength, and status as part of the design rather than as faults in it. It says in another place that if God had willed He could have made humanity a single community, but the will is to test people with what they have been given (5:48).

The testing runs in both directions. The wealthy are tested by their responsibility toward the needy, and the needy are tested by their trust in God and by the patience required to accept help from those who have less standing. Neither condition is a verdict on the person living in it.

**The Question the Disbelievers Ask**

The Book records the same reasoning in the story of Noah, where the notables demanded that he drive away the believers of low standing, and he refused on the ground that he could not expel those whose account rested with God (11:29; 11:31). The assumption that spiritual worth follows social rank was old before Makkah, and the Book treats it as a recurring error.

**The Question Posed From Both Sides**

Commentators note that the question may equally be asked by the poor, looking at the wealth of the disbelievers and wondering why God continues to grant them prosperity while the believers remain poor and exposed to persecution. Under that reading the sentence covers both directions of the same bewilderment, and the verse's answer serves both groups at once.

The Book deals with the second question in several places. The disbelievers should not imagine that living longer is good for them, since they are only given more time to increase in sin (3:178), and the wealth of an enemy is not evidence about his standing with God. The answer to both versions of the question is the same: the present distribution of comfort is a field of testing rather than a table of verdicts.

**What the Grateful Are and Why the Word Is Chosen**

Does Allah not best recognize the grateful? The question at the end defines the quality that distinguishes one person from another in this arrangement, and the quality is gratitude. The Book links gratitude to perception of the giver, and it describes the person who uses his abundance to acknowledge God rather than to congratulate himself. The opposite failure is described in the same sūrah a few verses later, where people in prosperity credit themselves and in adversity blame God's messenger (7:131, in the sūrah of the Heights).

---

## Verse 6:54

> When the believers in Our revelations come to you, say, “Peace be upon you! Your Lord has taken upon Himself to be Merciful. Whoever among you commits evil ignorantly ˹or recklessly˺ then repents afterwards and mends their ways, then Allah is truly All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

**A Greeting That States a Doctrine**

The verse instructs the Prophet to greet the believers with the words of peace, and then to follow the greeting with the reason it can be said: their Lord has taken upon Himself to be merciful. The greeting is the one the Book records as the welcome of the people of Paradise (7:46; 14:23; 16:32; 19:62), and it is also the form of address used between the prophets and their guests, as when Abraham's visitors greeted him with peace (15:52; 51:25).

**The Mercy God Prescribed for Himself**

The sentence repeats a claim the sūrah made earlier, where God is described as having taken upon Himself to be merciful (6:12), and it is the only attribute the Book says God prescribed for Himself. The earlier verse stated the fact in the context of a warning about the gathering; this verse states it in the context of forgiveness for a person who has sinned and repented, which shows the doctrine at work.

The Book keeps the two contexts together in other passages, where mercy is described as encompassing all things while punishment falls on whoever God wills (7:156). The verse in front of us gives the practical reason for the self-imposed obligation. A community of human beings will contain people who fail, and the arrangement had to include a route back.

**"Commits Evil Ignorantly or Recklessly"**

The phrase describes the ordinary way in which people do wrong: not as a deliberate campaign against God but as a lapse, a failure of attention, or a decision made without weighing what it means. The Book uses the same description where it speaks of those who commit evil out of ignorance and then repent (4:17–18), and where it says that God accepts the repentance of the one who does wrong and then turns back.

That wording keeps the door wide while it keeps the requirement firm. The ignorance does not excuse the act, and it does not remove the need for repentance, but it puts the act inside the category of the foreseeable rather than the unforgivable. What the verse will not do is remove the conditions attached to the promise.

**The Report About God's Right Over His Servants**

The tafsīr literature connects this passage with a hadith in which the Prophet asked his Companions whether they knew the right of God over the servants. Answered that it is that they worship Him and do not associate partners with Him, and then asked whether they knew the right of the servants over God if they do that, and answered that He will not punish them. The report is quoted in the commentaries on this verse to show the reciprocal nature of what God has bound Himself to.

Read with the verse, the report explains the phrase about the Lord taking mercy upon Himself. The obligation is not something God owed anyone by nature; it is a commitment He announced, and the Book treats it as the ground on which a person may approach him with hope rather than despair.

---

## Verse 6:55

> This is how We make Our signs clear, so the way of the wicked may become distinct

**Clarity as a Deliberate Act**

The verse states that the signs have been made clear, and it states it as something God does rather than as something that happens. The Book uses this vocabulary repeatedly, and the sūrah alone names it several times, including where the signs are explained for people who know (6:97) and where the Prophet is told to follow the road that has been made plain (6:126). The same formula appears in the sūrah of Fussilat, which takes its title from the word.

The claim matters because the Makkans had been complaining about ambiguity. They wanted a sign that would settle the matter beyond argument, and the verse answers that the Qur'anic method is to make the truth clear by accumulated explanation, argument, warning, and illustration. What they were asking for was not clarity but the end of the discussion.

**The Purpose Named: Distinguishing a Road**

Then the verse states why the clarity is given: so that the way of the wicked may become distinct. The phrase is spatial, and the Book's imagery of two roads runs through it. There is a path and there is a deviation, and the Book's work of explanation is what makes the difference visible. The sūrah earlier used the same image where it asked whether the blind and the seeing could be equal (6:50), and where it described being guided to a Straight Way in contrast to being left to stray (6:39).

That purpose is easy to misread as hostile. The point is not that God marks out a group of people to condemn them but that the clarity of the message makes the course of conduct visible for what it is. A road can only be avoided by a person who can see where it goes.

**The Book's Insistence That the Message Is Not Cryptic**

The Book returns to this point in several places because it was a live complaint. It says that it has set out every kind of example for people in the recitation, though most of them persist in disbelief (17:89). It states the same in the sūrah of the Cave (18:54) and in the sūrah of Ṭā-Hā (20:113); and it describes the revelation as a Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail (11:1). The clearest statement of the principle is that the message is a clarification from God rather than a puzzle.

The Makkan objection, in the sūrah's own account, was not that the message was unclear. It was that the message was unwelcome, and the Book's response is to keep making the same points in fresh forms rather than to make them obscure.

**The Reader Left With the Two Roads**

A person reading the sentence is left with the choice the sentence describes. The clarity has been supplied, and the two paths have been laid out: the one that the believers walk with their Lord morning and evening (6:52), and the one the wicked take by crossing the bounds that the signs have marked. The sūrah does not present the matter as unresolved, and it does not present it as a question the reader is free to leave open.

The verses that follow give the Prophet the words to declare where he stands, and they give the audience the sight of a community that has already chosen its road.

---

## Verse 6:56

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I have been forbidden to worship those you invoke besides Allah.” Say, “I will not follow your desires, for I then would certainly be astray and not one of those ˹rightly˺ guided.”

**A Prohibition Stated in the First Person**

The verse begins with the Prophet announcing that he has been forbidden, and the passive construction carries the authority without requiring him to name himself as the source of the rule. The prohibition concerns the objects the Makkans invoke, which the sūrah has already examined from several angles: they can neither harm nor benefit (5:76), they cannot respond or protect (20:89), and they are created beings like the people who worship them (7:194).

Making the announcement in the first person is a public act of separation. The Prophet is not reporting a difference of opinion between himself and his people; he is declaring a boundary that has been drawn for him by someone else, which leaves no room for negotiation and no room for the political compromise the Makkans had been seeking.

**The Two Results of Following Desire**

The verse then states the consequence of the decision the Prophet refuses to make: for then I would certainly be astray and not one of those rightly guided. He is not describing a possible lapse in his own function as a messenger; he is describing what happens when a person with knowledge allows appetite to set the course. The Book states the same principle for all people when it describes those who take their own desire as a god and are then left to stray with their hearing sealed and a cover over their sight (45:23).

The language of straying and guidance returns here because the sūrah has just used it for the two roads (6:55). A person who follows desire has left the road, and the Book does not soften the description by suggesting that departure can be partial.

**What the Two Sayings Establish**

The next verses continue with the Prophet's declarations about the proof he stands on and the punishment his people were demanding he hasten. The sūrah is putting the whole of his position on the record in his own voice, so that no one could claim afterward that the terms had been left vague.

The Book shows what the compromise would have looked like in other passages, and none of them make pleasant reading. The Prophet is told that his opponents wished him to accommodate them so that they would accommodate him (68:9). The sūrah of al-Isrāʾ reports that they nearly lured him from what had been revealed to him, in the hope that he would attribute something to God that had not come from Him (17:73), a scheme the Book says would have brought him a double punishment in this life and the next (17:75). The same refusal is stated in the shortest terms in the sūrah of the Disbelievers, which ends the matter with the declaration that each party has its own way (109:6).

The same warning is given to the Prophet repeatedly, including where the Book tells him not to follow the desires of the People of the Book (5:48–49) and where it warns him against their wish that he compromise (17:73).

---

## Verse 6:57
> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Indeed, I stand on a clear proof from my Lord—yet you have denied it. That ˹torment˺ you seek to hasten is not within my power. It is only Allah Who decides ˹its time˺. He declares the truth. And He is the Best of Judges.”

**A Claim Several Messengers Have Made**

The declaration that the speaker stands on a clear proof from his Lord is placed on the lips of several prophets in the Book. Hūd announced it twice to his people (11:28; 11:63), Shuʿayb said the same when he was accused of seeking his own advantage (11:88), and the Book describes the believers generally as those who stand on clear proof from their Lord (11:17). The statement is therefore not a personal boast on the part of one man but the standing of every messenger in relation to the message he was given.

**The Demand to Hasten What Cannot Be Hastened**

The verse then names the thing the Makkans had been pressing for: the punishment they were seeking to hasten. The Book records the styles of that demand in several places. They asked, if the message was true, for stones to rain down on them from the sky (8:32); they asked when the promise would come to pass if it was true (27:71); and the Book answers once that perhaps some of what they were seeking to hasten was close at hand (27:72). Elsewhere the Prophet is told that the wrongdoers have a share of torment like that of their predecessors and that they should not ask for it to be brought forward (51:59).

**Judgment Belongs to God Alone**

The sentence states that it is Allah alone who decides the time, and the Book states the same principle for judgment generally. The sūrah itself says a few verses later that all authority belongs to God and to Him people will be returned (28:88), and it says elsewhere that no one shares in His rulings (12:40). The Book makes the point repeatedly because the Makkans' demand assumed that the messenger or his God could be manipulated into a display on request.

The verse's effect is to state a limit on the messenger's function, and the sūrah will state that limit again in different terms when it asks the Prophet to say that he follows only what is revealed to him (6:50). The man who delivers the warning has no control over the timetable of the thing he is warning about.

**"The Best of Judges"**

The verse closes with a name that appears at the end of several passages. Shuʿayb told his people to be patient until God judges between them, calling Him the Best of Judges (7:87). The Prophet is told to follow what is revealed to him and to be patient until God passes judgment, with the same name attached (10:109). Noah used it when he appealed to his Lord about his son (11:45); and the eldest brother of Joseph used it when he refused to leave the land (12:80).

The Book ends the whole recitation with the same question: is Allah not the most just of all judges (95:8).

Putting the name at the end of a verse about the punishment the Makkans were demanding means the answer to their challenge is already settled in principle. A judge who is the best of judges does not need assistance or prompting from the parties before him, and the timing He has chosen is not something the accused can schedule.

---
## Verse 6:58

> Say ˹also˺, “If what you seek to hasten were within my power, the matter between us would have already been settled. But Allah knows the wrongdoers best.”

**What the Hypothetical Reveals About the Delay**

The sentence explains the delay as the product of a decision that has not yet been reached rather than as a failure of the threat. The Book makes the same point at length when it describes how the wrongdoers are drawn on step by step in ways they cannot understand, and how their end is postponed while the planning proceeds (7:182–183). The delay is described elsewhere as a period in which people are given time, and the Book warns against reading the postponement as approval (3:178).

For the Prophet's own audience, that meant the shouting matches of the Makkan market were still possible because they were being tolerated. The verse turns the delay into a fact about God's patience rather than a fact about the Prophet's weakness, which is the whole purpose of putting the statement in his mouth.

**The Verse Pair and the Difference Between Them**

The two verses stand together as a single answer. The first says that the punishment is not in the Prophet's power and that judgment belongs to God, who declares the truth and is the best of judges (6:57). The second says what would have happened if the first statement had been false. Together they form a demonstration that the audience had asked for, delivered in the form of a condition.

**"The Matter Between Us Would Have Been Settled"**

The phrase describes a dispute brought to its end by a ruling, and the sūrah has just described God as the one who decides. The Book uses similar language where it speaks of judgment being passed between people in fairness, with no one wronged (10:47), and where it says that God will judge between those who differ. The settlement in view here is final and total, and it includes the people who are asking the questions.

**"Allah Knows the Wrongdoers Best"**

The closing clause might look like a step down from the high statement of judgment that preceded it. It is not. The sentence states that the parties to the dispute are known, and that the one who will judge has the full record of every one of them. The Book uses the same construction where it says that God knows the wrongdoers best, and elsewhere when it says that He knows best who has strayed from His path and who is rightly guided (6:117).

Knowledge of that kind removes the last remaining hope that any party could argue its case on the day. The Makkans could accuse the Prophet in the market, and he could answer them, and the Book's response to the whole exchange is that the judge already knows what both sides have done.

---

## Verse 6:59

> With Him are the keys of the unseen—no one knows them except Him. And He knows what is in the land and sea. Not even a leaf falls without His knowledge, nor a grain in the darkness of the earth or anything—green or dry—but is ˹written˺ in a perfect Record

**The Only Place the Keys Are Named**

This is the only verse in the entire recitation that says God has the keys of the unseen, and the phrase gave its name to works of scholarship across the centuries, including the great commentary of Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī. The Book states elsewhere that God alone knows the unseen, as in the verse where He tells the Prophet to say that none in the heavens or the earth knows the unseen except God (27:65), and in the passage where He says that the unseen belongs to Him alone (10:20).

**The Five Keys in the Reported Tradition**

The commentaries connect the phrase with a report transmitted from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, in which the Prophet said that the keys of the unseen are five and then recited the verse in the sūrah of Luqmān: with Allah is the knowledge of the Hour, He sends down the rain, He knows what is in the wombs, no soul knows what it will earn tomorrow. No soul knows in what land it will die (31:34). The report is recorded by al-Bukhārī in his Ṣaḥīḥ in the book of the rain prayer, and it is reported from Buraydah in the Musnad of Aḥmad with a chain the scholars judged sound.

ʿĀisha used the same material to correct a claim about the Prophet himself, saying that anyone who told her that Muhammad had seen the unseen was a liar, because God says that none has knowledge of the unseen but He, and then quoting the Prophet's statement about the five keys. The point of the tradition is not to restrict God's knowledge to five items but to name the matters people most wanted disclosed and to place them beyond every created claimant.

**Knowledge That Reaches the Smallest Things**

The verse then widens its scope from the hidden to the visible: He knows what is in the land and sea. The statement moves from the general to the minute, and the two examples chosen are a falling leaf and a grain buried in the dark of the earth. Neither object has any importance to a human observer, and both are beyond observation altogether, which is precisely why the Book selects them.

The same argument appears in the sūrah of Yūnus, where the Book says that not even an atom's weight is hidden from the Lord on earth or in heaven, nor anything smaller or larger than that, but that it is written in a perfect record (10:61). The sūrah of Naml makes the same point about what is hidden in the heavens and the earth being recorded in a clear book (27:75), and the sūrah of Sabāʾ states that nothing escapes the Lord's knowledge by so much as an atom's weight (34:3).

**The Clear Record and What It Means**

The verse ends by saying that all of this is written in a perfect record. Commentators identified the record with the Preserved Tablet, of which the Book speaks when it describes the Qur'an as inscribed on a tablet well preserved (85:22), and some held that the expression refers to God's own knowledge rather than to a created object. The Book uses the same phrase for revealed scripture in several places, which shows how flexible the image is in its hands.

What the phrase accomplishes in this verse is to add permanence to knowledge.

---

## Verse 6:60
> He is the One Who calls back your souls by night and knows what you do by day, then revives you daily to complete your appointed term. To Him is your ˹ultimate˺ return, then He will inform you of what you used to do

**Sleep Described With the Language of Death**

The verb the verse uses for what happens at night is the same verb the Book uses for death, and the commentators noticed it. Yatawaffā is the word that appears when a person's life is taken at the end of his term, and here it describes the ordinary experience of falling asleep. In sleep, the senses and the limbs stop functioning, and what continues is something inward, which is why the Book can use one vocabulary for both states.

The Book makes the comparison explicit in the sūrah of al-Zumar, where it says that God calls back the souls of people at their death and the souls of the living during their sleep, keeping those for whom death has been decreed and releasing the others until an appointed time (39:42). The sūrah of al-Furqān describes the night as a covering, sleep as rest, and the day as a rising (25:47), which is the same three-part sequence this verse follows.

**Waking as a Daily Resurrection**

The same method appears with the revived earth. The Book describes God sending the winds and driving clouds to a dead land so that rain falls and fruit comes forth, and then says that in the same way He will bring the dead to life (7:57).

It repeats the image in the sūrah of the Bee (16:65), the Pilgrimage (22:5), the Spider (29:63), the Romans (30:24), and Fussilat (41:39), and the audience is told in one of those passages that even the idolaters admit who sends the rain.

**The Appointed Term**

Then the verse says that people are revived each day so that a term appointed may be fulfilled. The Book attaches an appointed term to individuals and communities alike, saying that every community has its term and cannot delay or advance it by a single hour (7:34; 10:49), and describing the sun and moon as running their courses for a set period (13:2; 31:29; 35:13; 39:5). The whole creation is described as having been made with a purpose and an appointed term (46:3).

**The Return and the Reporting**

To Him is your ultimate return, and then He will inform you of what you used to do. The Book uses this closing formula across many sūrahs, sometimes attached to the warning that the guilty will be told what they did, sometimes to the promise that those who did right will be shown their deeds. The sūrah of the Cave states it in almost the same words in the middle of a comparison between two groups of people, and the same ending appears in the sūrahs of Joseph, the Spider, and Luqmān.

The Book describes the record of each person being handed over on that day, with those who receive it in their right hand reading it with joy (17:71; 69:19), and the Book says that every deed, whether small or large, is written down for exactly this purpose.

---
## Verse 6:61

> He reigns supreme over all of His creation, and sends recording-angels, watching over you. When death comes to any of you, Our angels take their soul, never neglecting this duty

**A Statement the Sūrah Makes Twice**

The opening clause of this verse repeats almost word for word a sentence the sūrah used earlier, where God is described as reigning supreme over His creation and as being All-Wise and All-Aware (6:18). The repetition is not accidental, since the two statements sit inside the same argument, and the second adds the machinery that makes the first concrete: not only does God hold authority over what He has made, but there are appointed agents watching what every human being does.

**The Angels Who Write**

Then the verse describes the recorders: vigilant, honourable angels who note everything and know what people do (82:10–12). The sūrah of Qāf describes two of them sitting on the right and the left and states that no word is uttered without an observer ready to write it down (50:17–18). The Book refers to the same system when it says that every soul has successive angels before and behind it, protecting it by God's command (13:11).

**The Moment of Death**

When death comes to any of you, Our angels take their soul, never neglecting this duty. The verse moves from the ongoing record to the single moment in which the record is closed, and the phrasing about the angels never neglecting their task is a reassurance to the friends of God and a warning to everyone else. No one dies unattended, and no one dies at a moment other than the one that was fixed.

The Book describes the taking of the souls of the wrongdoers in harsher terms elsewhere. When the angels seize the souls of those who wronged themselves, they ask them what they were doing, and the reply from the people who claim they were oppressed in the land is met with the question of whether God's earth was not wide enough to move within (4:97). Another passage describes the angels striking the faces and backs of those who disbelieve (47:27), and the sūrah itself will describe the same scene with the angels stretching out their hands (6:93).

**A Reassurance That Cuts Both Ways**

The sentence about the angels fulfilling their duty is addressed to a community in which the manner of a person's death carried heavy social meaning. A death far from home, or an early death, or a violent death, was read as a judgment on the person and his tribe. The verse removes that reading by stating that the taking of the soul is performed by appointed agents who never fall short, which means the circumstances of a death are not a verdict on the one who dies.

That is the mercy in the sentence. The same clause also establishes the point the Book repeats in several places: the moment cannot be moved. The sūrah had said earlier that every community has its appointed term and cannot delay or advance it (7:34; 10:49), and here the application is made to a single person. Neither the physicians of the city nor the idols at the shrine have any part in the timing.

---

## Verse 6:62

> Then they are ˹all˺ returned to Allah—their True Master. Judgment is His ˹alone˺. And He is the Swiftest Reckoner

The verse describes the return of every soul to God and calls Him their true Master, and the word translated as Master is one the Book uses for the person who owns, protects, and manages the affairs of another.

The Book uses the same word in a passage about intercession, where it says that the believers have God as their Patron while the disbelievers have no patron at all (47:11), and the sūrah states plainly that apart from Him no one has a protector and no one has an intercessor (6:51).

**A Question Asked on That Day**

The Book describes a scene in which the ownership of everything is publicly announced. On the day when all appear before God, nothing about them will be hidden from Him, and the question will be asked: who does all authority belong to this day? The answer given is to Allah, the One, the Supreme (40:16). The Book says elsewhere that true authority on that day will belong only to the Most Compassionate, and that it will be a hard day for the disbelievers (25:26).

**Judgment Is His Alone**

The sūrah has already said once that the decision belongs to God, when the Prophet was told to say that he had no power over the timing of the punishment and that God is the best of judges (6:57). The same claim returns here as the conclusion of the passage on knowledge and death. What began with God knowing every leaf and every grain ends with God alone passing sentence on what every soul has done.

The Book states that God will pass judgment between people on that day in fairness, without the least injustice, and it says that every soul will then be rewarded for what it did, with no wrong done at all (40:17). The two claims belong together: the judge who knows everything has no need of witnesses, and the judge who is the best of judges has no need of advocates.

**The Chain That Ends Here**

Follow the verses as an unit. God holds the keys of the unseen and knows every falling leaf and buried grain (6:59). God takes the souls of people by night and knows what they do by day, reviving them each morning until the appointed term is complete (6:60). God has appointed angels who record everything and angels who take the soul at death without fail (6:61). Then the souls are returned to their Master, who alone judges and who settles accounts without delay (6:62).

The sūrah of the Cow uses it when it says that some people receive their reward and that God is swift in reckoning (2:202), and the sūrah of Ghāfir uses it in the announcement that every soul will be repaid in full (40:17).

The Book records the idolaters themselves conceding that their gods are names they gave (12:40), and it reports God's challenge for them to show anything their associate-gods had created (35:40).

---

## Verse 6:63

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Who rescues you from the darkest times on land and at sea? He ˹alone˺ you call upon with humility, openly and secretly: “If You rescue us from this, we will be ever grateful.”

**The Prayer of the Tight Corner**

The verse asks the Makkans about a moment every one of them knew: the point at which a situation has become hopeless and the only remaining act is to call out. The Book returns to this scene repeatedly because it is the one place where a human being's actual beliefs become visible. The idolaters could argue in the market about their gods, and in the dark they abandoned them without discussion.

The Book describes the same behaviour elsewhere, sometimes with the vivid detail that a person in danger calls on God with sincere devotion but then returns to his old associations once he is safe (29:65). Sometimes with the observation that when people are asked who saves them, they answer that it was God (31:32). The verse in front of us turns that knowledge into a question and lets the audience supply the answer the Book already knows they will give.

**Land and Sea**

The pairing of land and sea covers the two great fears of a trading city, and the merchants of Makkah knew both. A caravan crossing the desert faced thirst, sandstorms, and raiding; a ship faced the possibility that the water would simply close over the crew, with no grave and no witness. The Book uses the sea in several places as an image of helplessness, describing the drowning of the people of Noah and the rescue of Jonah. It describes people in a ship as calling upon God with sincere faith until He brings them safely to land, at which point they begin to associate others with Him (17:67).

**Calling With Humility, Openly and Secretly**

The two adverbs reveal how thin the Makkan position was. A person who prays in secret has admitted where the power lies, and he cannot then claim that his public position represents his belief. The Book recorded this inconsistency long before: those who call upon God in fear and hope, and those who cry out when harm befalls them, and then attribute a share of what they have been given to others (16:53; 39:8).

**Why the Question Is Put to the Prophet to Ask**

The command begins with Say, which places the question in the Prophet's mouth rather than in God's. The effect is that the audience is being answered by the man they were disputing with, using material they cannot deny. The sūrah uses this device repeatedly, as it did with the question about hearing and sight (6:46) and the question about being overwhelmed by the punishment (6:47).

**The Same Scene in the Sūrah of Yūnus**

The Book tells this story elsewhere with details that make the Makkan setting unmistakable. In the sūrah of Yūnus, people are described boarding a ship that sails with a favourable wind while the passengers rejoice, and then the wind turns, the waves come from every side, and the travellers are certain they are about to die. At that point they cry out to Allah with sincere devotion and promise that if they are saved they will certainly be grateful (10:22). The wording of the prayer in that account is virtually the same as the wording preserved in this verse, which is a sign of how closely the two passages are related in the Book's argument.

The sūrah of the Ant carries the same question in the form of a challenge: who responds to the distressed when they cry to Him and relieves their affliction, and who makes people successors in the earth? Is it another god besides Allah, asks the verse, before noting that people are hardly mindful (27:62). The Book's audience is asked to answer its own question in three separate sūrahs, and the answer is never disputed.

The sūrah of Yūnus describes the pattern exactly, saying that when God rescues people they immediately transgress in the land unjustly, and then addresses them with the reminder that their transgression is against their own souls (10:23).

---

## Verse 6:64

> Say, “˹Only˺ Allah rescues you from this and any other distress, yet you associate others with Him ˹in worship˺.”

**The Answer Stated as a Verdict**

The previous verse asked who rescues from the dangers of land and sea, and this one answers without waiting for the audience's reply. The answer is Allah alone, and the wording makes the exclusion total: no partner, no intercessor, no shrine receives credit for the rescue.

The sūrah of Yūnus contains a similar question about who provides from heaven and earth, who owns hearing and sight, and who conducts every affair, and it records that the answer people give is Allah, followed immediately by the question of whether they will not then be mindful (10:31).

**The Meaning of Associating Others With Him**

The charge in this verse is that they associate others with Him in worship, and the sūrah has been dismantling that practice from every angle. The associated beings were created things like the worshippers (7:194), they could neither harm nor benefit anyone (5:76), they could not respond or protect (20:89), and they had been given names by their own ancestors with no authority from God (12:40).

What this verse adds is the specific context of gratitude. The Book treats the blessing and the rescue as coming through channels that would be completely the work of God even if the idols were real, and it treats the assignment of a share of thanks to a created thing as a falsification of the record. The person who thanks a stone for a rescue he received from God has made a mistake about what happened to him.

**The Book's Pattern of Testing Its Audience With Their Own Words**

The Book's practice of using the audience's confessions is a feature of its method, not an accident of this passage. It asks who created the heavens and the earth. The answer recorded is Allah (29:61); it asks who sends down rain to revive the dead earth, and the answer is Allah (29:63); and in each case the question is followed by a rebuke that turns on the gap between the admission and the practice.

That method leaves no exit. A person who answers Allah to these questions has already accepted that the beings he invokes alongside God have no share in the matters he is asking about, and the verse in front of us closes the discussion by naming the inconsistency directly rather than by arguing about the idols' nature.

**What Gratitude Would Have Required**

The verse's charge also states what the correct response would have been. The rescue and every other deliverance come from one source, and the acknowledgment belongs with it. The Book describes gratitude as a form of recognition that produces conduct, and it pairs the ungrateful with those who reject the signs, as it does in the sūrah of Luqmān, where the Book says that none rejects the signs except the treacherous and ungrateful (31:32).

The Book states the rebuke in a compact form in the sūrah of the Bee, where it says that every blessing people have comes from God and that whenever hardship touches them it is to Him alone that they cry for help. Then asks why they turn away from the One who gave them what they have (16:53–54). The movement from gratitude to association is described there and here in the same order, which shows that the charge is a general diagnosis of human conduct rather than a complaint about one city.

---

## Verse 6:65

> Say, “He ˹alone˺ has the power to unleash upon you a torment from above or below you or split you into ˹conflicting˺ factions and make you taste the violence of one another.” See how We vary the signs, so perhaps they will comprehend

**Three Ways for a Community to Be Punished**

The verse lists three possibilities, and the order matters. The first two are punishments that come from outside: a torment from above, and one from below. The third is not an external punishment at all but a condition the community brings upon itself, in which it splits into factions and each group makes the others taste violence. The Book places the self-inflicted one last, which is where the emphasis falls.

Commentators interpreted the torment from above as stones or a storm, or as domination by a tyrant, and the torment from below as an earthquake, a sinking, or being swallowed by the earth. The Book's catalogue of destroyed peoples fits both categories: some were struck by a storm of stones, some were overtaken by a blast, some were made to sink into the earth, and some were drowned (29:40). The sūrah of Sabāʾ states the same power in a conditional: if God willed, He could cause the earth to swallow the people or cause deadly pieces of the sky to fall on them (34:9).

**The Third Punishment and Why It Is the Worst**

The Book warns about this kind of trial elsewhere in a form that applies it to communities generally. It says that people should guard against a trial which will not strike only the wrongdoers among them (8:25), which is the Book's way of saying that disorder does not restrict itself to the guilty. The sūrah of al-Anʿām has already stated the same warning in the story of the earlier peoples it describes as having been split into groups. It will return to the subject of divisions among the believers later, when it tells the Prophet that he bears no responsibility for those who split their faith into sects (6:159).

**"See How We Vary the Signs"**

The verse closes with the same formula that appeared earlier in the sūrah, where the Prophet was asked to see how the signs were varied while the people still turned away (6:46), and where the explanation of the signs was said to make the way of the wicked distinct (6:55). The recurrence is deliberate. The sūrah keeps a running account of the methods being used on its audience, and this verse places the warning about punishment among the variations.

The different reactions attached to the same formula mark the progression across the sūrah. At the earlier point the complaint was that the audience turned away; here the purpose clause is that they might understand. The Book's expectation is stated as a hope, and the hope is what keeps the warning from being merely a sentence. To understand in this context describes the act of grasping something with the mind and turning it over, and the Book uses it for the kind of understanding that changes behaviour. A person who understands the argument of this verse has understood that the power which rescued him at sea (6:63) is the same power that can reach him on land, and that the quarrel he is pursuing may itself be the form the punishment takes.

---

## Verse 6:66

> Still your people ˹O Prophet˺ have rejected this ˹Quran˺, although it is the truth. Say, “I am not a keeper over you.”

**A Statement That Acknowledges the Grief**

The verse does not open with an argument or a command. It states a fact about the Prophet's situation, and it states it in a way that acknowledges how heavy the fact was: your people have rejected this, though it is the truth. The Book speaks to its messenger here in the manner of one who knows what has been happening, and the sentence does not pretend that the rejection is small.

The sūrah has already recorded the accusations, the obstruction, the demands for signs, and the mockery, and the Prophet has been told that the people who did these things are not questioning his honesty but denying God's signs (6:33). This verse adds the element of personal grief, and the Book elsewhere shows how deep that grief went when it tells him that he might destroy himself with sorrow over people who would not believe (18:6; 26:3).

**"Although It Is the Truth"**

The clause about the truth is the pivot of the sentence. The rejection is not a matter of an unresolved dispute in which one party may turn out to be right; the Book has settled the question of what the Qur'an is, and the rejection is being recorded against that settled fact. The Book uses this construction repeatedly, placing the truth of the message next to the rejection of it as a way of naming the tragedy rather than merely the disagreement.

That pattern is worth seeing in full. The Book states the same combination in the sūrah of Yūnus, where the Prophet is told to say that the truth has surely come from his Lord and that whoever chooses guidance or straying does so for his own account. Then to say that he is not a keeper over the people (10:108). The sūrah of the Troops makes the identical statement when it says that the Book has been revealed with the truth for humanity and that the messenger is not a keeper over them (39:41). The claim is that the rejection happens in the presence of the truth rather than in its absence.

**The Office of Keeper and What It Excludes**

The Prophet is instructed to say that he is not a keeper over them, and the word used here appears twice more in this same sūrah. Earlier he is told to follow what is revealed to him and to turn away from the idolaters, in a passage that ends with the same denial of keepership (6:106–107). Earlier still he is told that his insight has come from his Lord and that he is not a keeper over the people (6:104). The repetition marks it as a settled feature of his commission.

A keeper in this sense is the person charged with the management and outcome of another's affairs. The Book denies that role to the Prophet in matters of belief, and it does so at the very points where the temptation to take that role would have been strongest. What is left to him is the delivery of the message and the reminder, which the Book describes in the sūrah of the Overwhelming when it says that his duty is only to remind (88:21).

---

## Verse 6:67

> Every ˹destined˺ matter has a ˹set˺ time to transpire. And you will soon come to know

**A Sentence About Appointments**

The verse states that everything destined has a time at which it arrives, and the word used for the appointment comes from the same root as the terms and spans the Book applies to human lives and communities. The sūrah used it a few verses earlier when it said that people are revived each day so that an appointed term may be completed (6:60), and it used it for the communities whose fixed spans cannot be delayed or advanced (7:34; 10:49). Here the same structure is applied to the events the audience was impatient about.

The Book states the principle in almost this exact wording at the end of a passage about the demands for signs, where it says that no messenger could bring a sign except by God's permission and that every destined matter has a set time (13:38). Read together, the two verses answer the Makkan demand for a miracle and the Makkan demand for the punishment in the same currency: the timing of both rests with God.

**The Threat and the Promise in the Same Clause**

And you will soon come to know is a formula the Book uses in several tones. It appears where it warns people who reject the revelation that they will soon know (37:170), where the Prophet is told to bear with the mockers and answer them with peace because they will soon come to know (43:89). Where the Book tells the audience that they will certainly know the truth of the warning before long (38:88). The same words serve as a warning and as an assurance depending on who is hearing them.

**The Impatience of the Audience**

The sūrah has recorded the demand to hasten the punishment several times, and the Book treats that impatience as a feature of the human being rather than a considered position. It says in the sūrah of the Prophets that humanity is made of haste, and follows the observation with a warning that the signs will be shown and should not be demanded ahead of their time (21:37). The sūrah of Yūnus ends a discussion of the delay with the instruction to keep waiting, since the Prophet is also waiting (10:102).

**The Transition It Creates**

The two verses just completed move the sūrah from public argument to practical instruction. The Prophet has been told that he is not a keeper over his people (6:66), and the appointment of every matter has been stated (6:67). What follows is a set of directions about how the believers are to conduct themselves when the revelation is mocked, and how far their responsibility extends toward the mockers.

The Book keeps a copy of the same appointment in its account of the Hour, where the Prophet is told to say that knowledge of the time belongs to his Lord alone and that it will take people by surprise (7:187).

The sūrah of Ṭā-Hā ends with the Prophet being told to say that each party is waiting and that the outcome will soon be known (20:135), and the context of that verse is a community being asked to continue in the conduct the message requires.

---

## Verse 6:68

> And when you come across those who ridicule Our revelations, do not sit with them unless they engage in a different topic. Should Satan make you forget, then once you remember, do not ˹continue to˺ sit with the wrongdoing people

The verse gives a rule about physical presence, which is the kind of instruction the Book issues when a principle needs a form a community can actually follow.

The rule is stated in the second person singular. Its addressee is the Prophet, but the sūrah of the Women makes the same statement to the whole community in the same words, saying that when they hear God's revelations being denied or ridiculed they should not sit in that company unless the conversation changes, or else they will be like those who mock (4:140).

**The Reason for the Rule**

The reason given in the sūrah of the Women is that sitting with the mockers makes the listener like them, and that reasoning explains the shape of the rule in this verse. Presence at a gathering is a form of participation in it, and the Book does not treat a person's silence as neutrality when the revelation of God is being treated as a joke.

That principle is worth stating in full because it is the hinge of the passage. The Book is not asking the believers to avoid people who disagree with them, since the same Book tells the Prophet to argue gracefully with the People of the Book (29:46), and it tells him that the message has been sent as a mercy to all people. What is forbidden is attendance at an assembly whose purpose is to degrade the revelation.

**Where the Believers Were Not to Sit**

The phrase about the wrongdoing people at the end of the verse connects the instruction to the sūrah's larger argument. The sūrah has described the wrongdoers across the previous sixty verses: those who turn others away from the message (6:26), who deny the signs (6:49), who respond to God's rescue by associating partners with Him (6:64). Who were warned about the punishments that could reach them from above, from below, and through their own divisions (6:65).

The command to leave that gathering is therefore a command to leave the company of a group the Book has just finished describing, and the leaving has a doctrinal weight.

**What Kind of Discussion the Verse Is About**

Commentators drew a line between the discussion that this verse forbids and the deliberation that the Book encourages. The Arabic verb used for the mockers' activity describes argument that is disparaging and disrespectful rather than inquiry, and al-Rāzī rejected the reading that would ban religious discussion in general, since the Book elsewhere commands people to reflect and to reason about the signs. The distinction is between a conversation that treats the revelation as a target and one that treats it as a question.

The Book's own usage supports that reading. The believers are described among the successful as those who avoid idle talk (23:3). The sūrah of the Cattle states that God has made enemies for every prophet from among the devilish humans and jinn, whispering elegant words of deception, and then tells the Prophet to leave them and their deceit (6:112). What is being excluded is a specific kind of speech, not the exchange of views.

The Book uses the same device in the story of Moses and his companion, who explains that only Satan made him forget the fish (18:63).

---

## Verse 6:69

> Those mindful ˹of Allah˺ will not be accountable for those ˹who ridicule it˺ whatsoever—their duty is to advise, so perhaps the ridiculers will abstain

**A Question the Previous Verse Raises**

Having been told to leave the company of the mockers, a believer might reasonably ask what he owed them afterward. The verse answers that the mindful are not accountable for the mockers in any respect, and the answer is paired with a duty that remains: to advise them. The sentence separates two things that people tend to confuse, which are responsibility for another person's conduct and responsibility for what one says to him.

That separation is stated as a general principle elsewhere in the Book, where it says that no soul burdened with sin will carry the burden of another (6:164), and where the sūrah of the Troops says that the messenger is not a keeper over the people. The verse in front of us applies the principle to the specific case of a man who has walked out of a gathering and may be worrying about what becomes of the people he left behind.

**What the Duty of Advice Includes**

The word for advising describes a reminder delivered with the aim of correcting conduct, and it does not require a sermon. The Book uses the same idea when it tells the Prophet that his only duty is to remind (88:21), and when it describes the believers as those who call others to good and forbid what is wrong. The duty in this verse is narrower than a general obligation to reform the society; it concerns the mockers the believer has just left.

**"So Perhaps They Will Abstain"**

The clause also explains why advice is worth giving to people who are mocking. The person mocking the revelation may stop, and the Book's approach to the hardened is to keep the possibility open, which is why it tells the Prophet to bear with the mockers and answer them with peace (43:89). The stance the Book recommends is neither contempt nor cooperation but a persistent reminder delivered without hostility.

**The Duty Stated Elsewhere in the Book**

The Book describes the messenger's obligation in the same terms in several places, and each statement narrows the task to the delivery of the message. The Prophet is told that if people turn away after the invitation, his only duty is to deliver (3:20). He is told that whether he sees the punishment or dies before it, his duty is to deliver and the judgment is God's (13:40); and the sūrah of the Spider states that the messenger's only obligation is clear delivery (29:18). The sūrah of the Women puts the same thought in the form of a promise, saying that whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed God, and that whoever turns away has not been placed in the Prophet's keeping (4:80).

Read with the verse in front of us, those statements explain why the believers' duty toward the mockers is limited to advice. The task of the messenger and of the community that carries his message is to make the truth clear, and the Book repeats the boundary so often because the temptation to go beyond it is permanent.

---

## Verse 6:70

> And leave those who take this faith ˹of Islam˺ as mere play and amusement and are deluded by ˹their˺ worldly life. Yet remind them by this ˹Quran˺, so no one should be ruined for their misdeeds. They will have no protector or intercessor other than Allah. Even if they were to offer every ˹possible˺ ransom, none will be accepted from them. Those are the ones who will be ruined for their misdeeds. They will have a boiling drink and painful punishment for their disbelief

The verse begins by telling the Prophet to leave a group and ends by telling him to remind them, and the combination states the Book's position on people who treat religion as entertainment.

The sūrah of the Cave tells the Prophet to say that the message is the truth from his Lord and that whoever wills may believe and whoever wills may disbelieve (18:29). The sūrah of the Troops ends a similar discussion by describing the messenger as a warner who is not a keeper.

**"Play and Amusement" as a Description of Conduct**

The phrase describes people whose relation to the message is that of an audience watching a performance. The Book uses the same language to describe the worldly life itself, saying that it is no more than play, amusement, luxury, mutual boasting. Competition in wealth and children (57:20), and it says that those who took the faith as amusement and play and were deluded by the life of this world will be told on the day of judgment that they are ignored just as they ignored the meeting of that day (7:51).

The two images belong together. A person who treats the revelation as entertainment has already accepted the priority the Book assigns to the worldly life, and the Book's description of that life as play is not contempt for the world but a statement about its duration. The same pair appears in the sūrah of the Kneeling, where people are told that they made a mockery of God's revelations and were deluded by the worldly life (45:35).

**"Deluded by Their Worldly Life"**

The sūrah has been dismantling that estimate since the passage about the poor believers. The Book told the Prophet not to dismiss the humble worshippers whose devotion the notables regarded as an embarrassment (6:52), and it said that the differences between people are a test by which God distinguishes the grateful (6:53). The people in this verse are the ones who failed that test, and their amusement is the form the failure took.

**No Protector, No Intercessor, No Ransom**

The verse then describes what the reminder is meant to prevent. They will have no protector or intercessor other than Allah, which is the same statement the sūrah made in connection with the warning given to those who fear the gathering (6:51), and even if they offered every possible ransom it would not be accepted. The Book repeats that image where it says that a disbeliever possessing everything in the world twice over could not ransom himself from the punishment of the day of judgment (5:36), and where it describes the wrongdoers offering what they have in the world in exchange for escape (39:47).

The sūrah of the Cow states the same three denials in a single verse about the day when no soul will help another and no intercession will be accepted and no ransom taken (2:48). The Makkans' religious system rested on the assumption that their patrons and their gods would negotiate on their behalf, and the Book closes that door in every sūrah where it describes the reckoning.

---

## Verse 6:71

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Should we invoke, other than Allah, those ˹idols˺ which cannot benefit or harm us, and turn back to disbelief after Allah has guided us? ˹If we do so, we will be˺ like those disoriented by devils in the wilderness, while their companions call them to guidance, ˹saying˺, ‘Come to us!’ Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah’s guidance is the ˹only˺ true guidance. And we are commanded to submit to the Lord of all worlds

**A Question Put in the First Person**

The sūrah has been answering the Makkans for seventy verses, and here the Prophet is told to put the argument in his own mouth as a question. Should we invoke, apart from God, something that can neither benefit nor harm us? The construction makes the proposal state itself, since the moment the objects of worship are described in those two words, the absurdity is on the surface and no further argument is needed.

The Book uses the same description repeatedly where it deals with the idols. The sūrah of the Table states that those whom the pagans worship besides God can neither harm nor benefit anyone (5:76). The sūrah of Yūnus describes people who worship beings of that kind and call them their intercessors with God (10:18). The sūrah of the Bee asks whether anyone would take besides God lords who cannot even benefit or protect themselves (13:16); and the sūrah of the Prophets records Abraham putting the same question to his people (21:66). The description is not a debating point invented for this verse but the Book's settled account of what an idol is.

**Turning Back on the Heels**

The second clause of the question is about the cost of the choice: should we turn back to disbelief after God has guided us? The Book states the danger of that reversal in the strongest terms, saying that whoever among the believers renounces his faith and dies a disbeliever has rendered his deeds void in this life and the next (2:217). Telling the community that if they yield to the disbelievers they will be dragged back to disbelief and become losers (3:149).

**The Man in the Wilderness**

The verse then supplies a simile: like one bewildered, seduced by satans on the earth, though he has companions who call him to guidance, saying, Come to us! The picture is of a man who has lost his way in open country, is being pulled in the wrong direction by whispering companions, and is being called back by others. The Book's word for the seducers is plural and general, and the commentators noted that it can cover both the human and the unseen agents who do Satan's work, since the Book speaks elsewhere of devilish humans and jinn who whisper to one another with elegant words of deception (6:112).

**Allah's Guidance Is the Only Guidance**

The Prophet's answer to the imaginary proposal is short: Allah's guidance is the only true guidance. The statement appears in the same wording in the sūrah of the Cow, where the Prophet is told to say it after being pressed to follow the faith of the People of the Book (2:120). In the sūrah of the Family of Imran, where the same claim is made against a claim of exclusive ownership of guidance (3:73). The sūrah of Fāṭir puts the same idea in a form that leaves no doubt: what God opens of mercy cannot be withheld, and what He withholds cannot be released (35:2).

The Book places the same declaration on the lips of the Prophet in the sūrah of Ghāfir, where he announces that he has been forbidden to worship what the people worship besides God, since clear proofs have come to him. That he has been commanded to submit to the Lord of all worlds (40:66).

---

## Verse 6:72

> establish prayer, and be mindful of Him. To Him you will all be gathered together

**Why Prayer Comes First**

The Book treats the prayer as the marker of a community's identity and the ordinary channel of its connection with God. The sūrah of Ṭā-Hā gives the command in the first person on God's behalf, telling Moses that He is God and that there is no deity but Him, and then instructing him to worship and to establish the prayer for the remembrance of his Lord (20:14). The sūrah of the Spider states that the prayer restrains people from indecency and wrongdoing (29:45), and the sūrah of the Cow tells the believers to seek help through patience and prayer (2:45).

Those verses show what the prayer was understood to do. It is not presented as an obligation discharged for God's benefit, since the Book states that God has no need of any of His creatures. It is presented as a discipline that builds the person who performs it, which is why the sūrah of the Spider attaches moral effects to it and why this verse attaches it to submission.

**Mindfulness as the Ground of Conduct**

The second item is to be mindful of Him, and the Book's word for mindfulness is one of its central concepts. It describes a person who orders his conduct around the awareness that God is present, sees what he does, and will hold him to account. The sūrah of the Cow opens by describing the Book as guidance for those who are mindful (2:2), and the Book commands mindfulness repeatedly in connection with specific acts rather than as a general sentiment.

Al-Rāzī arranged the three items of this passage into a scheme that shows how the Book's religion fits together: submission is the best of the acts of the heart, prayer is the best of the acts of the limbs. Mindfulness is the best of the acts of restraint, since it is the quality that keeps a person from what he should avoid.

**The Gathering at the End**

The sentence closes with the statement that it is unto Him that you shall be gathered, which returns to the theme the sūrah has been developing since its account of the keys of the unseen. The Book states the gathering in dozens of places, and the sūrah has already described the return of every soul to its true Master (6:62). Here the statement comes at the end of a declaration of worship, which gives it a different weight: the person who prays and lives mindfully is not performing an isolated act but travelling toward an assembly.

**The Book's Instructions About the Prayer and Mindfulness**

The Book repeats the two obligations of this verse in forms that show what it expected of the community. The believers are told to observe the prayers, especially the middle prayer. To stand in true devotion to God (2:238); they are described among the successful as those who humble themselves in their prayer (23:2); and they are commanded to be mindful of God in the way He deserves and not to die except in a state of submission to Him (3:102). The sūrah of the Mutual Disillusion adds the concession that the obligation is measured to a person's capacity, telling the believers to be mindful of God to the best of their ability (64:16).

---

## Verse 6:73

> He is the One Who created the heavens and the earth in truth. On the Day ˹of Judgment˺ He will say, ‘Be!’ And there will be! His command is truth. All authority is His ˹alone˺ on the Day the Trumpet will be blown. He is the Knower of all—seen or unseen. And He is the All-Wise, All-Aware.”

**Creation in Truth Rather Than in Play**

The verse opens by describing God as the One who created the heavens and the earth in truth, and the phrase answers a question the Makkans had never posed but which the sūrah has been building toward. The Book states that the creation was not made as play. It repeats the point in several places: the sūrah of the Prophets asks whether God took the heavens and the earth and everything in them as a pastime. It answers that He did not; the sūrah of Ṣād denies that the creation of the heavens and the earth was in vain. And the sūrah of the Family of Imran describes the people of understanding as those who reflect on the creation and conclude that it was not made without purpose (3:191; 21:16; 38:27).

The same claim appears in the sūrah of the Smoke, where God states that He did not create the heavens and the earth and what lies between them as play (44:38).

**The Command That Brings Things Into Being**

The verse then names the mechanism of creation: on the day He says, Be! And it is. The Book uses this formula repeatedly for the divine command, and it attaches it to the original creation of the heavens and the earth (2:117), to the conception of Jesus (3:47), to the creation of Adam (3:59), and to resurrection (36:82; 40:68). The phrase's function is to deny any gap between God's intention and its execution.

**The Knowledge That Covers Both Sides of Reality**

The verse closes by naming God as the Knower of all, seen or unseen, and the pairing appears across the Book as a summary of His knowledge. The sūrah of the Thunder calls Him the Knower of the seen and the unseen, the All-Great, the Most Exalted (13:9). The sūrah of the Prostration gives the name at the head of a passage about His mercy (32:6); the sūrah of the Troops places it in a prayer for judgment (39:46); and the sūrah of the Gathering and the sūrah of Mutual Disillusion repeat the same pair (59:22; 64:18).

The pairing matters here because the sūrah has spent a long passage on things that human beings cannot see. The keys of the unseen belong to God (6:59), the leaf and the grain are recorded (6:59), the soul is taken at night (6:60), and the record is kept by angels (6:61). The verse now states the principle that covers all of those cases: nothing is outside the knowledge of the One who is described in the same sentence as the maker and the sovereign.

The Book mentions the trumpet in many places as the herald of the resurrection, and it describes the sequence: the trumpet is blown and everything in the heavens and the earth falls dead, then it is blown again and they rise up looking on (39:68).

The sūrah of Ṭā-Hā states that on that day God will gather the wicked, blue-faced from horror (20:102); the sūrah of Yā-Sīn describes people rushing from their graves to their Lord (36:51); and the sūrah of the Troops gives the same scene in the same terms as this verse.

---

## Verse 6:74

> And ˹remember˺ when Abraham said to his father, Ȃzar, “Do you take idols as gods? It is clear to me that you and your people are entirely misguided.”

**The Beginning of a Narrative Section**

After seventy verses of argument, the sūrah turns to a story, and the story is the one the Book treats as the model of the case it has been making. Abraham is introduced in the middle of a declaration about the One God, and what follows is the account of a man who arrived at that declaration through the created world. The sūrah of Maryam calls him a man of truth and a prophet (19:41), and the Book elsewhere credits him with sound judgement given to him early (21:51).

The placement matters. The sūrah has just stated that God created the heavens and the earth in truth, that His command brings things into being, that authority belongs to Him alone, and that His knowledge covers the seen and the unseen (6:73). Abraham's story is the demonstration of what those statements mean for a human being who looks at the world honestly, and the sūrah will end the section by calling his argument the one God gave him against his people (6:83).

**The Tension Between Kinship and Conviction**

The verse introduces a theme that returns at several points in the Abraham story and that the community reading the sūrah would have felt immediately. The Book elsewhere praises those who honour their parents and forbids the believer to obey them if they press him toward idolatry, while requiring that he keep their company courteously in this world (31:14–15). Abraham's case shows how far the requirement of courtesy could be stretched and where it ended.

The Book records the end of that story in the sūrah of Repentance, where it says that Abraham's prayer for his father's forgiveness was only in fulfilment of a promise he had made. That when it became clear to him that his father was an enemy of God he broke ties with him, while noting that Abraham was tender-hearted and forbearing (9:114). The Book's sympathy for the difficulty of that decision is visible in the wording, and the sūrah of Maryam preserves the words Abraham used when he left: peace be upon you, I will pray to my Lord for your forgiveness (19:47).

The same sūrah records how he spoke to the man. He called him dear father and asked him why he worshipped what could neither hear nor see nor benefit him at all (19:42); he told him that he had received knowledge his father had not received. Asked him to follow so that he could guide him to a straight path (19:43). He warned him against worshipping Satan, who is ever rebellious against the Most Compassionate (19:44); and he said that he feared his father would be touched by a punishment and become a companion of Satan in the Fire (19:45). Every sentence is an address of respect, and every sentence states the danger without softening it.

**What the Verse Assumes About the Audience**

The Makkans were the descendants of Abraham and knew it. The Book had already told them that Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian but a man of pure faith, a Muslim, and not one of those who associate partners with God (3:67), and it uses his story here because the claim of descent gave the argument its edge. The ancestor whose name they invoked had begun his mission by rejecting idols of the same kind.

In the Biblical account Abraham's father is Terah, and some held that Āzar was another name for the same man. Others suggested that Āzar was the name of an idol and that Abraham was addressing his father by it as a rebuke. And a third group held that Āzar was an uncle who was called a father in the wider sense in which the Book uses the word for older relatives, citing the verse where Jacob names Ishmael among his fathers though Ishmael was his uncle (2:133).

---

## Verse 6:75

> We also showed Abraham the wonders of the heavens and the earth, so he would be sure in faith

**The Showing Attributed to God**

The verse states that God showed Abraham the wonders of the heavens and the earth, and the attribution is the point of the sentence. The case for the oneness of God can be made from observation, and the sūrah has been making it that way, but this verse says that Abraham's insight was given rather than merely achieved. The Book's invitation to reflect on the created order is addressed to everyone, and it states that signs are of no benefit to people who refuse to believe (10:101), so the difference between Abraham and his people was not access to the world but the use made of it.

The Book places the same kind of statement at the end of the sūrah of Fussilat, where God says that He will show people His signs in the universe and within themselves until it becomes clear to them that the message is the truth (41:53). The sūrah of the Scatterers states the same in two verses that begin with the signs on the earth for those with sure faith and end with the signs within the readers themselves (51:20–21). What Abraham received in this verse is what the Book offers to every reader of it.

**What the Wonders Were**

Commentators differed on what exactly Abraham was shown.
