One reading takes the phrase at face value: the wonders of the heavens and the earth are the phenomena everyone can see, the sun, the moon, the stars, the turning of the day and night, and the ordering of the world. On that reading the verse describes Abraham applying his reason to what was in front of him, and the Book credits the sound judgement he had been given early for that use of it (21:51).

**Certainty as the Purpose**

The verse gives the purpose of the showing: so that Abraham would be sure in faith. The Book's word for that certainty describes knowledge that has passed beyond doubt, and it is used for the assurance the believers seek about the meeting with their Lord and about the reality of the accounting. The same word appears in the sūrah of the Cow, where Abraham asks to be shown how God gives life to the dead and explains that he already believes but wants his heart to be reassured (2:260).

**The Argument and the Certainty**

The sūrah closes the Abraham section by naming the discourse with his people as the argument God gave him against them, and by stating that God elevates in rank whoever He pleases (6:83). Read with this verse, the two statements describe the same gift from two angles. The certainty given to Abraham in this verse is what produced the argument in the following verses, and the argument is credited to God rather than to Abraham's ingenuity.

---

## Verse 6:76

> When the night grew dark upon him, he saw a star and said, “This is my Lord!” But when it set, he said, “I do not love things that set.”

**The Setting of the Sky**

The verse opens with the night darkening over Abraham, and the scene is the one his people knew best. The Makkans travelled by the stars, divided their calendar by the moon, and associated the heavenly bodies with the deities they served, and the region's older religions had made the sun, the moon, and the stars objects of worship. The sūrah's audience would have recognised the setting immediately, and the argument that follows is built from the objects they considered sacred.

The Book elsewhere denies the heavenly bodies any share in divinity while maintaining their place in the created order. The sūrah of Fussilat says that among God's signs are the day, the night, the sun, and the moon, and commands the reader not to prostrate to the sun or the moon but to prostrate to God who created them (41:37). The sūrahs of the Bee, the Prophets, and Ibrāhīm all describe the sun, the moon, and the stars as subjected by God's command (7:54; 14:33; 16:12; 21:33), which is the position Abraham states in the verses that follow.

**"This Is My Lord" and the Difficulty It Raises**

The sūrah itself resolves the question at the end of the section when it calls Abraham's discourse the argument God gave him against his people (6:83). Whichever reading of the individual sentences is adopted, the Book presents the sequence as a demonstration that belongs to Abraham's mission rather than as a record of a mistake he made and corrected.

**The Argument From Setting**

But when it set, he said, I do not love things that set. The argument is compressed into that phrase, and it takes the form of a test that anyone can apply: a god should not be subject to disappearance, change, or decay. The Book states the same principle about the created order when it describes the sun as travelling for its fixed term and the moon as passing through phases until it looks like an old curved palm stalk. When it says that the sun cannot catch up with the moon and that the night cannot outrun the day, since each is travelling in an orbit of its own (36:38–40).

The audience's gods shared the defect of the objects they were identified with.

**A Concession the Audience Would Have Made**

The Book records that the Makkans themselves admitted who had made the heavenly bodies when they were asked directly. The sūrah of the Spider says that if they were asked who created the heavens and the earth and subjected the sun and the moon, they would certainly say Allah, and it follows the admission with a question about how they could then be deluded (29:61). The sūrah of Luqmān and the sūrah of the Troops record the same answer to the same question (31:25; 39:38), and the sūrah of the Ornament adds that they would name the Almighty, the All-Knowing as the maker (43:9).

Those admissions are the ground on which Abraham's argument stands in this verse. The audience already accepted that the sun and the moon were creatures under God's command; what they had not done was follow the concession to its conclusion. The verse shows the founder of their tradition doing exactly that.

---

## Verse 6:77

> Then when he saw the moon rising, he said, “This one is my Lord!” But when it disappeared, he said, “If my Lord does not guide me, I will certainly be one of the misguided people.”

**The Second Candidate**

The moon rises next, and the language of the search is repeated: This one is my Lord. The sūrah is building a sequence, and the pattern of the previous verse is preserved: the appearance, the identification, the disappearance, and the rejection. What changes is the scale of the object, since the moon is brighter and more regular than the star, and the audience that venerated it would have considered it the stronger candidate.

The Book repeatedly describes the moon as a creature under command. The sūrah of Yā-Sīn says that God ordained precise phases for it until it ends up like an old curved palm stalk (36:39). The sūrah of the Heights includes the moon among the objects subjected by God's command, together with the sun and the stars (7:54); and the sūrah of Ibrāhīm describes the sun and the moon as constantly orbiting by God's arrangement (14:33). Each description leaves the moon doing what it was told, which is the argument the verse has Abraham making.

**How the Rejection Changes**

The Book records the same posture in the sūrah of the Cattle's later description of him when it says that God showed him the wonders of the heavens and the earth so that he might be among those who have certainty (6:75), which means the guidance he asked for had already been promised.

**The Moon and Its Place in the Calendar**

The moon had a particular hold on the region's religious practice, since its phases governed the months and the times of pilgrimage and sacrifice. A man who abandoned the moon as a god was therefore not making a small adjustment to the local pantheon; he was cutting off the object most closely bound to the rituals of the community. The sūrah's own audience would have felt the weight of that, and the Book records elsewhere that the new moon is appointed for the timing of pilgrimage and for the reckoning of time (2:189).

The Book states the rule that Abraham was invoking when it says that whoever God guides is truly guided, and that whoever He leaves to stray will find no guardian besides Him (7:178). The sūrah of the Cave repeats the sentence in the middle of its account of the sleepers, and the sūrah of the Night Journey gives it in a longer form that ends with the warning that such people will be dragged before their Lord deaf, dumb, and blind (17:97; 18:17). Abraham's plea is therefore not a formula of humility but an accurate description of where guidance comes from, and the Book states elsewhere that the Prophet himself cannot guide whomever he loves, since it is God who guides whom He wills (28:56).

The contrast the verse draws is between the calendar and its maker. The Book nowhere suggests that timekeeping is a fault; it presents the sun and the moon as marks by which people can count the years and the reckoning. The fault is in the worship, and the verse shows Abraham separating an useful creature from the Creator whom it serves.

Abraham's final statement is not a rejection but a declaration of disownment: he turns his face toward the One who originated the heavens and the earth, upright, and declares that he is not among those who associate partners with God (6:78–79).

---

## Verse 6:78

> Then when he saw the sun shining, he said, “This must be my Lord—it is the greatest!” But again when it set, he declared, “O my people! I totally reject whatever you associate ˹with Allah in worship˺

**The Setting That Ends the Case**

But again when it set, he declared his rejection of everything his people associated with God. The sentence moves directly from the observation to the address, and the address is public: O my people! The earlier verses recorded Abraham's words to himself or to his father; this one records him speaking to the community that worshipped the sun, and he says that he is done with the whole set of their deities.

The Book's word for the setting describes disappearance or decline, and its use here clinches the argument the previous verses were making. Whatever is subject to the horizon cannot be the One who placed it there, and the Book states the same principle in its own voice when it commands the reader not to prostrate to the sun or the moon but to the God who created them both (41:37).

**The Public Break With the Community**

The Prophet had been told in this very sūrah to say that he had been forbidden to worship what they invoked and that he would not follow their desires (6:56). The passage about Abraham supplies the model of what that refusal looked like when it was made by the founder of their tradition.

**"Whatever You Associate With Allah in Worship"**

The Book makes the same point where it reports the idolaters admitting that their gods were names they and their fathers had invented, with no authority from God (12:40). Abraham's declaration follows from that: what he rejected was not a set of powerful rivals but a set of names attached to creatures. The word he uses in this verse states the category, which is why the sentence applies to every generation that has made the same arrangement.

**Sun Worship in the Book's Accounts**

The Book records the worship of the sun as the practice of another ancient people, described in the report of the hoopoe that Solomon received: I found her and her people prostrating to the sun instead of God, with Satan having made their deeds appealing and hindered them from the right way (27:24). The verse treats the practice as a familiar error rather than as an exotic one, and it places the sun where the sūrah has placed it, among the created things that human beings have taken as lords.

The Book also describes the end of the sun's course, since the objects the audience venerated are among the first to be extinguished on the day of judgment. The sūrah of the Resurrection says that the moon will be dimmed and the sun and the moon will be brought together (75:8–9), and the sūrah of the Folding Up opens with the sun being put out and the stars falling (81:1–2). Whatever a community has worshipped from the sky is so shown in the Book to be waiting for its own end.

The sūrah of the Tested describes Abraham and those with him saying to their people that they dissociate themselves from them and from whatever they worship besides God, and that the enmity between them will last until the people believe in God alone (60:4).

---

## Verse 6:79

> I have turned my face towards the One Who has originated the heavens and the earth—being upright—and I am not one of the polytheists.”

**The Act of Turning**

I have turned my face toward the One Who has originated the heavens and the earth. The sentence is built on the image of a person who changes direction, and the Book uses the same verb for the act of turning to God in repentance and for the turning of a face away from what is false. Abraham's declaration begins with a movement rather than with a proposition, and the movement is the whole of his religious position in a single image.

The Prophet is commanded to say that he will not take any guardian other than God, the Originator of the heavens and the earth, who provides for all and needs no provision (6:14), and the sūrah later uses the same title in the passage about God's having no offspring (6:101).

**The Meaning of Upright**

The Book applies the description to Abraham in a series of passages that read like a refrain. Abraham was neither a Jew nor a Christian, but he submitted in all uprightness and was not a polytheist (3:67). The believers are told that the faith of Abraham the upright, who was not a polytheist, is the answer to the claims of the People of the Book (2:135); and the Book describes him as a model of excellence, devoted to God, perfectly upright, and not a polytheist (16:120). The recurrence of the same sentence across sūrahs shows how much work the Book assigns to it.

**The Prophet and the Faith of Abraham**

The Book commands the Prophet to follow the same way, and it does so in the language of this verse. He is told that his Lord has guided him to a straight path, a perfect way, the faith of Abraham, the upright, who was not one of the polytheists (6:161). The Book commands him to follow the Way of Abraham the upright (3:95; 16:123); and it says that God revealed this instruction to him. The sūrah's Abraham section is therefore not only an argument against the Makkans but a statement about the identity of the community being formed.

The connection runs through the House as well. The Book records Abraham raising the foundations of the House with Ishmael and praying that it be accepted (2:127). It recalls God assigning to him the site of the House with the instruction not to associate anything with God in worship and to purify it for those who circle it and bow and prostrate (22:26). The religion being restored was the one Abraham had practised at the same place, which is why the declaration in this verse matters for the city the sūrah is addressing.

**What the Declaration Cost**

The Book records what Abraham's people did with his religion in the sūrah of the Prophets, where he smashed their idols and left the greatest of them standing so that the people might turn to it for answers (21:58). Where his community concluded that they should burn him to avenge their gods (21:68). The sūrah of the Ranged records the same verdict in the words of the crowd that called for a furnace for him (37:97). The declaration of belief is followed in the Book's narrative by a trial of fire.

That sequence gives the sūrah's audience a frame for the verses they had just heard about the Prophet. He had been told to declare that he did not worship what they invoked (6:56), and the section that follows shows what such a declaration had meant for the man whose tradition they claimed. The Book is not presenting Abraham's story as a distant legend; it is presenting a precedent.

---

## Verse 6:80

> And his people argued with him. He responded, “Are you arguing with me about Allah, while He has guided me? I am not afraid of whatever ˹idols˺ you associate with Him—˹none can harm me,˺ unless my Lord so wills. My Lord encompasses everything in ˹His˺ knowledge. Will you not be mindful

**The Argument His People Made**

And his people argued with him. The sūrah does not record the terms of their argument, and that silence is informative. What the Book preserves instead is Abraham's reply, and the reply shows what the dispute was about: fear. The Makkans of his day were not defending a philosophical position but a relationship they believed they had with powers that could be offended, and Abraham's answer addresses that belief directly.

The Book describes the same style of argument among the polytheists of the Prophet's own time, who said that if God had willed they would never have worshipped anything other than Him, nor forbidden anything without His command (16:35). The sūrah of the Ornament records the same claim in almost the same words, and adds that the speakers have no knowledge in support of it and do nothing but lie (43:20). The dispute with Abraham belongs to that long-running argument about human practice and divine permission.

**"While He Has Guided Me"**

Are you arguing with me about Allah, while He has guided me? The clause places guidance at the centre of the dispute, and it does so before Abraham says anything about the idols. His position rests on something he has received rather than on something he has worked out, and the sūrah has already described that gift in its account of the showing given to him (6:75).

The Book treats guidance as the decisive element in every such dispute, and it states that whoever God guides is truly guided while whoever He leaves to stray will find no guardian besides Him (7:178). The two participants in this argument were therefore not equals exchanging opinions. One had been shown the wonders of the heavens and the earth, and the other had inherited a set of names.

**"I Am Not Afraid of What You Associate With Him"**

The sentence addresses the practical stake of the controversy, and it removes the fear that the community's religion was built on. Abraham's position is that the idols cannot harm him, and the Book makes the same claim in its own voice when it challenges the idolaters to consider whether their gods could undo a harm God willed or withhold a mercy He granted (39:38).

The concession that follows is important: unless my Lord so wills.

**The Knowledge That Encompasses Everything**

My Lord encompasses everything in His knowledge. The sentence gives the reason Abraham can speak with confidence about a matter that involves the unseen, and it repeats one of the sūrah's central claims. The sūrah said earlier that God knows what is on land and sea and that not a leaf falls without His knowledge (6:59), and it described the record kept of every deed (6:61).

The same phrase appears on the lips of Moses in the sūrah of Ṭā-Hā, where he tells his people that their only god is God, and that He encompasses everything in His knowledge (20:98). The sūrah of the Divorce states that God encompasses all things in knowledge (65:12), and the sūrah of the Women says that God is fully aware of everything (4:126). The claim functions in these verses as a boundary on human speculation: the audience may not know what the gods are doing, but nothing is outside the knowledge of the One Abraham serves.

---

## Verse 6:81

> And how should I fear your associate-gods, while you have no fear in associating ˹others˺ with Allah—a practice He has never authorized? Which side has more right to security? ˹Tell me˺ if you really know!”

**The Comparison of Fears**

And how should I fear your associate-gods, while you have no fear in associating others with Allah? The verse turns the audience's objection back on them, and it does so by comparing two fears. Abraham is being told that he should be afraid of the gods his people serve, and his answer is that they should be far more afraid of what they are doing with those gods. The question is not whether fear is appropriate but where it belongs.

The argument is built on a comparison of magnitudes that the audience would have accepted if they had been willing to state their beliefs openly. Their gods were objects they had made, inherited, or named (12:40); the One they associated with those gods had made the heavens and the earth (6:73). A person who fears a stone and does not fear its maker has made an error about proportions, and the verse lets the error speak for itself.

**Nothing Authorizes the Practice**

The clause about practice He has never authorized is the legal form of the same accusation, and the Book states it repeatedly. It says that the names the idolaters use are inventions that God never authorized (12:40), and it asks in the sūrah of the Romans whether God has sent down any authority that shows what the people associate with Him (30:35). The sūrah of Yūnus records the claim that God has offspring and answers that the claimants have no proof and are speaking about God what they do not know (10:68).

The Book's point throughout is not that the audience had chosen badly among options but that they had no warrant at all. Nothing in the revelation, and nothing in the created order, gives a human being permission to assign a partner to God, and the verse describes the practice as unauthorized rather than merely mistaken.

**The Idiom of Right and Entitlement**

The phrase the translation renders as which side has more right, with the instruction to tell if you know, is an invitation to judge between two claims. The Book uses this form of challenge in several places where it asks the audience to arbitrate against itself, as when it asks whether the blind and the seeing can be equal or whether darkness and light are the same (13:16).

Those challenges work because the audience already knows the answer. The Makkans would not have claimed that a stone had as much right to their fear as the Creator of the sky, and the verse in front of us asks them to say so out loud. The final words, if you really know, carry the sting, since the sūrah has been demonstrating for eighty verses what they did not know.

**What the Two Verses Establish**

Read together, the two verses state Abraham's full position before his people: no fear of the idols, no acceptance of an unauthorized practice, and a question about where security is to be found. The next verse gives the answer as a statement of fact rather than as an argument, and the sūrah then names Abraham's discourse as the argument God gave him against his people (6:83).

---

## Verse 6:82

> It is ˹only˺ those who are faithful and do not tarnish their faith with falsehood who are guaranteed security and are ˹rightly˺ guided

**The Answer to the Question of Security**

The previous verse asked which party had more right to security and told the audience to answer if they knew. This verse supplies the answer as a statement of fact: those who believe and do not tarnish their faith with falsehood are the ones guaranteed security and guided. The sentence names the two conditions and then attaches the two results, and the structure matches the pairing the sūrah used earlier when it described the believers and the deniers (6:48–49).

The Book states the same arrangement in other words when it says that whoever submits themselves to God and does good will have their reward with their Lord, and that there will be no fear for them nor will they grieve (2:112). The security in this verse is therefore not a general sense of safety but the specific condition of a person who has placed his trust where the power actually lies.

**The Word That Alarmed the Companions**

The condition about tarnishing faith with falsehood produced one of the most famous exchanges in the early community, and the Book's commentators record it in connection with this verse. According to the report in al-Bukhārī, when the verse was revealed the Companions were distressed by it and asked which of them had not mixed his belief with wrongdoing, since the word used here can mean any injustice or sin. The Prophet answered that the verse did not mean that, and directed them to the saying of Luqmān to his son: never associate anything with God in worship, for associating others with Him is truly the worst of all wrongs (31:13).

**Guidance as the Second Result**

The verse ends by saying that those who meet the condition are rightly guided, which adds the inward result to the outward one. The Book treats guidance as both a gift that precedes belief and a state that follows it, and the sūrah earlier described the Prophet as one whom God had guided while his people were arguing with him about God (6:80). The sentence here follows the same pattern: the person who holds to the faith without mixing it is confirmed in the direction he has taken.

The Book's description of the guided elsewhere attaches the same two marks. It says that whoever God guides is truly guided and that whoever He leaves to stray will find no guardian (7:178). It describes the people of understanding as those who reflect on the creation and the alternation of night and day and are led by that reflection to the acknowledgment of their Lord (3:190–191). Guidance in the Book's usage is not a status conferred on a group but a condition that shows itself in conduct and in the direction a person is facing.

---

## Verse 6:83

> This was the argument We gave Abraham against his people. We elevate in rank whoever We please. Surely your Lord is All-Wise, All-Knowing

**The Argument Credited to God**

This was the argument We gave Abraham against his people. The sentence closes the pericope with the Book's own verdict on the sequence just related, and it settles the question the commentators had raised about the star, the moon, and the sun. Whatever Abraham said and however the statements are read, the discourse as a whole is described here as something given to him rather than as something he produced, and the giving is attributed to God.

The Book uses the vocabulary of proof for the mission of the messengers generally when it says that they were sent as bearers of news and warnings so that humanity would have no excuse before God after their coming (4:165).

**Abraham's Case Elsewhere in the Book**

The Book records Abraham making the same kind of case in other settings and in other forms. In the sūrah of the Cow he argues with the king who disputed with him about his Lord. He answers the claim to give life and death with a demonstration that the king cannot perform (2:258). In the sūrah of the Prophets he questions his people about the statues to which they are devoted and then acts against them (21:52–58). And in the sūrah of the Poets he recites the catalogue of what his Lord has done for him, ending with the promise of forgiveness on the day of judgment (26:69–82).

**The Elevation of Whom He Pleases**

We elevate in rank whoever We please. The sentence states the principle that explains why Abraham could see what his people could not, and it repeats a formula the Book uses whenever it accounts for differences between people. The sūrah of Joseph uses the same words at the moment when Joseph's plan succeeds, noting that God elevates in rank whoever He wills and that above everyone with knowledge is the One who knows all (12:76). The sūrah of the Gatherings states that God will elevate those who believe and those given knowledge by degrees (58:11).

The principle also answers a question the sūrah raised much earlier, in the passage about the poor believers and the wealthy notables. The sūrah said that God tests people by means of one another (6:53), and here it states the same arrangement in rank. Differences of understanding and of station are part of the design, and the sūrah's closing verse will return to the subject when it describes the ranks by which people are tested with what they have been given (6:165).

**The Transition to the Prophetic Line**

The verses that follow list the men who inherited the same guidance: Isaac and Jacob, Noah and his descendants, David and Solomon, Job and Joseph, Moses and Aaron (6:84), and then Zachariah, John, Jesus, and Elias (6:85), and then Ishmael, Elisha, Jonah, and Lot (6:86). The sūrah has placed Abraham at the head of a chain, and the gift of guidance is described as passing through it.

That arrangement gives the audience a way to read their own history. The Prophet being addressed in Makkah stood in a line whose members had all been given what Abraham was given, and the sūrah's next verses name them one by one so that the pattern cannot be mistaken for a claim about one exceptional man.

---

## Verse 6:84

> And We blessed him with Isaac and Jacob. We guided them all as We previously guided Noah and those among his descendants: David, Solomon, Job, Joseph, Moses, and Aaron. This is how We reward the good-doers

**The Gift of Descendants**

And We blessed him with Isaac and Jacob. The sentence follows the account of Abraham's trial with the announcement of what he received, and the Book pairs the two elsewhere in the same order. The sūrah of the Ranged records his prayer for righteous offspring (37:100), and the sūrah of the Prophets records its answer, saying that God blessed him with Isaac as a son and Jacob as a grandson, as an additional favour, and made all of them righteous (21:72). The addition of the words as an additional favour is the Book's way of saying that the gift exceeded what was asked.

The two names matter for the sūrah's audience as well as for Abraham. The tribes of Israel traced themselves to Jacob, and the Book elsewhere tells the Prophet that the believers make no distinction among the prophets and that they believe in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and their descendants (2:136; 3:84). The line that begins here is the line the sūrah will follow through the next two verses.

**"We Guided Them All"**

We guided them all as We earlier guided Noah. The sentence places guidance rather than lineage at the centre of the gift, and it names Noah as the standard by which the others are measured for a reason that the next verse makes explicit: the men listed here are described as being among his descendants. The sūrah of Iron states the arrangement directly when it says that God sent Noah and Abraham and reserved prophethood and revelation for their descendants (57:26).

**The Reward Attached to Good Conduct**

This is how We reward the good-doers. The sentence names the reward and the conduct it answers, and the reward in context is the guidance just described. The Book repeats the connection in several places: those who struggle in God's cause will be guided along His ways (29:69), and Noah is described as utterly grateful for God's favours, with the result that God chose him and guided him to the straight path (16:121). Guidance, in the Book's usage, is both the gift that starts a person on the road and the reward that follows the conduct it produces.

The sūrah of Consultation states the same principle in a form that ties it to the whole line of prophets, saying that God has ordained the way He decreed for Noah. Abraham, Moses, and Jesus, and that He chooses for Himself whoever wills and guides to Himself whoever turns to Him (42:13). The catalogue in this verse is a list of people who did what that verse describes, and the reward they received is the guidance the sūrah says belongs to God.

**Why the List Matters to the Makkans**

The sūrah's audience had claimed Abraham and used the claim to argue that their religion was the religion of their fathers. The catalogue in this verse and the two that follow it widens the frame: the prophets who carried the same message include men from outside the Arabian peninsula and outside the audience's lineage. The Book had already said that God chose Adam, Noah, the family of Abraham, and the family of ʿImrān above all people of their time (3:33).

---

## Verse 6:85

> Likewise, ˹We guided˺ Zachariah, John, Jesus, and Elias, who were all of the righteous

**The Second Group in the Catalogue**

Likewise, We guided Zachariah, John, Jesus, and Elias, who were all of the righteous. The sentence continues the list from the previous verse without introducing a new subject, and the connecting word makes the catalogues one continuous act of guidance. The four names belong to the later part of the Israelite prophetic tradition, and they sit in the same list as David and Moses with no distinction drawn between them.

The arrangement is deliberate. The sūrah's audience argued with the Prophet about the standing of particular prophets, and the Christians of the region had raised Jesus to a status the Book rejects. The verse places him in a line with Zachariah and John and gives the whole group one description: they were all of the righteous. The Book makes the same point in the sūrah of the Family of Imran, where the believers are told that they make no distinction between any of the prophets (3:84).

**The Pair of Zachariah and John**

Zachariah and John are named together because their stories are joined, and the Book tells that story in detail in the sūrah of Maryam. Zachariah prayed for a heir in his old age, the prayer was answered with John, and the Book describes the household as one that used to race in doing good, calling upon God with hope and fear, and humbling themselves before Him (21:90). The father and the son stand at the head of the group in this verse as an example of what the guidance produces.

---

## Verse 6:86

> ˹We also guided˺ Ishmael, Elisha, Jonah, and Lot, favouring each over other people ˹of their time˺

**The Favour Named at the End**

The phrase translated as favouring each over other people of their time is the Book's language of election, and it is used for the prophetic calling in several places. The sūrah of the Family of Imran says that God chose Adam, Noah, the family of Abraham. The family of ʿImrān above all people of their time (3:33); the sūrah of the Bee describes Abraham as a model of excellence whom God chose and guided to a straight path (16:121); and the sūrah of the Ranged records Lot being described as one of the messengers (37:133).

The wording also makes clear what the favour was not. Being preferred over the people of one's time is a statement about a mission and its reception, not a claim about the inherent superiority of one lineage over another. The sūrah has already said that God elevates in rank whoever He pleases (6:83), which removes the possibility of reading the catalogue as a genealogy of merit.

**Ishmael and the Arabian Line**

Ishmael's place in the list would have struck the sūrah's first audience with particular force, since the northern and central Arabian tribes traced their descent to him. The Book records him building the foundations of the House with Abraham and praying for acceptance (2:127), and it describes him as true to his promise and as a messenger and a prophet. The sūrah of Maryam gives him a place among those mentioned with honour, and the sūrah of the Prophets describes him with Enoch and Dhū al-Kifl as steadfast.

His inclusion in this verse means that the Arabian line is not absent from the prophetic catalogue. The audience could not claim that God had confined guidance to the descendants of Isaac, and they could not claim that their own ancestor had been passed over. The sūrah places Ishmael in the same list as the Israelite prophets and gives the whole group the same description.

**Lot and the Cities**

Lot's story is the Book's account of a community destroyed for conduct that the Book describes in detail, and his name in this list places him among the messengers who warned their own people. The sūrah of Hūd records the angels' arrival at his house and their instruction that he should travel with his family in the dark of night and not look back, with the exception of his wife, and it records their announcement that the appointed time of the cities was the morning (11:81).

**Jonah and the Prayer From the Dark**

Jonah is the prophet whose story the Book tells in the sūrah of the Prophets, where he leaves his city in anger, is swallowed, and calls out from the veils of darkness that there is no god but God and that he has certainly done wrong (21:87). The Book records that his prayer was answered and that he was rescued from anguish, and it adds the sentence that this is how the believers are saved (21:88).

---

## Verse 6:87

> And ˹We favoured˺ some of their forefathers, their descendants, and their brothers. We chose them and guided them to the Straight Path

**The Two Verbs at the End**

We chose them and guided them to the Straight Path. The sentence states two distinct acts, and the Book uses both for the prophetic mission. To choose is to select a person for a purpose, and to guide is to set him on the road that leads to it. The sūrah of Maryam uses the same pair when it describes the prophets as those whom God has blessed among the descendants of Adam, of those carried with Noah, and of the descendants of Abraham and Israel, and as those whom God rightly guided and chose (19:58).

The pairing protects a truth about the religious life that the sūrah has been teaching throughout. Election without guidance would be a status without a path, and guidance without election would be a road that no one had been appointed to walk. The Book's descriptions of the prophets consistently hold the two together, and the sūrah states both in a single clause.

**The Straight Path as the Common Destination**

The phrase the Book uses for the road here is the same one it uses for the Prophet's own path in the sūrah's later declaration, where he says that his Lord has guided him to a straight path, a perfect way, the faith of Abraham the upright (6:161). The recurrence is not incidental. The catalogue of prophets and the sūrah's final statement of the Prophet's religion end at the same phrase, which tells the reader that the road named in this verse is the road the sūrah has been describing from its opening verses.

**The Book's Wider Use of the Formula**

The Book uses the language of choosing and favouring for whole communities as well as for individuals, and the sūrah of the Cow records that God made Abraham a role model for the people (2:124). The sūrah of the Family of Imran states the principle that God chose certain families above all people of their time (3:33), and the sūrah of the Pilgrimage says that God named the community of believers the ones who submit in the earlier scriptures and in this recitation (22:78).

**The Sentence That Follows**

The guidance given to all these men is named in the next verse as God's guidance, given to whoever He wills of His servants, and the verse after it makes the startling statement that if they had associated others with God their deeds would have been wasted (6:88). The two sentences take the catalogue that has just been assembled and use it as the basis for the sūrah's final argument about what the audience's practice would have cost even the greatest of the men whose names they honoured.

The reader is therefore meant to carry the names forward into those statements. The prophets were chosen and guided, and the guidance was a gift rather than a possession, and it could be lost. That is the lesson the sūrah draws from the list, and it states it without qualification in the next sentence.

---

## Verse 6:88

> This is Allah’s guidance with which He guides whoever He wills of His servants. Had they associated others with Him ˹in worship˺, their ˹good˺ deeds would have been wasted

**The Guidance Identified as God's Gift**

This is Allah's guidance with which He guides whoever He wills of His servants. The sentence states what the catalogue of names has been demonstrating, and it names the guidance as God's rather than as the possession of the men who received it. The Book uses the same construction when it describes the guidance given to the prophets of the line of Abraham, and the possessive form keeps the gift attached to its giver.

The statement also answers the question the sūrah's audience had been raising about the Prophet. If guidance is given to whoever God wills, then the identity of the man carrying the message is not the decisive question, and the Book had already said as much in the verse about the poor believers when it stated that God knows best who is grateful (6:53). The catalogue in the previous verses shows the same principle at work across centuries.

**The Conditional Sentence About the Prophets**

The Book states the same rule elsewhere in the second person. The sūrah of the Troops records the revelation given to the Prophet and to the prophets before him, that if anyone associates others with God his deeds will certainly be void and he will be among the losers (39:65). The verse in front of us takes that rule and applies it hypothetically to the greatest figures in the catalogue, which is the Book's way of making the point impossible to evade by reference to ancestral standing.

**What the Sentence Is Not Saying**

The point lands with particular force because the sūrah has just included Abraham in the catalogue, and Abraham's declaration ended with the words that he was not one of the polytheists (6:79). The audience's claim to his legacy is so met with a statement about what would have happened to him had he done what they were doing, and the sūrah supplies no room for negotiation on that point. The verse that follows this one describes the men of the catalogue as those to whom God gave the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood, and it warns that if the audience disbelieves in the message, God has already entrusted it to a people who will not disbelieve in it (6:89). The two sentences belong to one movement: the gift is named, the danger to its recipients is stated, and the message is passed to those who will carry it.

---

## Verse 6:89

> Those were the ones to whom We gave the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood. But if these ˹pagans˺ disbelieve in this ˹message˺, then We have already entrusted it to a people who will never disbelieve in it

**The Three Gifts Named Together**

Those were the ones to whom We gave the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood. The sentence summarizes what the catalogue of names had in common, and it names three gifts where a reader might have expected one. The Scripture is the revelation given to a messenger; wisdom is the understanding that allows it to be applied; prophethood is the office of carrying it to a people. The Book uses the same triple formula for the children of Israel generally, saying that God gave them the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood, granted them good and lawful provisions, and favoured them above the others (45:16).

The combination is important for the argument the sūrah has been making.

**Wisdom as a Distinct Gift**

The Book treats wisdom as a distinct gift from revelation, and it describes its own message as containing both. The sūrah of the Family of Imran says that it is not appropriate for a man blessed with the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood to tell people to worship him instead of God, but that he would instruct them to be devoted to the worship of their Lord (3:79). The sūrah of the Women describes the descendants of Abraham receiving the Book and wisdom along with great authority (4:54).

**The Transfer of the Trust**

But if these pagans disbelieve in this message, then We have already entrusted it to a people who will never disbelieve in it. The sentence announces a transfer, and it does so without naming the people who will receive it. Commentators took the community in question to be the believers of Madinah and the generations that carried the message afterward, and the Book states the same principle in several places: if the audience turns away, God will replace them with another people (47:38; 9:39).

The sūrah of the Table gives the promise in a form that describes the replacement community, saying that if any among the believers abandon their faith, God will bring a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble with the believers and firm toward the disbelievers, striving in His way and fearing no blame from anyone (5:54). The verse in front of us states the same fact in the past tense, as though the transfer had already been decided, which is the Book's way of expressing certainty about what God will do.

**The Comfort the Sentence Gives the Prophet**

The sentence also addresses the Prophet's situation directly. He had been told that he was not a keeper over his people (6:66), that every matter has a fixed time (6:67), and that he would face mockery (6:68). The announcement of a people who would never disbelieve gave him something to look toward while the city he lived in refused the message.

The Book states the same comfort in other forms, including the promise that God is enough for the Prophet against those who mock him and the statement that the message is a reminder to the whole world (6:90, in the sentence that follows). The sūrah is moving from the argument with Makkah toward a declaration of the message's scope, and the next verse completes that movement by commanding the Prophet to follow the guidance of the men just catalogued.

---

## Verse 6:90

> These ˹prophets˺ were ˹rightly˺ guided by Allah, so follow their guidance. Say, “I ask no reward of you for this ˹Quran˺—it is a reminder to the whole world.”

**The Command to Follow the Guidance of the Prophets**

These prophets were rightly guided by Allah, so follow their guidance. The command is addressed to the Prophet and through him to the community, and it treats the catalogue of the previous verses as a practical model rather than as a historical record. The line of men who received the Scripture, wisdom, and prophethood are to be followed in the guidance they carried, and the book of their message is the same book the Prophet was reciting.

The Book issues the same command in connection with Abraham, telling the Prophet that God revealed to him to follow the faith of Abraham the upright (16:123), and telling the believers that they follow the way of their forefather Abraham (22:78). The sūrah of the Family of Imran states the same instruction twice, first as an answer to the claims of the People of the Book (3:95) and then in the declaration that God has guided the Prophet to the faith of Abraham (6:161, in this sūrah). The command in this verse extends the model to the whole prophetic line.

**What Following the Guidance Involves**

The Book states the continuity explicitly when it says that God has ordained the way He decreed for Noah, for Abraham, for Moses, and for Jesus, and that the instruction given to all of them was to uphold the faith and make no divisions in it (42:13).

**The Refusal of Payment**

Say, I ask no reward of you for this. The sentence detaches the messenger from any material interest in the response, and the Book repeats it in several sūrahs. The Prophet is told to say that he does not ask for any reward but that whoever wishes may take a path to his Lord (25:57). The sūrah of Sheba gives the answer that if he had ever asked for a reward they could keep it, since his reward comes from God alone (34:47); and the sūrah of Yūnus records the same declaration in the words of Noah to his people (10:72).

**A Reminder to the Whole World**

It is a reminder to the whole world. The sentence states the scope of the message in the widest terms available, and the Book repeats it in the same words in other places, saying that the Qur'an is nothing but a reminder to the whole world (12:104; 38:87). The sūrah of the Prophets states the same universality in a different form, telling the Prophet that God has sent him only as a mercy for the whole world (21:107).

The claim has consequences for the audience's assumption about their own standing. A reminder addressed to the whole world cannot belong exclusively to one city, one clan, or one line of descent, and the sūrah has just said that the message would be entrusted to a people who would not disbelieve in it (6:89). The two sentences together define the message's reach and remove the audience's ability to treat it as a family possession.

The Book's teaching on the prophets is that they form a single community with a single message and that God has favoured some of them over others in particular respects (2:253).

---

## Verse 6:91

> And they have not shown Allah His proper reverence when they said, “Allah has revealed nothing to any human being.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Who then revealed the Book brought forth by Moses as a light and guidance for people, which you split into separate sheets—revealing some and hiding much? You have been taught ˹through this Quran˺ what neither you nor your forefathers knew.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Allah ˹revealed it˺!” Then leave them to amuse themselves with falsehood

**The Charge of Failing to Appraise God**

And they have not shown Allah His proper reverence. The Book uses this formula in two other places, and each time it is attached to a failure of understanding. The sūrah of the Troops uses it for people who cannot conceive of God's power to hold the earth in His grip and roll up the heavens on the day of judgment (39:67). The sūrah of the Pilgrimage uses it in a passage about those who worship beings that can create nothing (22:74). Here it is attached to a claim about revelation, and the claim is that God has never revealed anything to any human being.

The severity of the sentence comes from what the claim implies. A person who says that God has never spoken to humanity has denied the whole of the tradition he claims to honour, and the Book's answer begins by naming the one book the audience could not deny.

**The Book of Moses as the Counter-Example**

Who then revealed the Book brought forth by Moses as a light and guidance for people? The question is addressed to people who acknowledged the Torah, and it leaves them with no answer except the one they were refusing to give. The Book uses the same method of argument elsewhere, appealing to the earlier scriptures as evidence that revelation happens, since the audience accepted those scriptures as genuine.

The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God revealed the Book in truth confirming what came before it, as He revealed the Torah and the Gospel (3:3). The sūrah of the Cow says that Gabriel revealed the Qur'an confirming what came before it, as a guide and good news for believers (2:97). The continuity between the earlier books and this one is the sūrah's theme in these verses, and the audience's own acceptance of Moses is the ground on which the argument stands.

**The Charge of Concealment**

Which you split into separate sheets, revealing some and hiding much. The sentence accuses the custodians of the earlier scripture of selective disclosure, and the Book makes the same accusation in several places. It says that the Messenger has come revealing much of what the People of the Book used to hide (5:15). It records the covenant taken from those given the Scripture to make it known and not hide it, and their casting it behind their backs in exchange for a fleeting gain (3:187); and it says that a group of them hides the truth knowingly (2:146).

**The Dismissal at the End**

Then leave them to amuse themselves with falsehood. The verse ends by withdrawing the Prophet from the argument, and the instruction uses the same vocabulary the sūrah used earlier when it told him to leave those who take the faith as play and amusement (6:70). The people who have just denied that God ever revealed anything to a human being are described in the terms of the earlier passage, and the sūrah declines to make their amusement the Prophet's responsibility.

The closing instruction also prepares the reader for the verse that follows, which presents the Book as a blessed revelation sent down to warn the Mother of Cities and those around it.

---

## Verse 6:92

> This is a blessed Book which We have revealed—confirming what came before it—so you may warn the Mother of Cities and everyone around it. Those who believe in the Hereafter ˹truly˺ believe in it and guard their prayers

**The Confirmation of the Earlier Scriptures**

Confirming what came before it. The Book states this function of the Qur'an in many places and attaches it to the act of revelation itself. The sūrah of the Family of Imran says that God revealed the Book in truth confirming what came before it, as He revealed the Torah and the Gospel (3:3). The sūrah of the Cow says that Gabriel brought the revelation to the Prophet's heart by God's will, confirming what came before it (2:97); and the sūrah of the Table describes the Qur'an as a confirmation of previous scriptures and a supreme authority over them (5:48).

**The Mother of Cities and Those Around It**

So you may warn the Mother of Cities and everyone around it. The Book uses the same expression in the sūrah of Consultation, where it says that the Prophet is to warn the Mother of Cities and all around it about the Day of Gathering (42:7). The phrase identifies Makkah as the first city in the mission's scope, and the words that follow extend the warning outward without limit.

That expansion is worth noticing because the sūrah's audience might have taken the warning to be addressed to their city alone. The Book's statement that the message is a reminder to the whole world (6:90) has already fixed its scope, and this verse's reference to the cities around the sanctuary establishes the order in which the warning reaches them. The Book elsewhere describes the settlement at the sanctuary as established for the sake of all people, and it tells the Prophet that he was sent as a mercy to the worlds (21:107).

**The Sign of Belief in the Hereafter**

Those who believe in the Hereafter truly believe in it and guard their prayers. The verse gives the audience a test that distinguishes the two kinds of response, and it builds the test from two things the sūrah has been teaching: conviction about the coming judgment and the practice of prayer. The sūrah has already described the believers who call upon their Lord morning and evening (6:52) and it has stated that the Prophet has been commanded to establish the prayer and to be mindful of God (6:72).

The pairing appears throughout the Book. The sūrah of the Believers lists those who humble themselves in prayer among the successful and includes those who are properly observant of their prayers among the inheritors of Paradise (23:9). The sūrah of the Cow describes the Book as guidance for those who believe in the unseen, establish the prayer, and are certain of the Hereafter (2:3–4). The sūrah's description of belief in this verse uses the same two marks.

**Why the Hereafter Is the Test**

The choice of the Hereafter as the marker is not arbitrary, and the sūrah has spent many verses on the subject. Belief in the Hereafter is the conviction that requires a person to live by a standard other than his neighbours' approval, and the Makkans' resistance to the message was rooted in their assumption that death ended everything. The sūrah recorded their insistence that there is nothing beyond this worldly life and that they would never be resurrected (6:29).

A person who believes in the Hereafter has already accepted that the present arrangement of wealth and power is not the final one, and the sūrah has used that acceptance throughout as the dividing line. The Book states the same criterion in the verse about the warning given to those who fear the gathering (6:51), and it repeats it here as a description of those who will accept the Book.

The sūrah of the Prophets calls it a blessed reminder sent down and asks its audience whether they will deny it (21:50). The sūrah of the Cattle itself repeats the description near its end when it says that this is a blessed Book revealed so that people might follow it and be shown mercy (6:155).

---

## Verse 6:93
> Who does more wrong than the one who fabricates lies against Allah or claims, “I have received revelations!”—although nothing was revealed to them—or the one who says, “I can reveal the like of Allah’s revelations!”? If you ˹O Prophet˺ could only see the wrongdoers in the throes of death while the angels are stretching out their hands ˹saying˺, “Give up your souls! Today you will be rewarded with the torment of disgrace for telling lies about Allah and for being arrogant towards His revelations!”

**The Greatest Wrong, Stated Again**

Who does more wrong than the one who invents a lie about God? The question appears at several points in the Book, and each time it is attached to a particular offence. This sūrah used it near its beginning for the person who invents a lie about God or denies His signs (6:21 states it in that form). It also appears in the sūrah of the Heights and the sūrah of the Spider in similar words. Here it introduces a passage about people who claimed to receive revelation.

The sūrah has been dealing with the Makkans' claims about the Book, and this verse turns the accusation around. The men who said that God had revealed nothing to any human being (6:91 records the claim) are themselves placed among those who manufacture revelation. The verse then names three claims in a row: inventing a lie against God, claiming that revelation has come to one who received nothing, and promising to bring down the like of what God has sent. All three are the same act in different sizes.

**The Claimants the First Readers Knew**

The community's early historians recorded who was meant. In the Prophet's own lifetime two men set themselves up as rivals with revelations of their own, one in the Yemen and one in Yamāmah, and the second of them produced rhymed sentences and claimed they were scripture. The verse is not only about them, since its words cover every pretender in every age, but they give the accusation a face.

The verse's sharpest phrase is the middle one: that revelation was sent to him, when nothing had been sent to him at all. The Book's claim is that revelation is a gift God gives, not a rank a person takes. So the crime is not the making of a book. It is the taking of a position that only God can give.

**The Scene at Death**

The verse then opens a window: if you could only see the wrongdoers in the throes of death while the angels stretch out their hands. The sūrah has already described the angels who take a soul and never neglect their duty (6:61 gives that), and this verse supplies the scene the earlier one left out.

The angels extend their hands and order the dying to hand over their souls, and the order comes with an announcement of what waits for them. The Book describes the same moment elsewhere with harsher detail. It records the angels striking the faces and the backs of those who disbelieve (47:27 states it), and it records the angels taking the souls of those who wronged themselves and asking them what they had been doing (4:97 records the question). Here the picture is of a moment when a lifetime of argument is answered by the arrival of the angels, and the answer takes the form of an order that cannot be refused.

**The Charge Read Out at the Deathbed**

The announcement is short: today you will be repaid with the punishment of disgrace for what you used to say about God without knowledge, and for being too proud to accept His signs. Two offences are named, and they match the two habits of the audience this sūrah has been addressing. Earlier verses described people who denied the signs and turned away from them while they could still see and hear (6:46 describes that turning), and this verse states what the conduct receives at the end of it.

**What the Verse Completes in the Sūrah**

The verse closes a section that began with the claim that God had revealed nothing to anyone, and it continues into the treatment of people who claimed to produce revelation themselves (6:91 gives the claim). So the sūrah moves from the gift of revelation, to its denial, to its imitation, and it places all three inside one frame of accountability.

The verse that follows shows the same people arriving before God with nothing in their hands, and the two passages belong together. What the angels say at the deathbed is said again in full at the judgement, and the sūrah supplies both scenes so that its reader can see the whole of the sequence at once, from the claim made in the market to the sentence read out at the end.

---
## Verse 6:94

> ˹Today˺ you have come back to Us all alone as We created you the first time—leaving behind everything We have provided you with. We do not see your intercessors with you—those you claimed were Allah’s partners ˹in worship˺. All your ties have been broken and all your claims have let you down.”

**The Things Left Behind**

Leaving behind everything We have provided you with. The sentence names what has been stripped away, and it includes everything a person used to make himself secure: wealth, property, position, and the alliances that protected him in his city. The Book describes the goods of this world as provided for use in the present life, and it says that whatever is sent ahead for the soul is what a person will find waiting for him (73:20).

**The Intercessors Who Do Not Appear**

We do not see your intercessors with you, those you claimed were Allah's partners in worship. The verse takes the Makkans' own religious claim and tests it at the point where they expected it to pay off. The sūrah has described their belief that the idols were intercessors with God (6:51; 6:70), and it has described the impossibility of ransom or plea on that day (6:70).

The Book records the same scene in many other passages, saying that the beings the idolaters invoked will disown them and that the ties between them will be severed (6:24; 7:37; 14:22). The verse in front of us states the separation as a fact of the judgment rather than as a warning about it, and the sentence is spoken to the people who had built their lives around the claim.

**The Severing of Ties**

All your ties have been broken and all your claims have let you down. The Book's word for the ties is the one used for the connections between people and between a person and his patrons, and the sentence announces that the whole network has been dissolved. In a society organised around tribal protection, this was a total statement: the claims that gave a man standing in Makkah were precisely the ones that would not be available to him there.

The Book uses the same vocabulary when it says that the disbelievers will see the punishment and that every cause will be cut off from them (2:166), and when it describes the followers of the wrongdoers grieving over those they followed.

**What the Verse Says About Intercession as a Doctrine**

The sūrah has mentioned intercession several times and has consistently denied it to anyone besides God. It said that those warned about the gathering will have no protector and no intercessor besides Him (6:51), and it said that those who take their religion as play will find no protector or intercessor other than God, even if they offered every ransom (6:70). This verse completes the sequence by showing the intercessors themselves absent from the scene.

The Book's teaching on intercession in other passages is that it occurs only by God's permission and for those He permits, which is why the sūrah's denials are phrased as denials of any intercessor besides Him. What the verse eliminates is the assumption the Makkans had built their religious practice on: that the beings they associated with God held a claim on Him that they could exercise for their clients.

---

## Verse 6:95

> Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout. He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. That is Allah! How can you then be deluded ˹from the truth˺

**The Splitting of the Seed**

Indeed, Allah is the One Who causes seeds and fruit stones to sprout. The verse opens a series of signs drawn from the created order, and it begins with the smallest and most ordinary of them. A seed is buried, splits, and sends out a living shoot; a fruit stone does the same, and the process repeats itself in every field and orchard the audience walked past.

The sūrah of Yā-Sīn puts the two together when it says that there is a sign in the dead earth: God gives it life and produces grain from it for people to eat (36:33).

**The Name That Comes From Splitting**

Commentators noticed that the Arabic term for the One who splits the seed shares its root with the divine title the Originator, which the Book uses for God in the phrase Originator of the heavens and the earth. The title appears in the sūrahs of Ibrāhīm, Fāṭir, the Troops, Consultation, and elsewhere, and the sūrah used it in the declaration Abraham makes when he turns his face toward the One who originated the heavens and the earth (6:79).

That connection gives the image its religious weight. Creation in the Book's language is not assembly from existing parts but an originating, and the splitting of a seed is the visible form of the same power at work. The sūrah's audience, who watched the fields around their city come to life every season, was being shown the operation of the creating command in the ordinary course of nature.

**Bringing the Living From the Dead**

He brings forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. The sentence states the cycle in both directions, and the Book repeats it in several sūrahs. The sūrah of the Family of Imran uses it in a prayer that addresses God as the one who causes the night to pass into the day and brings the living from the dead (3:27). The sūrah of Yūnus includes it in a list of questions the idolaters answer by naming God (10:31); and the sūrah of the Romans states it and then says that this is how people will be brought forth from the grave (30:19).

The commentators explained the two directions in several ways. The living coming from the dead covers the creation of a human being and the raising of the dead; the dead coming from the living covers death itself and the natural cycle by which a living plant produces a seed that must be buried before it grows. The two divine names that the theologians attach to these acts, the Giver of Life and the Bringer of Death, name the same pair of operations.

**The Conclusion Drawn**

The sūrah has been asking this form of question throughout. It asked how those who had been rescued at sea could then associate others with God (6:64), and it asked how the people who argued with Abraham could fail to be mindful of the One who encompasses everything in knowledge (6:80). The question here closes the first of a series of signs, and the same structure returns in the verses that follow.

---

## Verse 6:96

> He causes the dawn to break, and has made the night for rest and ˹made˺ the sun and the moon ˹to travel˺ with precision. That is the design of the Almighty, All-Knowing

**The Night Made for Rest**

And has made the night for rest. The verse names a purpose for the darkness, and the Book states the same arrangement in the sūrah of the News, where it says that God made sleep for rest, the night as a covering, and the day for livelihood (78:9–11). The sūrah of the Discernment gives the same set of functions in slightly different words, saying that God made the night a covering, sleep a repose, and the day a rising (25:47).

The sūrah used this particular gift at length in the passage about God calling back the souls of people at night and reviving them by day (6:60), and it treated the alternation as a daily demonstration of the resurrection. Here the same gift is listed among the signs of the created order, which shows how firmly the sūrah's argument ties the ordinary rhythm of human life to its claims about the Hereafter.

**The Sun and the Moon With Precision**

And made the sun and the moon to travel with precision. The Book attaches the same description to the two luminaries in several places: the sūrah of the Most Compassionate says that the sun and the moon travel with precision (55:5). The sūrah of Yūnus says that God made the sun a radiant source and the moon a reflected light, with precisely ordained phases so that people might know the number of years and the calculation of time (10:5). And the sūrah of the Troops says that the sun and the moon each orbit for an appointed term (39:5).

The precision in view is the order that makes calendars possible, and the audience, whose religious year depended on the moon, understood what it meant. The sūrah of the Cow records the question about the phases of the moon and answers that they are a means for people to determine time and the pilgrimage (2:189). The verse in front of us states the arrangement as a design rather than as a convenience, and the design is credited to the One whose signs the sūrah has been presenting.

**The Two Names That Close the Sentence**

That is the design of the Almighty, All-Knowing. The two names state the attributes that the created order displays: the power that holds the arrangement in place and the knowledge that measured it out. The Book attaches the same pair to the same subject in the sūrah of Yā-Sīn, where it says that the sun travels for its fixed term and that this is the design of the Almighty, the All-Knowing (36:38).

**What the Sign Does Not Do**

The sūrah's description of the dawn, the night, the sun, and the moon is followed by the statement that God has made the signs clear for people who know (6:97). The sequence leaves open the outcome the sūrah has been documenting from its opening: the signs are clear, and clear signs do not compel a response. The sūrah has described people who saw the same sky and drew the conclusions the verses are correcting, and it has described people who read the signs and were led by them.

---

## Verse 6:97

> And He is the One Who has made the stars as your guide through the darkness of land and sea. We have already made the signs clear for people who know

**The Stars as Guides**

And He is the One Who has made the stars as your guide through the darkness of land and sea. The sūrah has just described the sun and the moon marking time; this verse names the stars and gives them a different function, which is navigation. The Book uses the same image in the sūrah of the Bee, where it says that by landmarks and stars people find their way (16:16), and it repeats the phrase about the darkness of land and sea in the passage on the keys of the unseen (6:59).

**The Stars as Subjected Creatures**

The Book's account of the stars in other sūrahs makes their status plain. The sūrah of the Heights describes the sun, the moon. The stars as subjected by God's command and says that creation and command belong to Him alone (7:54). The sūrah of the Bee lists the stars among the things made subject for human benefit and says that in this there are signs for people who understand (16:12). And the sūrah of the Discernment describes the constellations God placed in the sky along with a radiant lamp and a luminous moon (25:61).

There is a pointed contrast between the Book's treatment of the stars and the practices of the audience. The Makkans venerated certain heavenly bodies and consulted astrologers about their influence, and the sūrah of the Cattle earlier showed Abraham testing the star and rejecting it when it set (6:76). The same objects that were folded into the region's astrology are presented here as servants of the travellers who used them.

**The Darkness That Requires a Guide**

The phrase about the darkness of land and sea deserves attention because the Book regularly uses darkness as an image of confusion. The sūrah described the man seduced by satans in the open country as one who has lost his way (6:71), and it uses darkness for the state of a person without guidance in several passages. The gift of the stars is therefore an image of guidance in the plain sense as well as a service to travellers.

**The Signs Made Clear for People Who Know**

We have already made the signs clear for people who know. The sentence closes the verse with a statement about the audience's capacity rather than about the quality of the signs. The clarity has been supplied, and what the verse names is the kind of reader who benefits from it, which is the same qualification the sūrah attached to its earlier account of the varying of the signs (6:46).

The Book attaches the same qualification to other passages about its signs, saying that they are made clear for people who know, for people who understand, and for people who believe. The phrasing is the Book's way of stating that the evidence is not the variable. What differs between two people looking at the same sky is the knowledge they bring to it, and the sūrah has spent many verses describing what happens when that knowledge is absent.

The sūrah has already recorded the audience's use of the heavens in the story of Abraham, where the star, the moon, and the sun were examined and abandoned as candidates for divinity (6:76–78).

---

## Verse 6:98

> And He is the One Who originated you all from a single soul, then assigned you a place to live and another to ˹be laid to˺ rest. We have already made the signs clear for people who comprehend

**The Single Soul as the Origin of Humanity**

And He is the One Who originated you all from a single soul. The sentence introduces a new sign after the catalogue of the sky, and it turns from the heavens to the origin of human beings themselves. The Book states the creation from a single soul in several sūrahs: the sūrah of the Women opens with the command to be mindful of the Lord who created humanity from one soul and made its mate from it (4:1). The sūrah of the Heights gives the same account with the detail that the mate was made so that he might find comfort in her (7:189); and the sūrah of the Troops states it as the first item in a passage about the formation of human beings in the wombs (39:6).

The claim serves two arguments at once. It states God's power to originate a species from one beginning, which the sūrah uses as evidence for the power to raise the dead. It states the common origin of all people, which the Book uses to deny the claims of superior descent that the sūrah has been dismantling throughout this chapter. The sūrah of the Chambers draws the conclusion explicitly when it says that God made people into nations and tribes so that they might know one another, and that the most noble among them is the most mindful (49:13).

**The Two Places Assigned**

Then assigned you a place to live and another to be laid to rest. The Book's commentators offered several readings of the two terms. Some took them as the womb and the grave, which are the two stations a person passes through. Others took them as the settled dwelling and the place of rest in the earth; others applied the pair to the living and the dead among human beings; and some gave them a broader sense of the stations of the present life and the Hereafter.

What the readings share is a picture of human life as movement between two stations appointed by God. The sūrah has just described the soul taken at night and returned by day (6:60) and the angels taking the soul at death (6:61), and the pair of places in this verse continues the same account of a life that is measured out from beginning to end. The audience is being told that neither the arrival nor the departure is in its own hands.

**What the Verse Says About Human Equality**

The sentence also states a fact about the human family that the sūrah's audience needed to hear. The Makkans divided their society by clan, wealth, and lineage, and the sūrah had already told the Prophet not to dismiss the poor believers at the request of the notables (6:52) and had described the differences of rank as a test (6:53). The claim that all people come from one soul cuts across every division the city maintained.

---

## Verse 6:99

> And He is the One Who sends down rain from the sky—causing all kinds of plants to grow—producing green stalks from which We bring forth clustered grain. And from palm trees come clusters of dates hanging within reach. ˹There are˺ also gardens of grapevines, olives, and pomegranates, similar ˹in shape˺ but dissimilar ˹in taste˺. Look at their fruit as it yields and ripens! Indeed, in these are signs for people who believe

**The Rain and What It Produces**

And He is the One Who sends down rain from the sky, causing all kinds of plants to grow. The sūrah moves from the origin of human beings to the provision that sustains them, and it does so with a description that is unusually detailed for this part of the chapter. Grain in clustered heads, dates hanging within reach, vineyards, olives, and pomegranates: the verse names the crops of the region and invites the reader to look at them.

The Book returns to this list of fruits in several places. The sūrah of the Bee describes the rain producing crops, olives, palm trees, grapevines. Every type of fruit, and calls it a sign for those who reflect (16:11). The sūrah of the Believers names gardens of palm trees and grapevines with abundant fruits (23:19); and the sūrah of the Thunder describes neighbouring tracts of land irrigated by the same water, with some fruits made better in taste than others, calling this a sign for those who understand (13:4). The details in this verse belong to a pattern the Book uses whenever it wants its audience to look at what they eat.

**The Pairing of Similar and Dissimilar**

Similar in shape but dissimilar in taste. The phrase appears in this sūrah's own passage on the gardens (6:141) and again in this verse, and the observation it records is one the audience could verify in any market. The sūrah of the Thunder makes the same point about the same water feeding different soils and producing different flavours (13:4), and it draws the conclusion that this is a sign for people who understand.

**"Look at Their Fruit"**

Look at their fruit as it yields and ripens. The command to look is the Book's ordinary way of directing attention, and it appears throughout the created-order passages. The sūrah of ʿAbasa tells the reader to consider his food and then describes the rain, the earth, and the crops that come from it (80:24), and the sūrah of the Cow gives the same sequence in a passage about the revived earth.

The verse does not ask for an opinion about the origin of the world; it asks for attention to a process the reader can follow over weeks.

**The Signs for People Who Believe**

Indeed, in these are signs for people who believe. The clause closes the series that began with the seed by naming belief as the capacity in question, and it completes the progression across three verses. The signs of the sky were clear for people who know (6:97); the sign of the single soul was clear for people who understand (6:98); the signs of the harvest are clear for people who believe (6:99).

The variation in the closing clauses is not a stylistic flourish. The Book's vocabulary of reception moves from knowledge to understanding to belief, and it treats the last as the fullest response. A person may know that the rain produces the harvest, and understand that the same water feeds different soils; belief is what happens when he attributes the arrangement to the One the sūrah has been naming.

---

## Verse 6:100

> Yet they associate the jinn with Allah ˹in worship˺, even though He created them, and they falsely attribute to Him sons and daughters out of ignorance. Glorified and Exalted is He above what they claim

**The Jinn Associated With God**

Yet they associate the jinn with Allah in worship, even though He created them. The verse names a specific form of the audience's belief, and the Book records the same practice elsewhere. The sūrah of the Ranged says that the idolaters established a relationship between God and the jinn, and it notes that the jinn themselves know that those who say such things will be brought for punishment (37:158). The sūrah of the Jinn records the jinn's own declaration that their Lord has taken neither a mate nor offspring (72:3).

The claim being refuted took several forms in the region's religions: that the jinn were divine beings with a share in the rule of the world, that they were God's offspring, and that certain objects of worship were their representatives. The verse's answer to all of it is contained in a single clause: He created them. Whatever is created cannot be a partner of its creator, and the sūrah has been applying that reasoning to idols, celestial bodies, and prophets throughout the chapter.

**The Argument From Creation**
