The phrase He created them is the same argument the sūrah used against the idols, where it said that the beings invoked besides God are created like their worshippers (7:194). What changes from one passage to the next is only the category of being: in each case the Book removes the possibility of divinity by stating the fact of creation.

**The Attribution of Sons and Daughters**

And they falsely attribute to Him sons and daughters out of ignorance. The Book records several versions of this claim. The sūrah of the Night Journey asks the pagans whether their Lord has favoured them with sons while taking angels as daughters. It calls the claim outrageous (17:40); the sūrah of the Star records the same objection, asking whether they prefer sons for themselves while attributing daughters to God (53:21). And the sūrah of the Ornament says that they labelled the angels, who are servants of the Most Compassionate, as female, and asks whether they witnessed their creation (43:19).

The Book's response to the claim about the angels and the claim about Jesus follows the same line. The sūrah of the Table and the sūrah of the Women address the Christian formulation directly by saying that the Messiah was a messenger of God and that God is far above having a son (4:171). To attribute offspring to God is, in the Book's account, to import the conditions of created life into the divine, and the verse names the source of that error as ignorance.

**The Formula of Transcendence**

Glorified and Exalted is He above what they claim. The verse ends with the Book's standard declaration of God's transcendence, and the declaration is used throughout the Book at exactly these points, where a false claim about God has been described. The sūrah of the Cow records the claim that God has offspring and answers it with the same words before stating that everything in the heavens and the earth is subject to Him (2:116).

---

## Verse 6:101

> ˹He is˺ the Originator of the heavens and earth. How could He have children when He has no mate? He created all things and has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of everything

**The Originator of the Heavens and the Earth**

He is the Originator of the heavens and earth. The title is one the sūrah has used before, both in the command given to the Prophet to declare that he will take no guardian other than the Originator of the heavens and the earth (6:14) and in Abraham's declaration when he turned his face toward the One who originated the heavens and the earth (6:79). The Book uses the title consistently in the phrase Originator of the heavens and the earth, and the commentators noted that its root carries the sense of splitting open, which connects it to the image of the seed that splits at the beginning of this passage about signs.

**How Could He Have Children?**

How could He have children when He has no mate? The question reduces the claim to an impossibility in a single clause, and the Book makes the same argument elsewhere in its own voice. The sūrah of the Cow answers the claim that God has offspring by saying that everything in the heavens and the earth is subject to Him (2:116). The sūrah of Maryam states that it is not for God to take a son, since when He decrees a matter He simply says to it, Be, and it is (19:35).

The Book's argument about offspring rests on two observations: children are produced through the union of mates, and God has no mate; and everything other than God is created, whereas a child would have to share in the divine nature. The sūrah of Sincerity gathers the conclusion into a single declaration that God has never had offspring and was never born (112:3). The sūrah of the Women addresses the same claim in its discussion of Jesus, saying that the Messiah was no more than a messenger of God and that God is far above having a son (4:171).

**He Created All Things**

He created all things. The clause states the fact on which the argument turns. If everything besides God is created, then nothing besides God is ultimate, and the relations that belong to created things cannot be predicated of Him. The sūrah has used this reasoning in every form of the argument against idolatry it has presented: the idols are created like their worshippers (7:194), the jinn are created and therefore cannot be partners (6:100), and the angels are servants of the Most Compassionate (43:19).

The Book states the same principle in the sūrah of the Thunder, where it declares that God is the Creator of all things and the One, the Supreme (13:16). The sentence in this verse is therefore more than a statement of fact; it is the premise from which the sūrah's denials proceed, and the audience is expected to recognise it as the basis of everything the Book has been saying about worship.

**Knowledge of Everything**

And has perfect knowledge of everything. The verse closes with the attribute the sūrah has attached to God at several points in the chapter. It said earlier that God knows what is on land and sea and that not a leaf falls without His knowledge (6:59), and it described His knowledge as encompassing everything in the argument attributed to Abraham (6:80). The closing phrase here connects the account of creation with the account of the record, since the One who made all things is the One who knows all things.

---

## Verse 6:102

> That is Allah—your Lord! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him. ˹He is˺ the Creator of all things, so worship Him ˹alone˺. And He is the Maintainer of everything

**The Conclusion Drawn From the Argument**

That is Allah, your Lord! The sentence gathers the whole preceding account of the created order and attaches a name to it, and the Book's demonstrative form is the same one it used when it described the splitting of the seed and asked how the audience could be deluded (6:95). The verse identifies the subject of the signs: the One who originated, provided, guided, and knew, and it states the identity as a conclusion rather than as a new claim.

**The Creator Who Is Worshipped**

He is the Creator of all things, so worship Him alone. The verse moves from the fact of creation to the command to worship, and the connection is the sūrah's persistent argument. The Makkans already accepted that God had created everything; their error lay in the conclusion they drew from the fact, and the verse states what the conclusion should be.

The Book makes the same connection in the sūrah of the Troops, where it states that God is the Creator of all things and the Maintainer of everything (39:62). In the sūrah of the Thunder, where the acknowledgement of God as the Creator of all things is followed by the declaration that He is the One, the Supreme (13:16). The command to worship in this verse is the practical form of a fact the audience had already granted.

**The Maintainer of Everything**

And He is the Maintainer of everything. The Book's term describes the one who manages and arranges the affairs entrusted to him, and the sūrah has applied it to God at several points. The sūrah of the Troops uses the same title for God at the head of a passage on provision (39:62); the sūrah of Hūd states that God is the Trustee of all affairs (11:12); and the sūrah of the Confederates says that God is enough as a Trustee of affairs (33:3).

**Maintenance and the Everyday Evidence of It**

The title the Book attaches to God in this verse appears elsewhere in the context of provision. The sūrah of Fāṭir asks whether there is any creator other than God who provides for the audience from the heavens and the earth, and it follows the question with the same declaration of exclusive divinity that stands in this verse (35:3). The sūrah of Consultation says that God is kind to His servants and provides abundantly for whoever He wills, and it names Him the All-Powerful, the Almighty (42:19). Both passages join the act of providing to the title of maintainer, which is the pairing this verse makes.

The Book treats that pairing as the answer to the audience's practical worries. The sūrah of the Spider states that many creatures cannot secure their own provisions and that God provides for them and for the people who worry about them (29:60). The sūrah of Hūd says that no creature on earth lacks a provision guaranteed by God and that everything is written in a perfect record (11:6). The Makkans who heard this verse lived from trade caravans and from the produce of the oases, and the Book's claim was that the arrangement behind both was the same arrangement described in the verses about the rain and the harvest.

---

## Verse 6:103

> No vision can encompass Him, but He encompasses all vision. For He is the Most Subtle, All-Aware

**How the Companions Used the Verse**

The earliest community read this verse as a statement about the vision of God, and the reports about ʿĀisha are the clearest example. When she was told that some people claimed that the Prophet had seen his Lord, she rejected the claim and cited this verse, saying that anyone who told her that Muhammad had seen his Lord had told a lie, because God says that no vision can encompass Him. The report is recorded by al-Bukhārī and it shows how the verse functioned in the community's theology from the beginning.

**The Names at the End**

The sūrah has attached the same vocabulary to God throughout its account of the hidden and the seen. It said that God knows what is on land and sea and that not a leaf falls without His knowledge (6:59); it described His knowledge as encompassing everything in Abraham's argument (6:80); and it stated that He has the keys of the unseen (6:59). The names that close this verse are the summary of that whole account.

**The Creation and the Knowledge of It**

The sūrah of the Kingdom puts the two halves of this verse's logic together in a single sentence, asking how the One who created could fail to know what He has made, and naming Him the Most Subtle, the All-Aware (67:14). That is the reasoning that stands behind this verse: the maker of a thing knows it in a way no outside observer can, and the knowledge described in the second clause is the knowledge of the one who formed what He sees.

The Book states the same principle through the image of the mustard seed in the counsel of Luqmān, where the father tells his son that a deed the weight of a seed, hidden in a rock or in the heavens or the earth, will be brought out by God, who is the Most Subtle, the All-Aware (31:16). The closing names of this verse carry the same scope, and the sūrah of the Women and the sūrah of the Confederates attach the pair to the recording of what people do. A perception that runs through rock and seed and sky is the perception the sūrah has claimed for God throughout, and this verse states it as the ground of the account that follows.

**The Transition That Follows**

The next verse turns from the description of God to the responsibility of the people who have been given this knowledge, saying that insights have come from the Lord and that whoever chooses to see does so for his own good while whoever chooses blindness does so to his own loss (6:104). The verses function as a pair: the true account of God is stated, and the reader is told that the response to it is his own.

The sūrah has been making the same point since its account of the blind and the seeing (6:50), and the pairing in these two verses is its final statement of the principle in this section. What follows is a series of further signs, and the audience is left with a choice that the Book describes without pressure.

Some held that God cannot be seen at all, and they cited this verse together with the answer given to Moses when he asked to see his Lord and was told that he could not see Him, though the mountain was levelled when God appeared to it (7:143).

---

## Verse 6:104

> Indeed, there have come to you insights from your Lord. So whoever chooses to see, it is for their own good. But whoever chooses to be blind, it is to their own loss. And I am not a keeper over you

**The Insights Given From the Lord**

Indeed, there have come to you insights from your Lord. The Book's word for what has come is a plural of perception or discernment, and the commentaries describe it as the clear evidences by which a person sees the truth of the message. The sūrah of the Story uses the same word for the Scripture given to Moses, calling it an insight for the people, a guide, and a mercy (28:43), and the sūrah of the Kneeling describes the Qur'an as an insight for humanity, a guide and mercy for people of sure faith (45:20).

**Whoever Chooses to See**

So whoever chooses to see, it is for their own good. But whoever chooses to be blind, it is to their own loss. The two clauses use the language of sight and blindness, which the sūrah has already established in its earlier questions about whether the blind and the seeing can be equal (6:50). What the verse adds is the element of choice: the reader is described as the one who decides, and the consequences are attached to the decision rather than to the perception.

The Book states the same arrangement in several places. The sūrah of the Cave says that the message is the truth from the Lord and that whoever wills may believe and whoever wills may disbelieve (18:29). The sūrah of the Ant has the Prophet told to recite and then to say that whoever chooses guidance does so for his own good and that the messenger is only a warner (27:92); and the sūrah of the Troops says the same about guidance and straying (39:41). The formula protects the freedom that the Book's invitations assume.

**"I Am Not a Keeper Over You"**

And I am not a keeper over you. The sentence returns to a refrain the sūrah has used twice before. The Prophet was told to say that he is not a keeper over the people at the end of a passage about the truth that had come to him (6:66), and the same denial appears in the account of his own announcement that he follows what is revealed to him (6:50 context). The sūrah of Yūnus and the sūrah of the Troops attach the same statement to the message (10:108; 39:41).

The repetition is a feature of the sūrah's method rather than a lapse, and it marks the points at which the audience is being told that the messenger's role has limits. The insights have come from the Lord, the choice belongs to the person who hears them, and the man who delivers them is not responsible for the outcome. What the Book does with this recurring statement is to remove the pressure that the Prophet's grief over his people had placed on him. The next verse describes the varying of the signs and the accusation that will follow from it: the disbelievers will say that the Prophet has studied the earlier scriptures, and the Book will answer that the message has been made clear for people who know (6:105). The two verses belong together, since both concern the response of the people who were receiving the clearest possible presentation of the message.

---

## Verse 6:105

> And so We vary our signs to the extent that they will say, “You have studied ˹previous scriptures˺,” and We make this ˹Quran˺ clear for people who know

**The Fourth Statement About Varying the Signs**

And so We vary our signs. The sūrah has used this vocabulary three times before. It described the varying of the signs when the audience turned away from the argument about hearing and sight (6:46). It used the same word when the explanation of the signs was said to make the way of the wicked distinct (6:55); and it used it in the warning about the punishments that could come from above, from below, or through the community's own divisions (6:65). The recurrence marks the stages of the sūrah's argument, and each occurrence states a different purpose.

The Book uses the same term elsewhere for the presentation of the message in many forms. The sūrah of the Heights and the sūrah of the Bee describe the signs as varied so that people might be mindful and give thanks, and the sūrah of the Ant speaks of the varying of the signs for a people who understand. What the term describes is a method of instruction rather than a rhetorical flourish, and the sūrah presents it as a deliberate provision for an audience that needed the argument in more than one shape.

**The Accusation That Will Follow**

To the extent that they will say, You have studied previous scriptures. The sentence states what the varying of the signs will produce in the audience, and it records the accusation in advance. The charge is that the Prophet had learned his material from the People of the Book. The Book records the same charge in several passages: the sūrah of the Bee quotes it and answers that the person the accusers have in mind speaks a foreign tongue while the Qur'an is in eloquent Arabic (16:103). The sūrah of the Discernment records that they said the message was a fabrication made up with the help of others and that it consisted of ancient fables written down and rehearsed morning and evening (25:4–5).

**The Clarity Given to People Who Know**

And We make this Qur'an clear for people who know. The sentence closes the verse with the same qualification the sūrah has attached to its other accounts of the signs: the clarity is real, and it is realised in people who know. The sūrah has used the phrase about the stars and the signs (6:97), and it has used the same construction at the end of the passage about the varying of the signs earlier (6:55).

**The Sequence in Which the Verse Stands**

The verse follows the statement that the insights have come from the Lord, that the choice is the reader's, and that the messenger is not a keeper over the people (6:104). Read as an unit, the two verses describe the delivery of the message, the freedom of the response, the limits of the messenger's office, and the accusation that the Book knew would follow. The sūrah is preparing the reader for the rest of the chapter, which continues the argument with a people who had been shown the signs in every form.

---

## Verse 6:106

> ˹O Prophet!˺ Follow what is revealed to you from your Lord—there is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—and turn away from the polytheists

**The Command to Follow What Is Revealed**

Follow what is revealed to you from your Lord. The command stands at the head of a passage in which the sūrah turns from the argument with the disbelievers to the instructions given to the Prophet himself, and its position is significant. After many verses of signs, refutations, and accounts of what the audience said, the sūrah states the one thing the messenger is to do with all of it, which is to follow the revelation.

The Book's use of this command elsewhere gives the phrase its force. The sūrah of the Cattle ends its long list of prohibitions with the instruction to follow the straight path and with the declaration that this is what God has commanded (6:153). The sūrah of the Bee tells the Prophet that God has revealed to him to follow the way of Abraham, who was a man of pure faith (16:123). The command is the practical counterpart of the arguments that precede it.

**The Declaration That Accompanies It**

The sūrah has attached this sentence to several of its conclusions: to the account of the created order (6:102), to the description of the messenger's own position (6:19 context), and to the declaration of the truth that has come from the Lord. Here it accompanies the command to follow, and the two together state the terms of the Prophet's own obedience. What he is told to do is what every other person addressed by the sūrah was told to do, with the difference that his following includes the delivery of the message.

**The Instruction to Turn Away**

And turn away from the polytheists. The sūrah has issued a similar instruction at earlier points: it told the Prophet to leave those who took their religion as play and amusement (6:70) and it told him to say that he is not a keeper over the people (6:104). The command to turn away in this verse is not a command to abandon the argument, since the sūrah continues to address the audience's objections after it.

**The Verses That Define the Limits**

The verse that follows states the limits in a form that leaves no ambiguity: had God willed, they would not have been polytheists, the Prophet has not been appointed as their keeper, and he is not their maintainer (6:107). The two verses belong together, and they answer the question of how a person should act toward a community that has rejected the message he carries.

The Book uses the same structure in other sūrahs, joining a command to the Prophet with a statement about its scope. The sūrah of the Bee says God has guided whom He wills and that the Prophet's duty is to call people to the way of the Lord with wisdom and good instruction (16:125). This sūrah's version of the instruction is to follow, to declare, and to keep his own course, which is what the conclusion of the passage states.

The Book records the same ordering in the sūrah of the Star, where the Prophet's utterance is described as nothing other than revelation sent to him (53:4).

---

## Verse 6:107

> Had Allah willed, they would not have been polytheists. We have not appointed you as their keeper, nor are you their maintainer

**The Statement of Divine Will**

Had Allah willed, they would not have been polytheists. The sentence states that the existence of the audience's idolatry was permitted by God's will, and the sūrah has used the same form of statement elsewhere. It said that had God willed, the whole of humanity would have been one community, and it said that had God willed, no one would have associated partners with Him.

The Book's use of this statement is worth distinguishing from a claim the audience would later make themselves. In a verse near the end of this sūrah, the idolaters are recorded as pleading that if God had willed they would not have associated partners with Him or with Him forbidden anything, and the Book describes that argument as a lie (6:148). The Book makes the same claim as an acknowledgement of God's sovereignty and rejects it when it is offered by the idolaters as a justification for their conduct.

**Why the Two Uses Differ**

The sūrah of the Bee records the same argument from the idolaters, who said that had God willed, neither they nor their ancestors would have worshipped anything besides Him (16:35). The Book's answer is that the messengers were sent with clear proofs and that the communities' rejection did not alter the obligation to receive them.

**The Keeper and the Maintainer**

We have not appointed you as their keeper, nor are you their maintainer. The verse names two roles and denies both to the Prophet, and the choice of the two words covers the two ways a person might imagine the messenger's authority. The keeper is the one set over a people to direct and manage them; the maintainer is the one responsible for their affairs and answerable for their condition.

The sūrah has already told the Prophet to say that he is not a keeper over the audience (6:104), and the repetition of the term in this verse joins the two passages as one teaching. The Book's description of the messenger's office elsewhere uses the vocabulary of reminding and warning rather than governance and responsibility: the sūrah of the Covering says that the Prophet is only a reminder and not a controller over the people (88:21–22). The sūrah of the Prophets says that He was sent as a mercy to the worlds rather than as an enforcer of belief (21:107).

**What the Verse Says About the Audience's Freedom**

The Book states the same arrangement in the sūrah of the Cave, where the truth is said to come from the Lord and each person is left to believe or disbelieve (18:29). The two verses that stand here combine that freedom with the messenger's own limits, and the sūrah proceeds to the next instruction, which concerns how the Prophet and the believers should speak about the gods the idolaters venerate.

---

## Verse 6:108

> ˹O believers!˺ Do not insult what they invoke besides Allah or they will insult Allah spitefully out of ignorance. This is how We have made each people’s deeds appealing to them. Then to their Lord is their return, and He will inform them of what they used to do

**The Reason Given in the Verse**

Or they will insult Allah spitefully out of ignorance. The prohibition rests on the effect the believers' words would have on the people they were calling. A man who is told that his gods are worthless may respond by attacking the God he has been invited to worship, and the insult would be uttered in ignorance, which is the condition the sūrah has repeatedly attributed to the audience's theology.

The Book applies the same reasoning in its instructions about how the message should be delivered. The sūrah of the Bee commands the Prophet to call people to the way of the Lord with wisdom and good instruction and to argue with them in the manner that is best (16:125). The sūrah of the Spider gives the same instruction for disputes with the People of the Book (29:46). The prohibition in this verse is the negative form of that method: what the believers were not to do was to speak in a way that made their hearers worse.

**The Deeds Made Fair to Each Community**

This is how We have made each people's deeds appealing to them. The sentence explains why the insult forbidden in the first clause would have the effect described. The idolaters were not weighing the evidence; their practices had been made attractive to them, and the Book describes that condition in many places. The sūrah of the Family of Imran says that the disbelievers should not think that God's respite for them is good for them, since He grants them time only that they may increase in sin (3:178). The sūrah of the Ranged says that those who do wrong are misled in their ways; and the sūrah of the Bee records the idolaters' claim that God willed their practices.

**The Return and the Accounting**

Then to their Lord is their return, and He will inform them of what they used to do. The verse closes by referring the whole question to the judgment, and the Book uses the same formula at the end of its discussions of the idolaters' conduct. The sūrah of the Spoils uses the phrase after a passage about those who were left behind, saying that their return is to God and that He will inform them of what they did (9:94). The sūrah of Tobā gives the same statement about God's informing people of their deeds (9:105); and the sūrah of Yūnus, the sūrah of the Spider, the sūrah of Luqmān, the sūrah of the Troops, and the sūrah of the Congregation use the same formula in their own contexts.

---

## Verse 6:109

> They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that if a sign were to come to them, they would certainly believe in it. Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Signs are only with Allah.” What will make you ˹believers˺ realize that even if a sign were to come to them, they still would not believe

**The Oath and What It Promised**

They swear by Allah their most solemn oaths that if a sign were to come to them, they would certainly believe in it. The verse records the terms on which the audience offered to accept the message, and the Book has recorded the same kind of oath elsewhere. The sūrah of Fāṭir says that they swore by God their most solemn oaths that if a warner came to them they would be better guided than any other community, and that when the warner came it only increased their aversion (35:42). The sūrah of the Table and the sūrah of Light record comparable oaths from those who claimed they would follow if only a sign were shown.

The Book treats the demand for a sign as a negotiating position rather than an expression of an open mind, and the oaths recorded in these passages are the clearest evidence for that judgement, since in every case the sign came and the oath was broken.

**The Answer Given to the Prophet**

Say, Signs are only with Allah. The instruction tells the Prophet how to answer, and the answer moves the question out of the hands of the messenger. The Book states elsewhere that no messenger could bring a sign except by God's permission: the sūrah of the Thunder says that a messenger is not able to bring a sign save by God's leave (13:38), and the sūrah of the Forgiver states the same (40:78). The sentence therefore answers the demand by placing the signs with the One who alone decides whether they will be shown.

**What Will Make You Realise**

What will make you realise that even if a sign were to come to them, they still would not believe? The second half of the verse is addressed to the believers, and the Book uses this form of address when it wants its readers to notice something they had not considered. The question is not a request for information; it is a statement that the audience's objection was never the real obstacle.

---

## Verse 6:110

> We turn their hearts and eyes away ˹from the truth˺ as they refused to believe at first, leaving them to wander blindly in their defiance

**The Reports About the Turning of Hearts**

The commentaries connect this vocabulary to a well-known report recorded in the Sunan of al-Tirmidhī. Umm Salama was asked which supplication the Messenger of God made most often in her presence, and she answered that it was, O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm in Your religion. When she asked him why he supplicated so often, he answered that every human heart lies between two fingers of the Most Merciful, who keeps firm whom He wills and turns aside whom He wills. The narration also appears from Anas ibn Mālik with the same phrases, and the reports describe the same divine action that this verse names.

What the verse and the reports together describe is God's complete disposal of human inward life. The Book states the same fact in its passages about the sealing of hearts, eyes. Ears, where those who persist in denial are described as unable to perceive what is addressed to them (2:7; 6:46), and the sūrah has used that description in its account of the people who turned away from the signs.

**Leaving Them in Their Defiance**

Leaving them to wander blindly in their defiance. The sentence names the condition in which the people who refused are left, and the Book's term for the wandering describes a state of confusion in which a person cannot find his way. The image belongs with the sūrah's earlier descriptions of those who walk in darkness: the man whom the satans have seduced in the open country and who has companions calling him to guidance he does not follow (6:71), and the people whose hearts and eyes were turned away in this verse.

The Book is careful to state that the wandering is the consequence of a choice, and the commentators explained the arrangement in that sense: God does not interfere with a person's decision, does not cut short his life to prevent further wandering, and does not compel the path he takes.

**The Pattern of the Sealed Heart**

The sūrah has stated the same arrangement before, when it described the sealing of the hearts of those who denied the signs and the blindness that prevented them from seeing them (6:46; 6:25). The sūrah of the Cow states the pattern in its opening passage, where God is described as sealing the hearts of the disbelievers, covering their hearing. Placing a veil over their sight (2:7), and the sūrah of the Heights gives the same description with the detail that they will not believe until they see the painful punishment.

The verses in this sūrah belong to that family, and the point they make is about the relationship between a person's first decision and his subsequent capacity. The Book presents the relationship as one of consequence rather than of arbitrary imposition: the people who refused to believe when the message first reached them are left without the disposition to believe when the signs appear later.

---

## Verse 6:111

> Even if We had sent them the angels, made the dead speak to them, and assembled before their own eyes every sign ˹they demanded˺, they still would not have believed—unless Allah so willed. But most of them are ignorant ˹of this˺

**The Refusal That Would Remain**

They still would not have believed unless Allah so willed. The exception clause keeps the statement from becoming an absolute claim about human nature, and it aligns the verse with the rest of the sūrah's teaching about God's will. Belief is possible when God wills it, and the people described here were not among those for whom that will had been exercised.

The Book states the same exception in other places where belief is attributed to the will of God. The sūrah of Yūnus says that no soul can believe except by God's permission (10:100), and the sūrah of the Bee says that God guides whom He wills and that the Prophet's task is to convey the message. The verse here applies the principle to an audience that had seen enough evidence to believe and had refused it.

**The Ignorance of Most of Them**

The sūrah has used the charge of ignorance throughout its treatment of the audience. It said that they followed nothing but conjecture and that they spoke about God what they did not know; it described the attribution of children to God as a claim made out of ignorance (6:100); and it described the deniers as unable to perceive what was put before them (6:110). The sentence that closes this verse belongs to that pattern. The reports about the demand for a sign include the audience's request that a hill near the sanctuary be turned to gold, and they describe the Prophet beginning to supplicate for it before being told that such signs are followed by punishment when they are rejected.

The sūrah has stated that their hearts were turned away after their first refusal (6:110), and this verse supplies the consequence in evidence.

**The Pattern in the Earlier Communities**

The Book states the same judgement in the sūrah of the Night Journey, where God says that nothing keeps Him from sending the demanded signs except that the earlier peoples had already denied them, and where the she-camel given to Thamūd is named as an example (17:59). The verse establishes both halves of the principle the sūrah is stating here: signs are withheld in mercy, and the communities that received them and refused were destroyed.

The Book applies that precedent to the audience's demands at several points, and it consistently treats the request for a sign as a claim on God that He answers as He chooses. The sūrah of Yūnus says that no soul believes except by God's permission and that His wrath falls on those who are unmindful (10:100), which is the same arrangement the verse in front of us describes when it makes belief contingent on God's will.

---

## Verse 6:112

> And so We have made for every prophet enemies—devilish humans and jinn—whispering to one another with elegant words of deception. Had it been your Lord’s Will, they would not have done such a thing. So leave them and their deceit

**The Enemies Appointed for Every Prophet**

And so We have made for every prophet enemies, devilish humans and jinn. The verse states a fact about the prophetic calling that the Prophet had not been told before in this sūrah, and it does so as a general law rather than as an account of his own case. Enemies are part of the work, and the work has always involved them. The Book announces the same fact elsewhere: the sūrah of the Ranged records that the enemies of the former messengers received a respite in the world, and the sūrah of the Forgiver tells the Prophet that the messengers before him were also called liars.

**The Instruction Given to the Prophet**

The sūrah has issued the same instruction twice already, telling him to leave those who take their religion as play (6:70) and to turn away from the polytheists (6:106). The words are used again in the sūrah of the Bee, where the Prophet is told to strive against the disbelievers and hypocrites. The pattern is consistent throughout the Book: the messenger continues to speak, and the outcome is left to God.

The verse that follows states the purpose of the whispering in its own terms: the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter may receive it, be pleased with it, and become persistent in what they are doing (6:113). The passage therefore describes a mechanism with a target, and the target is the inner disposition of the people who have already turned away.

That description completes the sūrah's account of how refusal is produced and sustained. The signs are presented and rejected, the hearts are turned aside, and the messengers' enemies continue the work of persuasion. The sūrah states each stage plainly, and it does so while giving the Prophet the instructions that ordered his own conduct inside a city where all of it was taking place.

---

## Verse 6:113

> so that the hearts of those who disbelieve in the Hereafter may be receptive to it, be pleased with it, and be persistent in their evil pursuits

**The Purpose Clause of the Previous Verse**

So that the hearts of those who disbelieve in the Hereafter may be receptive to it. The verse states the outcome of the whispering described in the previous sentence, and it names the people in whom the whispering takes effect. The message of the enemies of the prophets reaches the people who have already denied the Hereafter, and the denial is given as the reason the message finds a place in them.

The connection between the denial of the Hereafter and the reception of deceptive speech is a theme the sūrah has been developing.

**The Three Terms of the Sentence**

The Book describes the same progression in its account of the people who did not expect to meet God. The sūrah of Yūnus says that those who do not hope for the meeting with God and who are content with the life of this world have their deeds made fair to them and are left in their confusion (10:7). The sūrah of the Ants describes those who do not believe in the Hereafter as people whose deeds have been made attractive to them and who wander blindly (27:4).

**The Receptivity the Verse Describes**

The Book's word for the heart's reception describes the opening of a container to what is poured into it, and the image appears in the sūrah of the Prophets, where the Prophet's enemies are described as listening to the message while covering their hearts so that they might not understand it (17:46). The verse in front of us states the opposite condition in the people who receive the whisperings, and the reading of the two verses together shows what is at stake.

The Book attaches the same condition to the hearts of those who reject the Hereafter in several passages. The sūrah of the Troops says that when God alone is mentioned, the hearts of those who disbelieve in the Hereafter are filled with disgust. The mention of the other gods brings them joy (39:45), and the sūrah of Fussilat states that God assigned to the deniers companions who made their conduct appear fair to them, so that they would be among those for whom the word was fulfilled (41:25). The verse in front of us states the arrangement in its most compact form.

The verse that follows turns from the enemies' deception to the Book's authority, with the Prophet commanded to say that he will not seek a judge other than God, who has revealed the Book perfectly explained (6:114). The two verses stand in deliberate contrast: the whisperings produce hearts that receive falsehood, and the clearly explained Book is what the Prophet is told to hold to.

---

## Verse 6:114

> ˹Say, O Prophet,˺ “Should I seek a judge other than Allah while He is the One Who has revealed for you the Book ˹with the truth˺ perfectly explained?” Those who were given the Scripture know that it has been revealed ˹to you˺ from your Lord in truth. So do not be one of those who doubt

**The Question Put to the Audience**

Should I seek a judge other than Allah? The sentence is a question addressed to the audience on the Prophet's behalf, and it follows a passage in which the people had been asking for signs and swallowing the whisperings of the satans among men and jinn.

The sūrah of the Table tells the Prophet to judge between the People of the Book by what God has revealed, and asks who could be a better judge for a people who are certain (5:50). The sūrah of Yūnus ends its account of the revelation with the instruction to follow what has been revealed and to be patient until God passes his judgement, since He is the best of judges (10:109).

**The Book Perfectly Explained**

While He is the One Who has revealed for you the Book with the truth perfectly explained. The second half of the question supplies the reason there is no need for another arbiter: the Book was sent down with the truth and set out in full detail. The Book uses the same description of itself in the sūrah of Fussilat, which calls it a Book whose verses are perfectly explained, a Qur'an in Arabic for people who know (41:3), and the sūrah of the Cattle itself says that God has already explained what He has forbidden (6:119).

The phrase also answers the audience's complaint about the message's content. The sūrah of Yūnus states that the Qur'an is a confirmation of what came before it and a detailed explanation of the Scripture, with no doubt in it, from the Lord of the worlds (10:37).

**The Testimony of Those Given the Scripture**

Those who were given the Scripture know that it has been revealed to you from your Lord in truth. The sentence turns from the audience's demands to the testimony of the communities that had earlier revelation. The Book records that testimony in many places: the sūrah of the Stories says that those to whom the Scripture had been given before believe in the Qur'an and declare on hearing it that this is the truth from their Lord, which they had already submitted to (28:52–53). The sūrah of the Night Journey says that those gifted with knowledge before it fall on their faces when it is recited to them. The sūrah of the Table describes the tears of those who recognise the truth; and the sūrah of the Family of Imran speaks of those among the People of the Book who believe in what has been revealed to the believers (3:199).

The Makkans lived alongside Jewish and Christian communities, and the sūrah has already appealed to the knowledge of the earlier scriptures in the argument about revelation (6:91–92). The verse in front of us states that the people who know those scriptures recognise the Qur'an's origin, which leaves the audience resisting a message that the other communities recognised.

The sūrah had already made the point at close range. It said in its earlier verses that those to whom God had given the Scripture recognise the Prophet as they recognise their own children, and that a group of them hides the truth knowingly (6:20; 2:146). The verse in front of us is the same argument applied to the origin of the Book rather than to the identity of the messenger, and it uses the witness of the same people.

The sūrah of Yūnus tells him that if he is in doubt about what has been revealed to him, he should ask those who read the Scripture before him, since the truth has come from his Lord (10:94).

The sūrah of the Cow gives the same instruction to the believers at the end of its accounts of the earlier scriptures: the truth is from your Lord, so do not be among the doubters (2:147).

---

## Verse 6:115

> The Word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice. None can change His Words. And He is the All-Hearing, All- Knowing

**The Perfected Word**

The Word of your Lord has been perfected in truth and justice. The verse states that God's word has reached its completeness, and it names the two qualities in which that completeness consists. The Book uses the same verbal form in the sūrah of Hūd, where it says that the word of the Lord will be fulfilled, and in the sūrah of Yūnus, where it says that the decree of the Lord has been proven true against the rebellious (10:33). What the verse in front of us states is the finished character of the revelation rather than its arrival at a future point.

**Truth and Justice Together**

The pair of terms describes the Book as both accurate and fair, and the Book uses the two together in other passages about its own character and about the conduct it requires. The sūrah of the Table says that God revealed the Book in truth and that it guides to a straight path (5:16 context), and the sūrah of the Troops says that God revealed the Book to the Prophet with the truth for humanity (39:41). The sūrah of the Cattle itself commands the Prophet to judge with justice in other passages.

**None Can Change His Words**

None can change His Words. The sentence states the security of the revelation, and the Book repeats it in several places. The sūrah of the Cave commands the Prophet to recite what has been revealed to him from the Book of his Lord and states that none can change His words, nor can any refuge be found besides Him (18:27). The sūrah of Yūnus declares that there is no change in the promise of God and that this is the great triumph (10:64). The sūrah of the Cattle earlier says that none can change the words of God and states that messengers before the Prophet endured rejection until God's help came (6:34).

The Book sets its own preservation against what it says happened to the earlier scriptures. The sūrah of the Table states that the People of the Book distorted the words of the Scripture and forgot a portion of what they had been reminded of (5:13). The sūrah of the Cow pronounces woe on those who write the Scripture with their own hands and then say that it is from God, seeking a fleeting gain (2:79); and the sūrah of the Women records those who take words out of their context and alter them (4:46). The sentence in this verse is the contrast to all of that.

**The Transition to the Next Verses**

The next verse says that if the Prophet were to obey most of those on earth, they would lead him away from the way of God, since they follow nothing but assumption and do nothing but lie (6:116). The verse after it states that God knows best who has strayed and who is guided (6:117). The two verses take up the question of authority from the side of numbers, and the Book answers it by pointing again to the knowledge of the One whose word has been perfected.

---

## Verse 6:116

> ˹O Prophet!˺ If you were to obey most of those on earth, they would lead you away from Allah’s Way. They follow nothing but assumptions and do nothing but lie

**The Assumption That Stands in Place of Knowledge**

They follow nothing but assumptions and do nothing but lie. The two clauses name the ground and the consequence. The Book's word for assumption describes the surmise a person inherits or inherits by habit. The sūrah of Yūnus states that most people follow nothing but inherited assumption and that assumption cannot replace the truth (10:36). The sūrah of the Star makes the same statement in the context of the claim that the angels are daughters of God (53:28); and the sūrah of the Cow describes the illiterate among the People of the Book as knowing nothing of the Scripture except lies and living on speculation (2:78).

**The Book's Use of This Argument**

The Book makes the majority's position a subject of remark in several passages, and it never treats numerical support as evidence. The sūrah of Yūsuf says that most people will not believe no matter how keen the messenger is (12:103), and it says that most of them do not believe in God without associating others with Him (12:106). The sūrah of the Cattle itself states at the end of the passage about signs that most of the audience are ignorant (6:111), and the sūrah of the Women and others describe the majority in similar terms.

The argument is not a rejection of consensus in favour of idiosyncrasy. What the Book rejects is the use of numbers as a criterion of truth, and it supports the rejection by pointing to the content of the majority's belief rather than to its size. The Makkan majority was large, established, and confident, and the verse says that its followers were following conjecture.

**What the Warning Meant in Makkah**

The warning had a practical edge for the Prophet and for the community that had joined him. The majority controlled the city's worship, its trade, and its kinship structures, and a person who accepted the message faced the loss of all three. The verse tells the Prophet that the majority's way is not a path to be accommodated, and its statement that the majority's followers do nothing but lie gives the reason.

The Book's accounts of the earlier messengers show the same situation. The sūrah of Hūd describes Noah's people rejecting him on the ground that only the lowest among them had followed him, and the Book records communities that measured a claim by the standing of the people who made it rather than by what was said.

**The Verse's Relation to the One Before It**

The verse follows the statement that God's word has been perfected in truth and justice and that none can change His words (6:115). The pair establishes the standard by which claims are measured: the perfected word on one side, and the conjecture of the many on the other. The sūrah has already told the Prophet to seek no judge besides God, and this verse removes the most obvious alternative, which is the opinion of the people around him.

The verse that follows answers the warning by stating that the Prophet's Lord knows best who has strayed from His way and who is rightly guided (6:117). The two verses together leave the question of standing where the sūrah has consistently placed it, in the knowledge of God rather than in the counting of heads.

---

## Verse 6:117

> Indeed, your Lord knows best who has strayed from His Way and who is ˹rightly˺ guided

**The Formula and Where It Appears**

Indeed, your Lord knows best who has strayed from His Way and who is rightly guided. The sentence closes the warning about the majority with a statement about knowledge, and the Book repeats it almost word for word in other places. The sūrah of the Pen uses it to answer those who accused the Prophet of being a madman, saying that his Lord knows best who has strayed from His way and who is rightly guided (68:7). The sūrah of the Star uses the same words at the end of its discussion of the people who followed assumption (53:30). And the sūrah of the Bee attaches the sentence to the command to invite people to the way of the Lord with wisdom and good instruction (16:125).

The repetition tells the reader how the Book wants the division between belief and disbelief treated. Both passages in which the formula appears most fully are answers to disputes in which the audience was claiming to know who was on the right road, and the Book's reply moves the question to the knowledge of God.

**The Name of the Way**

The verse speaks of God's Way, and the sūrah has used the same expression in the sentence before it, warning the Prophet that the majority would lead him away from the Way of God (6:116). The Book's term describes the road rather than the destination, and its usage throughout this chapter has been developed through the imagery of paths and travellers. The straight path is described at the end of the sūrah's long passage of prohibitions (6:153), and the sūrah of the Bee's version of this formula follows a command about inviting people to the Way of the Lord.

The same noun serves both verse halves here: there is one Way, and people either follow it or wander from it. The verse does not divide humanity into categories of temperament or standing; it describes them in relation to a road, and the road is the one the revelation marks out.

**What the Verse Meant for the Prophet**

The Prophet lived among people who had committed themselves to denying his message, and the formula was given to him as a form of relief. He was not required to determine the standing of each person he met, and he was not required to secure the outcome of the dispute. The sūrah had already told him that he was neither keeper nor maintainer over the people who denied his message (6:107) and that the matter would be settled on the day when God informs them of what they did.

**The Knowledge Claim in the Sūrah's Argument**

The claim that God knows the two groups is continuous with the sūrah's account of His knowledge in other passages. The sūrah stated that God knows the secret and the open, that not a leaf falls without His knowledge, and that He is the one who has the keys of the unseen (6:59; 6:60). The formula in this verse applies that knowledge to the question of guidance, and it does so in the context of the warning that the majority would lead the Prophet away from the Way.

---

## Verse 6:118

> So eat only of what is slaughtered in Allah’s Name if you truly believe in His revelations

**The Condition Attached to the Command**

The verse makes the dietary command a test of belief, and the Book attaches similar conditions to other commands. The sūrah of the Cow tells the believers to eat of the good things provided and to give thanks, and it makes the instruction part of the covenant that the community has accepted.

The sūrah has already stated that the people who follow assumption do nothing but lie (6:116), and this verse makes the practical consequence of that statement plain for the table.

**The Argument in the Audience's Practice**

The sūrah's later verses describe the audience's own rules about livestock in detail: the animals they set apart, the ones they forbade to certain people, and the shares they assigned to God and to their idols (6:136–139; 6:143–144). The command in front of us stands at the head of that whole section, and it states the principle that the section applies. What the audience did with their herds and flocks was governed by claims about what God had permitted and forbidden, and those claims were inherited rather than revealed.

The Book's account of the audience's system names some of its details: the animals whose backs were declared off limits, the livestock that no one was to eat, and the shares assigned to God and to the idols (6:136; 6:139). The sūrah sets those practices beside the command to eat of what the Name of God has been mentioned over, and the contrast between the two bodies of rules is the argument the section is making.

The Book's treatment of those claims follows the pattern the sūrah has established. It states what God has actually forbidden, it exposes the invention that has grown up around it, and it leaves the responsibility for the invention with the people who made it. The verse in front of us supplies the positive rule for food, and the passages that follow describe the inventions the rule replaced.

**What the Verse Says About Gratitude and Law**

The instruction to eat of what the Name has been mentioned over connects the meal to the acknowledgement of the One who provided it. The Book's dietary rules are not concerned with purity in the abstract; they concern the recognition of God's authority over what a person consumes, and the invocation at slaughter is the moment at which that recognition is expressed. The sūrah of the Bee states the same connection when it tells the believers to eat of what God has provided and to be grateful for His favours (16:114).

The Book's language about food elsewhere attaches the act of eating to worship, and the verse in this sūrah belongs to that usage. The command is addressed to a community forming around the revelation, and the rule tells it how the most ordinary act of the day is related to its confession.

**The Verses That Continue the Section**

The verse that follows asks why the believers should not eat of what has been slaughtered in God's Name when God has already explained what He has forbidden, with the exception of what compulsion requires (6:119). The two verses state the rule and then answer the objection to it, and the answer includes the observation that many people mislead others by their desires out of ignorance.

---

## Verse 6:119

> Why should you not eat of what is slaughtered in Allah’s Name when He has already explained to you what He has forbidden to you—except when compelled by necessity? Many ˹deviants˺ certainly mislead others by their whims out of ignorance. Surely your Lord knows the transgressors best

**The Explanation Already Given**

When He has already explained to you what He has forbidden. The sūrah refers to a body of rulings that the audience had received, and the commentators discussed whether the reference is to the detailed dietary laws given later in the sūrah of the Table or to the principle stated in this very passage. The Book's own statement in the sūrah of the Cattle is that God has explained what He has forbidden (6:119), and the sūrah goes on in later verses to list the prohibitions about dead animals, blood, and swine.

What matters for the argument is that the explanation exists and that the community has access to it. The Book's position throughout this section is that the rules come from God rather than from the customs of a region, and the verse states that the rulings have already been set out.

**The People Who Mislead by Their Desires**

The Book's description of the misleaders repeats the terms it has used throughout this section. The sūrah has described the audience as following assumption and lying (6:116), and the verse before us states the same condition in the vocabulary of desire. What changes from passage to passage in this sūrah is the subject under discussion, and the characterization of the people who argue against the revelation remains constant.

**The Closing Statement About Knowledge**

Surely your Lord knows the transgressors best. The verse closes with the same kind of statement that ended the previous passage about the majority, where the sūrah said that the Lord knows best who has strayed and who is guided (6:117). Here the object of the knowledge is the transgressors, and the sentence leaves their identification to God rather than to the community.

The next verse commands the community to shun open and secret sin and states that those who commit sin will be punished for what they earn (6:120). The command follows naturally from the dietary instruction, since the sūrah has just been describing conduct that rests on the desires of those who mislead, and the new sentence broadens the subject from food to the whole of a person's conduct.

---

## Verse 6:120

> Shun all sin—open and secret. Indeed, those who commit sin will be punished for what they earn

**The Command to Shun Sin**

Shun all sin, open and secret. The verse moves from the specific dietary rule to a general command, and it covers the two ways in which a person's wrong conduct can be conducted. The open sin is visible to others and is judged by them; the secret sin is hidden from the community, and the Book states that both are equally subject to the command.

The sūrah's treatment of the audience's conduct throughout the chapter has dealt with the hidden as well as the visible. It said that God knows what they conceal and what they disclose, and it described the whispering of the satans among humans and jinn as a hidden activity (6:112). The command in this verse addresses the whole field in which those activities occur. The commentators explained the open sin as the wrong that is done in public and the secret sin as what a person hides in his own conduct, and some applied the pair to the acts of the limbs and the intentions of the heart. The distinction appears in the Book's other passages about conduct, and the Book's word for the hidden covers both the concealment of an act and the inner intention behind it.

**The Statement of Punishment**

Indeed, those who commit sin will be punished for what they earn. The second sentence supplies the consequence and states it in the Book's vocabulary of earning. The Book frequently describes reward and punishment as the produce of what a person has collected for himself, and the same language is used in the sūrah's accounts of the day of judgment, when each person is confronted with what he has sent ahead.

The sūrah has described the audience's reckoning in several earlier passages: God knows what they do, their deeds will be recorded, and they will be informed of what they used to do (6:60; 6:108). The sentence in this verse states the same fact in the sin itself, and it applies it to the open and the hidden alike.

**What the Verse Says About the Concealed Act**

The sūrah has stated the same fact about knowledge in the passage on the keys of the unseen, where God knows the secret and the disclosure, the leaf that falls, and the grain in the darkness (6:59; 6:60). The command in this verse rests on that knowledge, and the punishment it states is the consequence that knowledge produces.

**The Transition to the Following Verse**

The next verse returns to food and forbids what has not had the Name of God mentioned over it, describing that as disobedience and connecting it to the whisperings of the devils and their associates (6:121). The shift back from general conduct to diet shows that the sūrah is treating the dietary system of the audience as one item in the register of conduct the Book is correcting.

---

## Verse 6:121

> Do not eat of what is not slaughtered in Allah’s Name. For that would certainly be ˹an act of˺ disobedience. Surely the devils whisper to their ˹human˺ associates to argue with you. If you were to obey them, then you ˹too˺ would be polytheists

**The Prohibition Stated in the Negative**

Do not eat of what is not slaughtered in Allah's Name. The verse states the prohibition that corresponds to the command given a few verses earlier, and the Book uses the same form in the sūrah of the Table, where the list of forbidden foods includes what has been dedicated to other than God. The audience's herds produced both kinds of meat, and the rule tells the community which of them belongs to the food of the believers.

The commentators connected the rule to the practice of the Makkans, who ate animals that had died of themselves and animals over which the names of their gods had been invoked. The verse's prohibition covers both, and the reason given in the same sentence is that such food is disobedience.

**The Meaning of the Charge of Disobedience**

The Book's usage attaches the term to the person who oversteps in a matter where God has given a rule, and the eating of what has not been consecrated is described in those terms because the Name was withheld deliberately. Where the invocation was not made, the person who killed the animal had chosen to omit it, and the food is the sign of that choice.

**The Whisperings of the Devils**

Surely the devils whisper to their associates to argue with you. The verse names the source of the arguments the community was facing, and the sūrah has described the same activity a few verses earlier, when it said that God made for every prophet enemies among devilish humans and jinn who whisper to one another with elegant words of deception (6:112). The whisperings in the two passages are the same activity directed at the same object.

The Book's account of the devils' associates appears elsewhere in its descriptions of the people who follow the promptings against the message. The sūrah of the Ornament describes God's placing a devil as a companion to the person who turns away from the Reminder of the Most Compassionate (43:36). The sūrah of the Poets records the Book's denial that the devils brought the revelation down and its description of the ones on whom they actually descend (26:210; 26:221). The argument that the audience raised about food is presented here as something the devils inspired, and the association gives the dispute its place in the sūrah's larger account of how denial spreads.

The passage that follows this verse turns to the details of the audience's system: the livestock they dedicated, the animals they set apart, the rules about who might eat what, and the shares assigned to God and to the idols (6:136–139; 6:143–144). The verse in front of us states the principle by which all of that is judged, and the sections that follow apply it.

---

## Verse 6:122

> Can those who had been dead, to whom We gave life and a light with which they can walk among people, be compared to those in complete darkness from which they can never emerge? That is how the misdeeds of the disbelievers have been made appealing to them

**The Parable of the Dead and the Living**

Can those who had been dead, to whom We gave life and a light with which they can walk among people, be compared to those in complete darkness from which they can never emerge? The verse states a comparison and leaves the answer to the reader, and the images it uses are the sūrah's own vocabulary from its earlier verses. The person who was dead and received life is the one who heard the message and responded; the light he walks by is the guidance; and the person in the darkness is the one who has been left in the condition described in the passage about the turning of the hearts.

The Book uses the same language about life and light in several places. The sūrah of Fāṭir says that the dead and the living are not equal and that God makes whom He wills hear (35:22). The sūrah of the Cow describes God as the guardian of the believers, bringing them out of darkness into light. The disbelievers have their guardians in false gods who lead them from light into darkness (2:257); and the sūrah of the Cattle itself uses the phrase about walking among people in the present verse.

**The Light and the Darkness**

The two images are the Book's standard figures for guidance and its absence. To walk among people by a light is to move through the world with a course set and a destination known, and the Book contrasts that condition with the man who gropes in a darkness from which he cannot emerge. The sūrah of the Cattle earlier described the person whom the satans have seduced in the open country, calling to others who call him back to guidance he does not follow (6:71), which is the same condition stated by a different image.

The Book's use of light for revelation appears throughout its descriptions of the earlier scriptures as well. The sūrah of the Table describes the Torah as containing guidance and light (5:44), and the sūrah of the Cattle will later describe the Book given to Moses as a light and guidance for people (6:91). The verse in front of us treats the effect of that guidance on a person's conduct, which is the light by which he walks.

**The Deeds Made Appealing**

That is how the misdeeds of the disbelievers have been made appealing to them. The sentence explains the condition of the second figure in the comparison, and it uses the same vocabulary as the earlier passages about the enemies of the prophets and the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter (6:112–113). The Book's account of the made-appealing of evil conduct appears at several points in this chapter and outside it, and it is always attached to the people who have chosen their course.

---

## Verse 6:123

> And so We have placed in every society the most wicked to conspire in it. Yet they plot only against themselves, but they fail to perceive it

**The Wicked Placed in Every Society**

And so We have placed in every society the most wicked to conspire in it. The verse states a fact about the societies to which the message came, and it does so as a general pattern rather than as a description of Makkah alone. Every community has had its leaders in opposition, and the Book connects the statement to the earlier verses about the enemies appointed to every prophet (6:112).

The Book's account of the opponents in other sūrahs identifies them with the same word the translation renders as the elite. The sūrah of Saba' says that whenever a warner was sent to a society, its elite said that they rejected what he had been sent with (34:34). The sūrah of the Ornament records the same response, with the elite saying that they had found their forefathers following a way and would follow in their footsteps (43:23). The sūrah's audience, which took its direction from the leading families of the city, is being shown the pattern of that leadership.

**What the Conspiracy Consists In**

The verse describes the activity of these people as conspiring or plotting, and the Book uses the term for the schemes by which the messengers were resisted. The sūrah of the Prophets records the plan that Abraham's people made against him and the response that made them the worst losers (21:70), and the sūrah of the Cattle has recorded several of the audience's own schemes in its accounts of their demands and objections.

The Book treats the plotting as the work of the people it names, and it does not attribute it to the whole of a society.

**They Plot Only Against Themselves**

Yet they plot only against themselves, but they fail to perceive it. The sentence states the outcome of the plotting, and the Book's claim is that the harm returns to the people who plan it. The sūrah of Fāṭir states that evil plotting only backfires on those who plot. It attaches the conduct to the people who behaved arrogantly in the land (35:43). The sūrah of the Ants records the plan made by a community against the messenger and says that God made a plan while they were unaware (27:50). And the sūrah of the Family of Imran tells the believers that if they are patient and mindful of God, the schemes of their enemies will not harm them (3:120).

**The Failure to Perceive**

The closing clause adds the reason the schemers continued: they did not perceive what they were doing to themselves. The Book attaches the same inability to the people whose conduct it has been describing. The sūrah has said that they follow assumption and lie (6:116), that their conduct has been made appealing to them (6:122), and that their hearts were turned away after their first refusal (6:110). The verse in front of us states the practical consequence of that condition in the field of action.

The inability to perceive is not presented as a lack of intelligence, since the leaders of the Quraysh were capable and experienced. What the Book describes is a blindness about the direction of their own conduct, and it states that the blindness is the product of the choices the people had made.

---

## Verse 6:124

> Whenever a sign comes to them, they say, “We will never believe until we receive what Allah’s messengers received.” Allah knows best where to place His message. The wicked will soon be overwhelmed by humiliation from Allah and a severe punishment for their evil plots

**The Demand Made at Every Sign**

Whenever a sign comes to them, they say, We will never believe until we receive what Allah's messengers received. The verse records the demand of the elite described in the previous verse, and the demand is not for evidence but for standing. What the messengers received was the message itself and the authority that accompanied it, and the audience's position was that they would accept revelation only if it came down to them personally.

The Book records the same disposition in other places. The sūrah of the Night Journey records the demand of the audience that the Prophet bring down a book they could read (17:93). The sūrah of the Family of Imran records the claim of those who said that God had commanded them not to believe in any messenger unless he brought an offering consumed by fire from the sky (3:183). The verse in front of us states the demand in its clearest form, since the audience wanted the office rather than the proof.

**The Answer Given to the Demand**

Allah knows best where to place His message. The sentence answers the demand by referring the matter of appointment to the One who makes it, and the Book states the same principle elsewhere in its accounts of the prophets. The sūrah of the Pilgrimage says that God selects messengers from both angels and people, for He is All-Hearing and All-Seeing (22:75), and the sūrah of the Cow records God's testing of Abraham and His promise to make him a leader for the people (2:124).

**The Humiliation and the Punishment**

The wicked will soon be overwhelmed by humiliation from Allah and a severe punishment for their evil plots. The verse closes by stating what the demand will produce, and the terms it uses are the ones the sūrah has applied to the opponents of the message throughout. Humiliation is the condition of the people who set themselves against the message, and the punishment is attached to the plotting described in the previous verse.

The Book's statements about the outcome of the plotters appear in several passages. The sūrah of Fāṭir states that evil plotting only backfires on those who plot (35:43). The sūrah of Tā-Hā records the magicians falling in prostration and believing after the sign was shown to them (20:70); and the sūrah of the Cattle has already described the wrongdoers in the throes of death and the torment that follows (6:93). The sentence in this verse attaches the same outcome to the demand for the messenger's office. The next verse states the arrangement by which the two responses are decided: whoever God wills to guide, He opens his heart to Islam, and whoever He wills to leave astray, He makes his chest tight and constricted as though he were climbing into the sky (6:125).

---

## Verse 6:125

> Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens their heart to Islam. But whoever He wills to leave astray, He makes their chest tight and constricted as if they were climbing up into the sky. This is how Allah dooms those who disbelieve

**The Opening of the Heart**

Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens their heart to Islam. The verse states the arrangement by which guidance occurs, and it does so in the physical language the sūrah has used before. The heart that is opened receives what is presented to it, and the same image appeared in the previous passage about the hearts of those who disbelieve in the Hereafter being receptive to the whisperings of the devils (6:113). The two verses describe the same faculty receiving two different things.

The Book uses the opening of the heart for the effect of the message on believers in other places. The sūrah of the Troops asks whether the person whose heart God has opened to Islam, so that he walks in light from his Lord, is like the person whose heart is hardened (39:22), and the sūrah of the Cattle earlier described the light by which the believer walks among people (6:122). The verse in front of us states the divine act that produces that condition.

**The Constricted Chest**

The Book uses the image of the chest in its accounts of the Prophet's own experience as well, and the sūrah of Tā-Hā records his request that his chest be expanded for the task given to him (20:25).

**The Two Conditions and the Sūrah's Argument**

The verse completes the answer to the demand recorded in the previous verse, in which the elite said they would not believe until they received what God's messengers received. The answer given here is that belief is a matter of the heart's condition rather than of the messenger's standing, and the persons who make the demand are described as unable to breathe in the presence of the message.

The sūrah's account of the two conditions has run from the beginning of the chapter. It described the sealing of the hearts of those who denied the signs (6:46), the turning of the hearts and eyes after the first refusal (6:110), and the receptivity of the hearts of those who denied the Hereafter to the whisperings of the devils (6:113). The verse in front of us states the principle that governs all of those passages.

**What the Verse Says About the Prophet's Task**

The verse relieves the Prophet of a burden that the sūrah has been addressing throughout the passage. He had been told to follow what was revealed, to turn away from the polytheists, and that he was neither keeper nor maintainer over the people (6:106–107); now he is told that the opening of the heart is God's act. The messenger delivers; the response is produced by a cause beyond his control.

The Book states the same arrangement in the sūrah of the Stories, where the Prophet is told that he cannot guide whom he loves but that God guides whom He wills (28:56). The two verses together define the limit of the prophetic office, and the sūrah's later instructions to the Prophet rest on that limit.

---

## Verse 6:126

> That is your Lord’s Path—perfectly straight. We have already made the signs clear to those who are mindful

**The Path Named**

That is your Lord's Path, perfectly straight. The verse identifies the way that the sūrah has been describing. The demonstrative reaches back through the passage about the opening of the heart and the constriction of the chest, and it gathers the whole account into a single noun: the path of the Lord. What the sūrah has been offering the audience is not a set of arguments alone but a road, and the sentence names it.

The sūrah of the Cattle itself returns to the image at the end of its long passage of prohibitions, where God tells the Prophet that this is His path, straight, and commands the community to follow it while warning against the paths that branch off and separate those who take them (6:153).

**The Straightness of the Path**

The Book's use of the image in the opening sūrah shows how central it is to everything the revelation asks. The prayer for guidance upon the straight path stands at the head of the Book (1:6). The sūrah of the Family of Imran closes the declaration attributed to Jesus with the same phrase, telling its hearers that God is his Lord and theirs, that they should worship Him, and that this is the straight path (3:51).

The description of the path as straight states its function: it leads to the destination without deviation. The sūrah of the Family of Imran calls the message itself a way that is straight, and the sūrah of the Consultation states that God guides whom He wills to a straight path and that the revelation is a light by which He guides His servants (42:52). The Book's repeated use of the image shows that its concern is with the direction in which a person is travelling rather than with the opinions he holds in isolation.

For the Makkan audience, the claim had a specific force. Their city was a place of pilgrimage roads, and the metaphor of the travellers' routes was part of ordinary life. The Book tells them that a single road leads to the Lord, and that the practices they inherited had taken them off it. The sentence names the road and leaves the choice of travelling it with the hearer.

**The Signs Made Clear for the Mindful**

We have already made the signs clear to those who are mindful. The clause closes the passage with the fourth and last form of the formula the sūrah has used after its accounts of signs. The stars and the darkness were made clear for people who know (6:97); the sign of the single soul was for people who understand (6:98); the harvest was for people who believe (6:99); and the path described here is for people who are mindful.

The progression moves from the faculty of knowledge to the action of remembering, and the sūrah's vocabulary marks the difference between receiving information and holding it present. The Book's commands to remember occur throughout its addresses to the community, and the sūrah of the Cow attaches the guidance of the Book to those who are mindful of God.

---

## Verse 6:127

> They will have the Home of Peace with their Lord, Who will be their Guardian because of what they used to do

**The Home of Peace**
