They will have the Home of Peace with their Lord. The verse names the destination of the road described in the sentence before it, and the name it gives the destination is the Book's own. The sūrah of Yūnus states that God invites all people to the Home of Peace and guides whoever He wills to the straight path (10:25). The sūrah of the Troops describes the arrival of the mindful at the gates of the garden, where the keepers greet them with the word of peace and tell them that they have been made pure (39:73).

The pairing of the home's name with the word the Book uses for peace is deliberate. The Book's peace is the condition in which a person is free of fear and loss, and the sūrah of the Cow attaches that condition to those who follow the guidance, saying that they will have no fear and no grief (2:62). The home named in this verse is the place where the condition is complete, and the sūrah places it at the end of the road it has just described.

**The Guardian**

Who will be their Guardian because of what they used to do. The Book's term for the guardian describes the one who takes charge of another's affairs. The sūrah of the Cow uses the same word for God at the end of its passage on light and darkness, saying that God is the Guardian of the believers who brings them out of darkness into light (2:257). The sūrah of the Kneeling states that God is the patron of the righteous while the wrongdoers are patrons of one another (45:19).

**Because of What They Used to Do**

The clause attaches the reward to the conduct of the people who receive it, and the Book uses the same formula at the end of many of its promises. The sūrah of the Bowing states that no soul can imagine the delights kept in store for the believers as a reward for what they used to do (32:17). The sūrah of the Prophets says that those for whom the finest reward has been destined will be kept far away from the fire (21:101). The formula is the Book's way of stating that the reward corresponds to the conduct it follows.

The Book's teaching on the relationship between deeds and reward appears throughout its addresses to the community. Its statements about forgiveness and grace stand alongside its statements about recompense, and the two belong together in a scheme in which the believers are told that their efforts are not disregarded and that what they send ahead will be found with God (73:20). The verse in front of us states the principle in its compact form, and the sūrah's later passages apply it to the reckoning of the communities. The next verse records the day when God will gather the beings together and address the jinn as the ones who misled great numbers of human beings, and the human associates will reply that they benefited from one another and have now reached the term appointed for them (6:128).

---

## Verse 6:128

> ˹Consider˺ the Day He will gather them ˹all˺ together and say, “O assembly of jinn! You misled humans in great numbers.” And their human associates will say, “Our Lord! We benefited from each other’s company, but now we have reached the term which You appointed for us.” ˹Then˺ He will say, “The Fire is your home, yours to stay in forever, except whoever Allah wills to spare.” Surely your Lord is All-Wise, All-Knowing

**The Gathering and the Address**

Consider the Day He will gather them all together and say, O assembly of jinn! You misled humans in great numbers. The verse opens one of the sūrah's accounts of the resurrection, and it begins with an address to the jinn as a body. The Book's word for the gathering of a whole order of beings at once is used elsewhere for the assembly of the communities. The sūrah of the Cattle records a comparable address a few verses later, when both the jinn and human beings are asked whether messengers did not come to them from among themselves (6:130).

The Book's accounts of the partnerships between the jinn and human beings appear in several places. The sūrah of the Jinn records that some men used to seek refuge with some jinn. That they increased one another in wickedness (72:6); the sūrah of Saba' records the disavowal of the jinn on the day of judgment, when they declare that it was the people who followed them and not the reverse (34:41). And the sūrah of the Heights describes the entry of the evil groups of jinn and humans into the fire together (7:38). The address in this verse belongs to that family of passages.

**The Admission of the Human Associates**

And their human associates will say, Our Lord! We benefited from each other's company. The reply admits the relationship and describes it as a mutual advantage. The Book's statements elsewhere fill in what the advantage consisted of: the humans received the jinn's services in fortune-telling, protection, and ritual instruction, and the jinn gained followers and influence among the people. The sūrah has already described the association of the jinn with God by the audience (6:100), and the sentence in front of us gives the human side of the arrangement.

The Book treats the partnership as a real one rather than as a metaphor, and it records the jinn's own report of it in the sūrah that bears their name.

**The Term Appointed**

But now we have reached the term which You appointed for us. The reply ends with the acknowledgement that the arrangement has run out. The Book's word for the term is the one it uses for the fixed span of every community and person, and the sūrah of the Cattle has already described God's holding of the souls and His revival of them by day within the span He has set (6:60). The sūrah of the Heights and the sūrah of Yūnus state that every community has its term which cannot be advanced or delayed (7:34; 10:49).

**The Sentence Passed**

Then He will say, The Fire is your home, yours to stay in forever, except whoever Allah wills to spare. The sentence states the outcome of the partnership, and it includes an exception clause. The Book uses the same form in the sūrah of Hūd, where the duration of the punishment is described as lasting while the heavens and the earth endure, except what the Lord wills (11:107), and the exception is understood in the light of the Book's statement that God does what He intends.

---

## Verse 6:129

> This is how We make the wrongdoers ˹destructive˺ allies of one another because of their misdeeds

**The Alliance of the Wrongdoers**

This is how We make the wrongdoers allies of one another because of their misdeeds. The verse states a general principle after the account of the partnership between the jinn and their human associates, and it describes the companionship of the wicked as something God brings about.

The sūrah of the Kneeling uses the pair of terms in a single sentence, saying that the wrongdoers are patrons of one another while God is the patron of the righteous (45:19). The verse in front of us states the first half of that contrast as a fact of the present order.

**The Book's Descriptions of the Wicked Company**

The Book returns to the company of the wicked in its accounts of the day of judgment, and it describes the company dissolving there. The sūrah of the Ornament states that close friends will be enemies to one another on that day except the righteous (43:67), and the sūrah of the Heights describes the groups of jinn and humans entering the fire together and cursing one another as they follow one another in. The sūrah of the Cattle has already described the partners in deception arriving at their term and being told that the fire is their home (6:128).

**What the Verse Says About the Strength of the Wicked**

The sentence describes an alliance that is real but ruinous, and the translation's word for it carries the sense of mutual destruction. The Book's accounts of the powerful in the world do not deny their power; the sūrah of Saba' and the sūrah of the Ornament describe the elites of the communities as established and confident. What the Book denies is that their alliance can secure them against the judgement that follows.

The sūrah has made the point in its passages about the plotters who did not perceive that they plotted against themselves (6:123) and about the elite who demanded the messenger's office (6:124). The verse in front of us states the same fact about the alliances those people formed, and it describes the mutual support as a mutual harm that they did not see.

**The Contrast With the Community of the Believers**

The Book sets the mutual support of the wrongdoers against another kind of company, and the contrast shows what the verse is describing. The sūrah of the Chambers states that the believers are but one brotherhood and commands them to make peace among themselves (49:10); the sūrah of the Family of Imran tells the community to hold firmly to the rope of God and not to be divided. It recalls the favour by which their hearts were united after enmity (3:103); and the sūrah of the Spoils says that God brought the hearts of the believers together when no amount of wealth could have done it (8:63).

The difference between the two companies lies in what holds them together. The alliance described in this verse rests on misdeeds and dissolves when its usefulness ends, while the brotherhood described in the other passages rests on a shared response to the message and is described as a gift from God. The sūrah's audience knew both kinds of bond, since the message had begun to create a community out of the tribes of a city organised around rivalry.

---

## Verse 6:130

> ˹Allah will ask,˺ “O assembly of jinn and humans! Did messengers not come from among you, proclaiming My revelations and warning you of the coming of this Day of yours?” They will say, “We confess against ourselves!” For they have been deluded by ˹their˺ worldly life. And they will testify against themselves that they were disbelievers

**The Address to Both Assemblies**

O assembly of jinn and humans! Did messengers not come from among you, proclaiming My revelations and warning you of the coming of this Day of yours? The verse records the address given to the assembled orders of created beings, and its form is a question that contains its own answer. The Book's word for the assembly is the one used in the previous address to the jinn (6:128), and the two sentences together frame the scene: the partners are gathered, and both are asked about the warning they received.

The Book states the universality of the warning in several places. The sūrah of Fāṭir says that the Prophet was sent with the truth as a deliverer of good news and a warner and that there is no community which has not had a warner (35:24). The sūrah of the Bee says that God has sent a messenger to every community with the command to worship God and shun false gods (16:36). And the sūrah of the Women says that messengers were sent with good news and warnings so that humanity should have no excuse before God after the coming of the messengers (4:165).

The Book's messengers are drawn from the communities they address, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God favoured the believers by raising a messenger from among them (3:164).

**The Confession**

They will say, We confess against ourselves! The answer admits the truth of the accusation without argument, and the Book records similar admissions at other points in its accounts of the judgement. The sūrah of the Heights describes the cry of the people overwhelmed by the punishment, saying that they had been wrongdoers (7:5). The sūrah of the Kingdom says that they will confess their sins and that the residents of the blaze are far from mercy (67:11). And the sūrah of the Prophets records the declaration of those who had taken lords besides God, freeing God of any partnership and confessing that they had been the ones at fault (25:18).

The sūrah of the Cattle has already described an audience that would deny everything at the resurrection, with their only argument being an oath that they were never polytheists (6:23). The verse in front of us supplies the other response, which is the confession that the sūrah's account of the judgement requires for its justice to be visible.

**The Testimony Against Themselves**

And they will testify against themselves that they were disbelievers. The verse closes with the completion of the admission, and the Book presents the record of a person's conduct as testimony that he gives against himself. The sūrah of the Night Journey states that every human being's destiny is bound to his neck and that a record will be brought out for him on the day of judgment, with the command to read it and the statement that the person is enough as an accountant against himself (17:13–14).

The sūrah has described the recording of deeds in its earlier passages, where the angels write down what the people do (6:61), and the verse in front of us shows the record being used. The testimony is not extracted by force; it is given by the people whose deeds it contains.

---

## Verse 6:131

> This ˹sending of the messengers˺ is because your Lord would never destroy a society for their wrongdoing while its people are unaware ˹of the truth˺

**The Reason for the Sending of the Messengers**

This is because your Lord would never destroy a society for their wrongdoing while its people are unaware of the truth. The verse states the principle that stands behind the address to the assembled jinn and humans, and it explains why the warning was delivered before the reckoning. The sending of the messengers is presented as the arrangement by which the judgement is made just, and the verse attributes the arrangement to the Lord's dealings with the societies He has addressed.

The Book states the same principle in several places. The sūrah of the Night Journey says that God would never punish a people until He has sent a messenger to warn them (17:15), the sūrah of the Stories says that the Lord would never destroy a society until He has sent a messenger to its capital reciting His revelations (28:59). The sūrah of the Poets declares that God has never destroyed a society without warners (26:208). The verse in front of us gives the same assurance in the context of the reckoning, where the confession of the assembled people has just been recorded.

**What the Verse Says About Ignorance**

The Book's word for the unawareness of the people describes the condition of those who have not been reminded, and the sūrah has used it for the people who turn away from the signs. The verse's point is that the warning removes the condition, and the Book's messengers are sent precisely so that the people who receive them can no longer plead that they did not know.

The Book's statements about responsibility elsewhere rest on the same arrangement. The sūrah of the Women says that messengers were sent with good news and warnings so that humanity should have no excuse before God after their coming (4:165), and the sūrah of Fāṭir states that there is no community without a warner (35:24). The verse in front of us states the purpose those verses describe, and it states it as a feature of the Lord's justice rather than as a record of the particular missions. The verse follows the account of the gathered assemblies and their confession, and it precedes the statement that each person will be assigned ranks according to what he has done (6:132). The three verses together present the judgement as a sequence: the warning, the acknowledgement, and the assignment of ranks.

The next verse states that each person will be assigned ranks according to his deeds and that the Lord is not unaware of what they do (6:132). The verse completes the account of the reckoning with the statement of the degrees that distinguish one person's outcome from another, and it attaches the knowledge of God to the assignment.

The passage that follows tells the Prophet that his Lord is the Self-Sufficient, Full of Mercy, and able to replace a people with another if He wills (6:133). The warning, the ranks, and the sufficiency of God together give the sūrah's audience the full measure of their position, and the verse in front of us supplies the assurance that the reckoning will be conducted on the basis of what was delivered.

---

## Verse 6:132

> They will each be assigned ranks according to their deeds. And your Lord is not unaware of what they do

**The Assignment of Ranks**

They will each be assigned ranks according to their deeds. The verse states the outcome of the reckoning for the assembled people, and it does so in the vocabulary of ranks or degrees. The Book uses the same term in its other accounts of the Hereafter: the sūrah of the Sand Dunes says that each of the two groups will be ranked according to what they have done so that God may fully reward all of them and that none will be wronged (46:19). The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that they each have varying degrees in the sight of God and that God sees what they do (3:163); and the sūrah of the Repentance places those who believed, emigrated, and strove with their wealth and their lives in greater rank before God (9:20).

The Book's teaching on ranks appears in its treatment of the present life as well. The last verse of this sūrah states that God has placed human beings as successors on earth and elevated some of them in rank above others as a test (6:165). The sūrah of the Ornament says that God has distributed among people their livelihood in the present life and raised some above others in degrees. What the verse in front of us states is the arrangement of the Hereafter, where the ranks correspond to deeds rather than to the circumstances a person was given.

**The Knowledge Attached to the Sentence**

And your Lord is not unaware of what they do. The clause states the ground on which the ranks are assigned, and the Book uses the same formula at the end of many of its passages about the deeds of the people it addresses. The sūrah of the Cow uses it after describing the hardness of hearts and the corruption of the Scripture (2:74; 2:85), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran attaches it to its statement about the varying degrees.

The formula is the Book's way of closing an account of human conduct without leaving the impression that the conduct goes unobserved. The sūrah has described God's knowledge of the leaf that falls and the grain in the darkness (6:59), and the sentence in this verse applies that knowledge to what people do, which is the point on which the assignment of ranks depends.

**The Verse's Place in the Passage**

The verse follows the address to the assembled jinn and humans and their confession, and it precedes the statement that the Lord is the Self-Sufficient, Full of Mercy, able to replace a people with another (6:133). The three verses give the reckoning its context: the warning that makes it just, the ranks that make it exact, and the sufficiency of God that makes it independent of the people who are judged.

The sūrah's later verses continue with the promise that what the audience has been warned about will come to pass and that they will have no escape (6:134), and then with the instruction to the Prophet to tell his people to persist in their ways while he persists in his (6:135). The verse in front of us supplies the measure for the reckoning those passages announce.

---

## Verse 6:133

> Your Lord is the Self-Sufficient, Full of Mercy. If He wills, He can do away with you and replace you with whoever He wills, just as He produced you from the offspring of other people

**The Self-Sufficient Lord**

Your Lord is the Self-Sufficient, Full of Mercy. The verse states two attributes together, and the pairing carries the argument of the passage. The Self-Sufficient describes a being who has no need of anything He has made, and the sūrah of Fāṭir addresses humanity with the same contrast, telling them that they are the ones who stand in need of God while He is the Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy (35:15). The sūrah of Luqmān states that whatever is in the heavens and the earth belongs to God and that He is truly the Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy (31:26).

The two attributes appear together in the Book's treatment of human ingratitude and divine patience. The sūrah of Ibrāhīm records Moses saying that if the people and everyone on earth were ungrateful, God would still be Self-Sufficient and Praiseworthy (14:8), and the sūrah of the Cow states that kind words and forgiveness are better than charity followed by injury, since God is Self-Sufficient, Most Forbearing (2:263). The verse in front of us names the same independence and joins it to mercy, and the combination is what makes the sentence that follows intelligible.

**The Power to Replace a People**

If He wills, He can do away with you and replace you with whoever He wills. The sentence states the consequence of God's self-sufficiency for a community that rejects His message. The Book makes the same statement in several places: the sūrah of the Women says that if it is God's will He can remove humanity altogether and replace them with others. That He is most capable of that (4:133); the sūrah of Fāṭir says that if He willed He could eliminate them and produce a new creation (35:16). And the sūrah of the Repentance tells the believers that if they do not march forth, God will afflict them and replace them with another people, and that they will not harm Him at all (9:39).

**The Comfort Contained in the Sentence**

The sūrah has given the same assurance in other forms. It told the Prophet that he was not a keeper over the people (6:107), that God knows best where to place His message (6:124), and that the opening of hearts belongs to God (6:125). The verse in front of us states the same fact about the outcome of the mission, and it grounds the statement in the attributes of the One who sends it.

The next verse states that what the audience has been promised will certainly come to pass and that they will have no escape (6:134). The two verses form a pair: the Lord is self-enough and free to replace them, and the promise he has made will be fulfilled regardless of their response. The passage then turns to the instruction given to the Prophet about how to conduct himself among the people who persist in their ways (6:135).

The sūrah of the Cattle states at its end that God placed human beings as successors on earth and raised some of them above others in rank as a test (6:165). The sūrah of the Human states that God created them and perfected their form and that if He willed He could replace them with others (76:28).

---

## Verse 6:134

> Indeed, what you have been promised will certainly come to pass. And you will have no escape

**The Certainty of What Was Promised**

Indeed, what you have been promised will certainly come to pass. The verse states the certainty of the outcome that the sūrah has announced throughout, and it addresses the audience directly. What was promised was the judgement, the resurrection, and the reckoning described in the surrounding verses, and the sentence removes the option of treating them as remote possibilities.

The Book's language about the promises of God is consistent: what God has promised comes to pass, and there is no alteration in His words. The sūrah has already declared that none can change the words of God (6:34) and that the word of the Lord has been perfected in truth and justice (6:115). The sentence in this verse applies that guarantee to the warnings the audience had received, and it states them as a fact of the future rather than as a threat contingent on their conduct.

**The Plain Statement of the Future**

The Book's habit in these passages is to state the coming of the judgement without argument, and the sūrah has used the same directness in its earlier warnings. It described the day when the wrongdoers will be gathered and told that the fire is their home (6:128), and it recorded the confession of the assembled jinn and humans when they are asked about the messengers (6:130). The verse in front of us states the same event in a single sentence and leaves the details to the passages around it.

The Book's announcements of the reckoning appear in every part of the Qur'an, and the language of certainty is attached to them throughout. The sūrah of the News says that the day is the ultimate truth and that whoever wills may take the path back to his Lord (78:39), and the sūrah of the Resurrection states that there will be no refuge on that day (75:11). The sentence in front of us belongs to that family of statements.

**What the Verse Says About the Delay**

The sentence answers the attitude that the sūrah has documented in its audience, which was the assumption that the warnings were addressed to a distant future and could be met at leisure. The Book's answer in other places is that every matter has its set time (6:67), and the sūrah has already stated that the community has a term that cannot be advanced or delayed (7:34; 10:49).

**The Verse in the Sequence of the Passage**

The verse follows the statement that the Lord is the Self-Sufficient, Full of Mercy, able to replace a people with another (6:133), and it precedes the instruction to the Prophet to declare to his people that each side will persist in its way (6:135). The three verses form the sūrah's final word to the audience within this section: God has no need of them, what He has promised will come, they will not escape, and the Prophet is to carry on with his task.

The passage that follows turns to the dietary system of the audience and to the shares they assigned to God and to their idols (6:136). The shift of subject does not break the sequence, since the sūrah's case against the audience's religion has been built on both the arguments about the signs and the examination of their practices.

The Book has stated that God knows what is on land and sea, that nothing is hidden from Him, and that the record of every person's conduct is kept (6:59–61).

---

## Verse 6:135

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O my people! Persist in your ways, for I ˹too˺ will persist in mine. You will soon know who will fare best in the end. Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed.”

**The Declaration Given to the Prophet**

Say, O my people! Persist in your ways, for I too will persist in mine. The verse instructs the Prophet to address his people with a declaration that both sides will continue as they are, and the Book gives the same words to other messengers. The sūrah of the Troops records the instruction with the addition that the audience will soon come to know who will be visited by a humiliating punishment and an everlasting torment (39:39–40). The sūrah of Hūd gives the words to Shuʿayb with the same conclusion (11:93); and another verse of the same sūrah instructs the Prophet to tell those who disbelieve to persist in their ways while the believers persist in theirs (11:121).

The declaration is not a threat of violence or a challenge to a contest of power. It is the Book's way of closing a dispute that has been conducted with arguments: each side has stated its position, and each will continue in the way it has chosen, and the outcome will show which of them was right. The Book's word for persisting carries the sense of working and acting within a chosen station, and the verses that use it consistently describe the two parties engaging in their own kinds of activity.

The sūrah of the Bee tells the Prophet to call people to the way of the Lord with wisdom and good instruction and to argue in the best manner, and it adds that the Lord knows best who has strayed and who is guided (16:125).

**The Wrongdoers Will Never Succeed**

Indeed, the wrongdoers will never succeed. The verse closes with the declaration of the outcome, and the sūrah has used the same sentence before. It appears at the end of the verse about the people who fabricate lies against God or deny His signs (6:21). Again in the words of Moses in the sūrah of the Stories, where he says that his Lord knows best who has come with true guidance and who will fare best in the end, adding that the wrongdoers will never succeed (28:37).

**What the Verse Requires of the Prophet**

The instruction gives the Prophet a way to conduct himself in a city that would not accept his message. He is not to abandon his people, since he addresses them as his own; he is not to pretend that the disagreement has been settled; and he is not to force the issue. He states the position of each side and continues with his work, and the outcome is left to God.

That pattern is consistent with the sūrah's earlier instructions: the command to follow what was revealed and to turn away from the polytheists (6:106), the reminder that the Prophet is neither keeper nor maintainer (6:107), and the statement that God's will decides who believes (6:111). The verse in front of us gives the practical form those instructions take in the Prophet's relationship with his own people.

---

## Verse 6:136

> The pagans set aside for Allah a share of the crops and cattle He created, saying, “This ˹portion˺ is for Allah,” so they claim, “and this ˹one˺ for our associate-gods.” Yet the portion of their associate-gods is not shared with Allah while Allah’s portion is shared with their associate-gods. What unfair judgment

**The Shares Assigned to God and the Idols**

The pagans set aside for Allah a share of the crops and cattle He created, saying, This portion is for Allah, so they claim, and this one for our associate-gods. The verse opens the sūrah's detailed treatment of the audience's ritual economy, and it describes a practice in which the produce of the land and the herds was divided between the Creator and the beings associated with Him. The Book records the same custom elsewhere, saying that the idolaters assign to the idols, who know nothing, a share of what God has provided for them (16:56).

**The Offence in the Arrangement**

The offence that the sūrah is describing is not only the association of other beings with God but the redirection of provision that God had given. The Book states that the crops and cattle were created by God, which establishes the claim of the Creator to what His creation produces, and the sūrah of the Bee records the charge that the idolaters assign shares to beings that know nothing of the gift.

The Book's treatment of offerings elsewhere makes the same point about the direction in which gratitude should run. The sūrah of the Cattle will later say that the audience forbade what God had provided, attributing the prohibition to Him without warrant (6:140), and the verse in front of us shows the same system operating in the opposite direction, with gifts directed to the idols instead of to their maker.

The verse begins a passage of several verses that examines the audience's rules about crops, cattle, and the offspring of the herds (6:136–139). Each verse takes up a particular practice, states it in the audience's own words, and passes judgement on it. The arrangement is the sūrah's method of showing the reader what the religion of the Makkans looked like when it was examined piece by piece.

The next verse describes the effect of the evil associates on the audience, saying that they made the killing of their own children appealing to them and that this led to their destruction and to confusion in their faith, before instructing the Prophet to leave them and their falsehood (6:137). The two verses belong together, since both describe the influence of the associated beings on the community's conduct.

The sūrah so moves from the division of harvests to the destruction of children, and the passage as a whole shows the consequences of the religion the audience had inherited. What the verses state is the practical result of the associations the sūrah has been examining since its account of the jinn (6:100).

---

## Verse 6:137

> Likewise, the pagans’ evil associates have made it appealing to them to kill their own children—only leading to their destruction as well as confusion in their faith. Had it been Allah’s Will, they would not have done such a thing. So leave them and their falsehood

**The Killing of Children**

Likewise, the pagans' evil associates have made it appealing to them to kill their own children.

The practice was the burial of infant daughters, which the audience carried out for reasons of poverty and honour, and the Book names the reason in its later prohibition: do not kill your children for fear of poverty, since God provides for them and for their parents (17:31). The sūrah of the Cattle's closing list of prohibitions repeats the command against killing children, and the sūrah of the Folding Up describes the day when the girl buried alive is asked for what crime she was put to death (81:8–9).

**The Standard the Book Exposes**

The Book returns to the practice and to the attitude behind it in several passages. The sūrah of the Bee describes the man who is given the news of a baby girl, whose face grows gloomy as he suppresses his rage. Who hides from his people wondering whether to keep her in disgrace or bury her alive, and the verse ends by calling the judgement of such people evil (16:58–59). The sūrah of the Ornament gives the same description of the man given news of what he attributes to the Most Compassionate (43:17), and the sūrah of the Star asks the audience whether they prefer sons for themselves while attributing daughters to God (53:21).

The Book's answer to the attitude is stated in the sūrah of Consultation, where God is described as creating whatever He wills, blessing whom He wills with daughters, blessing whom He wills with sons, granting both to whom He wills. Leaving whom He wills infertile, for He is All-Knowing and Most Capable (42:49–50). The verse in front of us describes the practice that the Book's statements about daughters answer, and the passage places the killing of children inside the larger account of the audience's false attributions.

**The Statement About God's Will**

Had it been Allah's Will, they would not have done such a thing. The sentence repeats the formula that the sūrah has used before in answering the audience's conduct, and its placement here is important. The same statement was made about the idolaters' association of partners with God (6:107), and the sūrah will later record the audience using the same plea as an excuse for their practices and rejecting it (6:148).

The formula states the dependence of everything on God's will, and the Book uses it in the passages that describe the resistance of the community to the message. What the sentence does not do is to excuse the practice. The Book elsewhere makes the same statement and then states the punishment, and the two belong together in its account of human responsibility.

**The Instruction to Leave Them**

So leave them and their falsehood. The verse closes with the instruction the Prophet has received twice already in this sūrah, when he was told to leave those who took their religion as play and amusement (6:70) and to leave the enemies with their deceit (6:112). The instruction in each case concerns the Prophet's own conduct rather than the abandonment of the mission, since the sūrah continues to address the audience's practices in the verses that follow.

---

## Verse 6:138

> They say, “These cattle and crops are reserved—none may eat them except those we permit,” so they claim. Some other cattle are exempted from labour and others are not slaughtered in Allah’s Name—falsely attributing lies to Him. He will repay them for their lies

**The Cattle Exempted From Labour**

Some other cattle are exempted from labour and others are not slaughtered in Allah's Name. The sentence names two further features of the system, and the commentators explained them as rules about which animals might be used for work and which might be killed without the invocation.

The two rules together show how far the community's practice had moved from the principle the sūrah stated when it commanded the believers to eat of what has been slaughtered in the Name of God (6:118–119).

**The Charge of Attribution**

Falsely attributing lies to Him. The sentence states the offence that underlies the whole system, and the Book uses the same charge throughout the chapter. The sūrah has said that the idolaters invent lies against God (6:21), that those who fabricate revelation are the greatest wrongdoers (6:93), and that the audience attributes to God sons and daughters out of ignorance (6:100). The practices described here are the practical form of the attribution: what was forbidden by custom was presented as forbidden by God.

The Book's objection is not to the existence of rules but to the claim of divine authority made on behalf of rules that God had not given. The sūrah has already established the standard for such claims when it stated that God has explained what He has forbidden (6:119), and the verse in front of us measures the audience's system against that standard and finds it wanting.

The verse stands between the description of the shares assigned to God and to the idols (6:136) and the rules about the offspring of the herds (6:139), and it deals with the same system from the standpoint of the community's own table. The sūrah has been examining the audience's religion piece by piece since it turned from the argument about the signs, and this passage examines the part of it that governed daily life.

The treatment is not incidental to the chapter's purpose. The sūrah's claim throughout has been that the authority to permit and forbid belongs to God, and the passage that begins with these verses shows what happens when that authority is claimed by custom instead.

The next verse records the rule the audience applied to the offspring of their herds, reserving it for the males and forbidding it to the females, with the qualification that a stillborn animal could be shared by all (6:139). The detail completes the survey of the system, and the verse closes the section with the statement that God will repay the audience for their falsehood and with the declaration that He is All-Wise, All-Knowing.

The passage that follows moves to the audience's practice of killing their children and forbidding what God has provided, and it states that those who do so have strayed (6:140). The sūrah's survey of the inherited system is therefore complete once these verses are read together, and the reader is left with the account of a religion that had replaced the law of God with the rulings of custom.

The sūrah of the Cow instructs the believers to eat of the good things provided and to give thanks (2:172), and the sūrah of the Bee commands the same and forbids the practices that restrict what God has given.

---

## Verse 6:139

> They ˹also˺ say, “The offspring of this cattle is reserved for our males and forbidden to our females; but if it is stillborn, they may all share it.” He will repay them for their falsehood. Surely He is All-Wise, All-Knowing

**The Rule About the Offspring**

They also say, The offspring of this cattle is reserved for our males and forbidden to our females. The verse records another of the community's rules, and this one governs the young of the animals they had set apart. The males of the community were permitted to eat the offspring, and the women were not, which made the rule a matter of the household's internal order as much as of food.

The Book records the same kind of regulation elsewhere. The sūrah of the Table lists the set-apart animals of the Arabian system, naming the animals freed in the name of the gods, those dedicated to them, and the males reserved from work and from slaughter, and it states that the disbelievers invented these categories (5:103). The system described in the verse in front of us belongs to the same family of practices.

**The Charge Reiterated**

He will repay them for their falsehood. The sentence repeats the formula that closed the previous verse, and the repetition binds the two rules together as a single offence. The audience's system attributed to God a set of regulations that He had not given, and the Book states that the attribution will be answered by the One who knows what they said.

The Book's treatment of such attributions appears in several passages. The sūrah of the Bee tells the audience not to utter falsehoods about what God has permitted and forbidden and to attribute the rulings to Him (16:116). The sūrah of Yūnus asks the audience whether God has given them permission for what they have made lawful or unlawful, or whether they are inventing the rulings themselves (10:59). The verses in front of us state the practice those passages address.

**What the Rule Revealed About the Community**

The regulation described here used food as a way of ordering the community's members, and the distinction it drew fell along lines of sex and household standing rather than along lines of conduct.

The sūrah of the Cattle's later passage about the gardens instructs the community to eat of the fruit they bear and to pay what is due at the harvest, without adding any of the distinctions the audience had built into its system (6:141).

**The Place of the Passage in the Sūrah**

The passage that begins with the shares assigned to God and the idols (6:136) and continues through the killing of children, the reservation of the herds. The rule about the offspring ends with the declaration that those who killed their children and forbade what God provided have strayed and are not guided (6:140). The four verses form a survey of the audience's dietary and domestic system, and each of them states a practice and the judgement it receives.

The sūrah's argument has been building toward the declaration of what God has actually forbidden and commanded, which appears in the long passage beginning at 6:151. What the passage in front of us provides is the negative side of that instruction: the system that the revelation came to replace, presented in the audience's own terms.

---

## Verse 6:140

> Lost indeed are those who have murdered their own children foolishly out of ignorance and have forbidden what Allah has provided for them—falsely attributing lies to Allah. They have certainly strayed and are not ˹rightly˺ guided

**The Verdict on the System Just Described**

Lost indeed are those who have murdered their own children foolishly out of ignorance and have forbidden what Allah has provided for them. The verse gathers the offences described in the preceding passage and states their result, and the Book's word for the lost describes a loss that cannot be recovered. The killing of children was described in the earlier verse as the work of the associates who made it appealing (6:137), and the prohibition of God's provision was described in the rules about the herds and the crops (6:138–139). The sentence in front of us states what the whole system produced.

**The Two Charges and Their Ground**

The clause foolishly out of ignorance names the cause of the killing, and the Book's word for the ignorance describes conduct undertaken without knowledge rather than a simple lack of information. The practice was carried out for reasons of poverty and honour, and the Book's later prohibition addresses the poverty directly, telling the audience not to kill their children for fear of want since God provides for them and for their parents (17:31).

**The False Attribution**

Falsely attributing lies to Allah. The clause names the act that made the system a religious offence rather than a local custom. The audience had attached the authority of God to regulations they had invented, and the sūrah has stated the same charge at each stage of its examination: the shares assigned to the idols (6:136), the killing of children (6:137), the reservation of the herds (6:138), and the rules about the offspring (6:139).

The Book's treatment of the charge of attributing lies to God runs through the chapter, and the sūrah has described the fabrication of revelation and the claim to have received it as the worst of offences (6:93). The verses in front of us apply the same charge to the community's regulation of its food, which shows how seriously the Book treats the claim of divine authority made on behalf of human invention.

The verse closes the passage that began with the shares assigned to God and the idols (6:136), and it states the judgement on the whole system the passage examined. The sūrah has been conducting its survey of the audience's practices for several verses, and the sentence in front of us supplies the conclusion that the survey was building toward.

The chapter then turns to the created order and to the food that God has actually provided, describing the gardens, the palms, the crops, the olives. The pomegranates (6:141), and it follows that description with the instruction to eat of what God has given and not to follow the footsteps of Satan (6:142). The verse in front of us marks the turning point of the section, and what follows supplies the positive account of the provision the audience had been managing by custom.

The passage that follows continues with the pairs of livestock and the argument about which animals God has forbidden (6:143–144), and it closes with the declaration that those who fabricate lies against God to mislead others will not be guided. The verse in front of us therefore stands at the head of the sūrah's positive account of what God has given, and the account is set against the system the previous verses described.

---

## Verse 6:141

> He is the One Who produces gardens—both cultivated and wild—and palm trees, crops of different flavours, olives, and pomegranates—similar ˹in shape˺, but dissimilar ˹in taste˺. Eat of the fruit they bear and pay the dues at harvest, but do not waste. Surely He does not like the wasteful

**The Gardens and Their Produce**

He is the One Who produces gardens, both cultivated and wild, and palm trees, crops of different flavours, olives, and pomegranates, similar in shape but dissimilar in taste. The verse returns to the theme of provision after the passage on the audience's regulations, and it lists the produce of the region in the detail the sūrah has used before. The gardens appear in the sūrah's earlier account of the rain that produces green stalks, clustered grain, dates hanging within reach, vineyards, olives, and pomegranates (6:99).

**What the Verse Says About the Present Order**

The Book's treatment of the subject elsewhere makes the same contrast. The sūrah of the Heights asks who has forbidden the adornments and the good provisions that God has brought forth for His servants, and it answers that they belong to the believers in this world and will be theirs alone on the day of resurrection (7:32). The verse in front of us belongs with that passage, and the two together state the Book's position on the goods of the world.

**The Link to the Earlier Passage on Provision**

The verse echoes the sūrah's earlier account of the rain and the produce of the earth (6:99), and the two passages share the same details: the palms, the crops, the olives, the pomegranates, and the observation about the similarity of shape and the difference of taste. The earlier passage closed with the statement that these are signs for people who believe, and the verse in front of us closes with the command to eat, to give, and not to waste.

The next verse returns to the livestock, stating that some cattle are fit for labour while others are too small, and it instructs the community to eat of what God has provided and not to follow the footsteps of Satan, who is an avowed enemy (6:142). The pair of verses covers the produce of the fields and the produce of the herds, and the instruction in both cases is the same.

The passage that follows takes up the argument about the pairs of animals and the claim that God had forbidden certain of them (6:143–144), and it answers the claim by asking the audience for the knowledge on which it rests. The verse in front of us supplies the positive command that the argument assumes, and the verses that follow remove the false restrictions that had been placed on the provision.

---

## Verse 6:142

> Some cattle are fit for labour, others are too small. Eat of what Allah has provided for you and do not follow Satan’s footsteps. Certainly, he is your sworn enemy

**The Cattle Given for Use**

Some cattle are fit for labour, others are too small. The verse returns to the livestock after the description of the gardens, and it names the two ways the herds served the audience. The larger animals carried loads and worked the land; the smaller ones were kept for their meat, their milk, and their hides. The Book's other passages describe the same provision, saying that God created the cattle as a source of warmth, food, and other benefits (16:5), and that they carry loads to distant lands that people could not otherwise reach without hardship (16:7).

The verse so presents the herds as a gift with functions, and the functions are the ones the audience depended on. The sūrah's earlier account of the audience's rules about the animals had examined the categories they had invented for these same creatures (6:138–139), and the sentence in front of us states the plain arrangement that the categories had obscured.

**The Command to Eat and the Warning**

Eat of what Allah has provided for you and do not follow Satan's footsteps. The sentence joins permission to a prohibition, and it uses the formula the Book applies to the food of the community in several places. The sūrah of the Cow instructs humanity to eat from what is lawful and good on the earth and not to follow the footsteps of Satan, describing him as an avowed enemy (2:168). The sūrah of the Table tells the believers to eat of the good things provided and to be mindful of God (5:88); and the sūrah of the Bee gives the same command with the addition that the eater should give thanks (16:114).

The warning about Satan's footsteps is attached to the dietary instruction in each of these passages, and the connection is the same in all of them. The footsteps are the practices that lead away from what God has provided, and the sūrah in front of us has just finished describing the system the audience had built in place of the plain command.

**The Enemy Named at the End**

Certainly, he is your sworn enemy. The clause identifies Satan, and the Book states the same fact in its addresses to humanity in several places. The sūrah of Yā-Sīn records the question put to the children of Adam on the day of judgement, asking whether they were not commanded not to follow Satan, since he is their avowed enemy (36:60), and the sūrah of the Heights describes the enmity declared at the beginning of human life. The sūrah of the Cow's dietary instruction ends with the same description.

The sentence gives the warning its reason. The practices that the sūrah has been describing were not neutral customs; they were the means by which the community was led away from the provision of God, and the Book names the agency behind them.

The two verses that follow record that answer, asking the audience whether it was the males, the females, or what was in the wombs that God had forbidden, and requiring them to produce the knowledge on which their claim rested (6:143–144). The verse in front of us supplies the command that the argument assumes, and the challenge that follows removes the ground on which the audience's rules had been built.

---

## Verse 6:143

> ˹Allah has created˺ four pairs: a pair of sheep and a pair of goats—ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Has He forbidden ˹to you˺ the two males or the two females or what is in the wombs of the two females? Tell me with knowledge, if what you say is true.”—

**The Pairs and the Question**

The verse opens the sūrah's final treatment of the dietary rules by taking up the claim that certain animals had been forbidden, and it puts the question to the audience through the Prophet's instruction. The created animals come in male and female, and whatever is carried in the womb belongs to the female of the pair, so the categories of the created order are only three.

The sūrah of the Troops states that God produced four pairs of cattle for humanity and that He creates people in the wombs of their mothers in stages, and the verse in front of us belongs to the same account of creation as provision (39:6).

**The Demand for Knowledge**

Tell me with knowledge, if what you say is true. The clause demands evidence, and the demand is the same one the sūrah has made throughout its treatment of the audience's claims. The sūrah has described the majority as followers of assumption who do nothing but lie (6:116), and it has stated that assumption cannot replace the truth (10:36). The sentence in front of us applies that standard to the audience's dietary rules.

**What the Argument Assumes About God's Commands**

The question assumes that a command of God would be knowable, and the assumption runs through the Book's treatment of the subject. The sūrah has stated that God has explained what He has forbidden (6:119), and it has recorded the revelation of the rules that the community actually received. A prohibition that could not be traced to the revelation, could not be derived from the categories of the created animals, and could not be stated in the terms of the audience's own practice had no claim to be a command of God.

**What the Verse Says About the Audience's Authority**

The verse addresses the audience as people who claimed to know what God had commanded, and it asks them to account for the claim. The sūrah has examined the shares they assigned to God and their partners (6:136), the killing of their children (6:137), and the rules that restricted their herds and crops (6:138–139), and it now asks them to state the basis of the system as a whole.

The Book's treatment of false attribution to God runs through the chapter, and the sūrah has described the claim to have received revelation as one of the gravest offences (6:93). The claim that God had forbidden certain animals belongs to the same family of assertions, and the question in front of us exposes it by requiring the claimant to produce the foundation of his claim.

The next verse names the second pair of animals and repeats the question about males, females, and wombs, before asking whether the audience was present when God gave the commandment and declaring that no one does greater wrong than the person who fabricates lies against God to mislead others without knowledge (6:144). The two verses are a single argument, and the second supplies the conclusion.

The passage that follows records the Prophet's declaration of what is actually forbidden and the rules that apply to the person compelled by necessity (6:145), and it then turns to the dietary practices of the Jewish communities and the guidance that the earlier scriptures contained. The verse in front of us stands at the head of that final treatment of food, and the challenge it issues governs everything that follows.

---

## Verse 6:144

> and a pair of camels and a pair of oxen. Ask ˹them˺, “Has He forbidden ˹to you˺ the two males or the two females or what is in the wombs of the two females? Or were you present when Allah gave you this commandment?” Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah to mislead others without ˹any˺ knowledge? Surely Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people

**The Second Pair and the Completed Enumeration**

And a pair of camels and a pair of oxen. The verse completes the list of the animals whose status the audience had regulated, and the four pairs named across this verse and the one before it are the sheep, the goats, the camels, and the oxen. The Book's account of the created order elsewhere states that God made spouses for human beings and mates for the cattle (42:11) and that He created all things in pairs (36:36), and the enumeration in this passage belongs to that account of creation.

The sūrah of the Troops states that God produced four pairs of cattle for humanity and joins the statement to its account of the creation of human beings in the wombs (39:6). The passage in front of us draws the animals into the argument about what God has forbidden, and the enumeration supplies the field within which the audience's claim must be examined.

**The Challenge About the Commandment**

Or were you present when Allah gave you this commandment? The clause adds a second challenge, and it addresses the claim of divine authority directly. The audience asserted that God had forbidden certain animals, and the sentence asks whether they had witnessed the giving of such a command.

The sūrah has told the Prophet to say that he has no knowledge of what the audience asserts and has described them as followers of assumption (6:116), and the verse in front of us puts the same challenge in the form of a question about witnessing.

**The Charge at the End**

Who does more wrong than those who fabricate lies against Allah to mislead others without any knowledge? The sentence is the sūrah's repeated formula, and it adds the element of misleading others. The sūrah used the formula at the beginning of the chapter, in the verse about those who invent a lie about God or deny His signs (6:21), and it used it in the passage about those who claim to have received revelation (6:93). The form in front of us identifies the harm the fabrication does to the community that accepts it.

The clause without any knowledge returns to the theme the sūrah has developed since its warning about the majority. The people who invented the restrictions had no revelation and no evidence, and the sentence states the effect of their invention on the people who followed them.

The next verse records the Prophet's declaration that he finds nothing forbidden to eat in what has been revealed to him except carrion, running blood, swine, and what has been offered in the name of other than God, with the exception of the person compelled by necessity (6:145). The declaration answers the fabricated rules with the actual prohibition, and it states the limits of what the revelation forbids.

The passage that follows turns to the restrictions imposed on the Jewish communities and to the mercy that the sūrah's audience was receiving, and it then addresses the polytheists' argument about God's will (6:146–148). The verse in front of us closes the sūrah's challenge to the audience's dietary system, and the declaration that follows supplies what the system had obscured.

---

## Verse 6:145

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I do not find in what has been revealed to me anything forbidden to eat except carrion, running blood, swine—which is impure—or a sinful offering in the name of any other than Allah. But if someone is compelled by necessity—neither driven by desire nor exceeding immediate need—then surely your Lord is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

Say, I do not find in what has been revealed to me anything forbidden to eat except carrion, running blood, swine, which is impure, or a sinful offering in the name of any other than Allah. The four categories named here are the prohibitions the Book states in its other dietary passages as well: the sūrah of the Cow lists carrion, blood, swine. What has been slaughtered in the name of other than God, and it adds the exception for the person compelled (2:173); the sūrah of the Table gives the same list; and the sūrah of the Bee repeats it with the same qualification (16:115).

**The Four Categories**

The verse names the prohibited things in an order that moves from the animal that died without slaughter, to the blood that pours from a slaughtered animal, to the swine, and finally to the animal offered to a being other than God. The Book's word for the third item describes it as impure, and the fourth is described as an offering made in disobedience. The sūrah has already forbidden the eating of what has not been slaughtered in the Name of God (6:121), and the list in front of us states the same principle in its full form.

The categories show what the Book's dietary law actually contains. It is not a system of inherited taboos about particular animals, and it does not rest on distinctions between the males and the females or on the condition of the unborn. It rests on the condition of the animal at the time of its death and on the name invoked over it, which is the principle the sūrah stated when it commanded the believers to eat of what has been slaughtered in the Name of God (6:118).

**What the Verse Says About the Revelation's Authority**

The sentence's reference to what has been revealed to the Prophet is the ground on which the whole dietary question is settled. The audience had appealed to the customs of their ancestors, and the Book answers with the content of the message. The sūrah has stated that God has explained what He has forbidden (6:119), and the verse in front of us states what that explanation contains.

The Book's use of the formula in other contexts shows that it is not a denial of the revelation's completeness but a statement of where the rulings are to be found. The sūrah of the Cattle itself will later say that the Book is fully explained and that nothing has been omitted from it (6:38), and the declaration in front of us belongs to that account of a revelation that states what it requires.

The verse stands between the challenge to the audience's dietary system (6:143–144) and the account of the restrictions imposed on the Jewish communities (6:146), and it supplies the answer that the challenge required. The sūrah's examination of food is complete once the positive statement of the law has been given, and the verses that follow show how the community's law relates to the laws given before it.

The passage that continues from here returns to the theme of the audience's arguments, with the polytheists claiming that God's will was behind their practices and the Prophet told to answer that God has the conclusive argument (6:148–149). The verse in front of us therefore closes one movement of the sūrah and prepares the reader for the next, and it does so by stating the law in its simplest form.

The Book's treatment of the earlier laws appears in several of its passages, and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that all food was lawful for the children of Israel except what Israel made unlawful for himself before the Torah was revealed (3:93). The verse in front of us supplies the ruling for the community that received the Qur'an, and the passage that follows explains why the ruling differs from what the earlier communities were given.

---

## Verse 6:146

> For those who are Jewish, We forbade every animal with undivided hoofs and the fat of oxen and sheep except what is joined to their backs or intestines or mixed with bone. In this way We rewarded them for their violations. And We are certainly truthful

For those who are Jewish, We forbade every animal with undivided hoofs and the fat of oxen and sheep except what is joined to their backs or intestines or mixed with bone.

In the sūrah of the Women, God states that He forbade the Jews certain foods that had been lawful to them because of their wrongdoing and because they hindered many from the way of God (4:160). The sūrah of the Bee says that to the Jews God forbade what had been related before and that they did not wrong God but wronged themselves (16:118).

**The Reason Given in the Sentence**

In this way We rewarded them for their violations. The sentence states the reason for the restriction, and the Book's account of the matter is that the prohibitions were imposed as a consequence of the community's conduct. The same connection appears in that sūrah's passage about the wrongdoing of the Jews, their taking of interest, and their consuming of people's wealth unjustly (4:160–161).

The Book's treatment of the earlier laws does not present them as burdens imposed without cause. The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that all food was lawful for the children of Israel except what Israel made unlawful for himself before the Torah was revealed, and it invites the audience to bring the Torah and recite it if they are truthful (3:93). What the restriction in this verse represents is a penalty attached to a community's violations, and the Book states it as a fact of the history it recounts.

The verse completes the sūrah's treatment of food, which has run from the command to eat of what has been slaughtered in the Name of God (6:118) through the examination of the audience's system (6:136–144) to the declaration of what is actually forbidden (6:145). The passage in front of us adds the account of the earlier community's law, and the section is complete once the comparison has been made.

The sūrah's argument in this section has followed a consistent method. The audience's practices are stated in their own terms, the revelation's ruling is given, the earlier communities' laws are explained, and the responsibility for the practices is left with the people who adopted them. The last of those elements is supplied here, and the passage that follows returns to the audience's own arguments about God's will.

The next verse tells the Prophet that if they deny him, he should say that the Lord is infinite in mercy but that His punishment will not be averted from the wicked people (6:147). The sentence follows the account of the restrictions and the testimony attached to it, and it states the two attributes that frame the audience's position: a mercy that gives time and a punishment that is not withdrawn from those who persist.

The passage that follows records the polytheists' argument that God's will lay behind their association of partners with Him and their prohibitions, an argument the sūrah answers by asking whether they have any knowledge to produce (6:148). This sentence stands between the two, and it states the position of the community that has received the revelation and the law in its plain form.

---

## Verse 6:147

> But if they deny you ˹O Prophet˺, say, “Your Lord is infinite in mercy, yet His punishment will not be averted from the wicked people.”

**The Instruction for the Case of Denial**

But if they deny you, say, Your Lord is infinite in mercy, yet His punishment will not be averted from the wicked people. The verse gives the Prophet the words for the situation in which the audience rejects the message, and it is placed immediately after the account of the earlier communities' dietary laws and the testimony that closed it. The instruction covers both sides of the matter in a single sentence: the mercy that gives the deniers their time and the punishment that remains in place for those who persist.

The Book's habit of pairing the two attributes is consistent throughout. The sūrah of the Rocky Tract tells the Prophet to inform the servants that God is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful. That His torment is the most painful (15:49–50); the sūrah of the Forgiver describes God as the Forgiver of sin, the Accepter of repentance, the Severe in punishment. The Infinite in bounty (40:3); and the sūrah of the Heights records God's answer to Moses that His mercy encompasses all things while His punishment falls on those who persist (7:156). The verse in front of us states the pair in the context of the Prophet's own situation.

**The Punishment That Is Not Averted**

The second half of the sentence states the limit of the delay, and the Book's word for the wicked describes those who persist in wrongdoing. The sūrah has described the same outcome for the communities that rejected their messengers, and it has stated that the destruction of a society is not attributed to God before the warning has been delivered (6:131).

The Book's statements about the punishment attached to persistent denial appear throughout the chapter. The sūrah has described the wrongdoers in the throes of death and the torment that follows (6:93), the humiliation and severe punishment awaiting those who demanded the messenger's office (6:124), and the fire described as the home of the people who misled and were misled (6:128). The sentence in front of us states the same outcome in the form of an answer to denial.

The verse stands between the account of the earlier communities' dietary restrictions and the testimony that the revelation's account of them is true (6:146), on one side, and the polytheists' argument that God's will lay behind their practices (6:148), on the other. It functions as the answer the Prophet is to give when the account is denied, and it prepares the reader for the fuller argument about divine will and human responsibility that follows.

The sūrah's treatment of the audience's appeal to God's will is one of the most important passages in the chapter, and the verse in front of us states the framework within which that appeal is answered. The mercy and the punishment are the two terms of the arrangement, and the audience's plea does not remove them.

The two verses belong together, since the appeal to divine will is the denial the previous verse anticipated, and the answer to it occupies the verses that follow. The sūrah's treatment of the subject ends with the declaration that God has the most conclusive argument and that He could have imposed guidance on everyone had He willed (6:149), and the verse in front of us opens that final exchange.

---

## Verse 6:148

> The polytheists will argue, “Had it been Allah’s Will, neither we nor our forefathers would have associated others with Him ˹in worship˺ or made anything unlawful.” Likewise, those before them rejected the truth until they tasted Our punishment. Ask ˹them, O Prophet˺, “Do you have any knowledge that you can produce for us? Surely you follow nothing but ˹false˺ assumptions and you do nothing but lie.”

**The Argument of the Polytheists**

The polytheists will argue, Had it been Allah's Will, neither we nor our forefathers would have associated others with Him in worship or made anything unlawful. The verse records the audience's appeal to the divine will as an excuse for their conduct, and the appeal is one the sūrah has already anticipated. The sūrah stated that had God willed, the idolaters would not have associated partners with Him (6:107), and it made the same statement about the killing of children (6:137); the verse in front of us shows the audience turning that statement into a defence.

The Book records the same argument from other communities. The sūrah of the Bee states that the polytheists said that had God willed, neither they nor their ancestors would have worshipped anything besides Him, nor would they have forbidden anything (16:35). The sūrah of the Ornament records their claim that if the Most Compassionate had willed they would never have worshipped the idols (43:20); and the sūrah of the Cow records the answer of those who were invited to follow the revelation, that they would follow only what their forefathers followed (2:170). The argument appears in each passage as the last resort of a people who have no evidence.

**The Demand for Knowledge**

Ask them, Do you have any knowledge that you can produce for us? The question turns the appeal back on the people who made it, and it asks them to produce something that would support their claim. The Book's word for the knowledge in question covers any evidence or revelation that would establish that God had willed their practices in the sense they claimed.

The demand is the same one the sūrah made when it challenged the audience's dietary rules and required them to state the basis of their claim (6:143–144). The method is consistent: the audience is asked for the foundation of its position, and the absence of any foundation is then stated.

**The Charge of Assumption and Falsehood**

Surely you follow nothing but false assumptions and you do nothing but lie. The sentence states the result of the challenge, and it uses the same vocabulary the sūrah has applied to the audience throughout. The verse about the majority said that they follow nothing but assumptions and do nothing but lie (6:116), and the sūrah of Yūnus states that most of them follow nothing but assumption and that assumption cannot replace the truth (10:36).

The two charges correspond to the two parts of the audience's conduct. The assumptions are the beliefs they inherited and never examined, and the lies are the claims they made about God on the basis of those beliefs, including the claim that He had forbidden certain things and willed their practices.

**What the Verse Says About the Appeal to God's Will**

The Book's resolution of the matter appears in the verse that follows, where God is said to have the most conclusive argument and to have been able to impose guidance on everyone had He willed (6:149). The divine will and human accountability are both affirmed, and the appeal that would use one to cancel the other is answered by the statement that God's case is complete and that the messengers were sent so that no one would have an excuse (4:165).

---

## Verse 6:149

> Say, “Allah has the most conclusive argument. Had it been His Will, He would have easily imposed guidance upon all of you.”

**The Counterfactual About Guidance**

Had it been His Will, He would have easily imposed guidance upon all of you. The clause states what God could have done, and the Book makes the same statement in several places. The sūrah of Yūnus asks the Prophet whether he would compel people to believe after stating that all the people of the earth would have believed had his Lord willed (10:99). The sūrah of the Adoration says that had God willed, He could have imposed guidance on every soul (32:13); and the sūrah of the Cattle itself, in its account of the Prophet's distress over the denial of his people, states that God could have guided them all had He willed (6:35).

The clause is the answer to the audience's argument in its own terms. If God's will were the standard by which conduct is excused, then the fact that He could have compelled belief shows that the present arrangement is not compulsion. The audience was left free, and the choice they made was theirs.

**What the Book Says About Compelled Belief**

The Book's statements about guidance consistently distinguish between the power to compel and the decision to leave people free. The sūrah of the Cave states that the truth is from the Lord and that whoever wills may believe, and the sūrah of the Bee tells the Prophet that his duty is to invite and that God knows best who has strayed and who is guided (16:125). The declaration in front of us belongs to that account of the relationship between God and human beings.

The verse completes the passage that began with the audience's appeal to God's will (6:148). The audience had argued that they were not responsible for their practices, and the Book's answer runs through the verse that preceded this one and this verse itself. The audience has no knowledge to produce, they follow nothing but assumptions, they do nothing but lie, God's argument is conclusive, and He could have imposed guidance on everyone had He willed.

The exchange therefore ends with the responsibility placed where the sūrah has placed it throughout: the message was delivered, the evidence was presented, and the response belonged to the people who heard it. The Book's claim in the next verse takes up the same subject from a different angle, with the Prophet told to demand witnesses from those who claim that God has forbidden certain things (6:150).

The next verse tells the Prophet to demand witnesses who can testify that God has forbidden the things the audience claimed were forbidden. It instructs him not to testify with them, not to follow the desires of those who deny the proofs, disbelieve in the Hereafter, and set up equals with their Lord (6:150). The demand for witnesses continues the challenge the previous verses began, and it returns the discussion to the dietary rules the audience had invented.

The passage that follows begins the sūrah's long recitation of what God has actually forbidden, running through the commands about parents, children, indecency, life, the property of orphans, honest measure, and the path of God (6:151–153). The verse in front of us closes the debate about the audience's appeal and hands the reader over to the positive statement of the law.

---

## Verse 6:150

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Bring your witnesses who can testify that Allah has forbidden this.” If they ˹falsely˺ testify, do not testify with them. And do not follow the desires of those who deny Our proofs, disbelieve in the Hereafter, and set up equals with their Lord

**The Demand for Witnesses**

Say, Bring your witnesses who can testify that Allah has forbidden this. The verse gives the Prophet a demand that turns the audience's claim into a matter to be proved, and the Book uses the same form of challenge in its arguments about the revelation. The sūrah of the Cow tells the doubters to produce a sūrah like the revelation and to call their helpers besides God if they are truthful (2:23), and the sūrah of the Ants asks whether another god could originate creation, resurrect it, and provide from the heavens and the earth (27:64).

The witness the verse demands is a person who can testify that God forbade the animals the audience had declared unlawful. The sūrah has already asked whether the audience was present when the commandment was given (6:144), and the demand for witnesses completes the challenge: the claim of a divine prohibition requires witnesses, and none exist.

**The Instruction Not to Follow Desires**

And do not follow the desires of those who deny Our proofs, disbelieve in the Hereafter, and set up equals with their Lord. They deny the signs, which the sūrah has described at length in its accounts of the demand for miracles. They disbelieve in the Hereafter, which the sūrah identified as the condition of those who receive the whisperings of the devils (6:113); and they set up equals with God, which is the charge that runs through the whole chapter.

The Book's warnings against following desire appear in several places. The sūrah of the Cattle itself tells the Prophet to say that he will not follow the audience's desires, since doing so would lead him astray from the guidance of God (6:56). The sūrah of the Kneeling describes those who have taken their own desires as their god and whom God has left to stray knowingly (45:23).

**The Reasons for the Instruction**

The Book gives the Prophet reasons for the limits it places on his engagement with the audience's claims. The sūrah has told him that he is not a keeper over the people (6:107) and that the hearts of those who refuse are left in their condition (6:110), and the sentence in front of us adds that the desires of such people are not to become the measure of his conduct.

The Book's treatment of the community's relations with people of other religions elsewhere is built on the same principle. The sūrah of the Spider tells the believers to argue with the People of the Book in the best manner and to state their belief in what has been revealed to them and to the earlier communities (29:46). The sūrah of the Clearing forbids the believers from allowing the hostility of others to drive them into injustice.

**The Transition to the Next Verses**

The next verses record the recitation of what God has actually forbidden, beginning with the association of partners with Him and continuing through the commands about parents, children, indecency. The taking of life (6:151), and then the commands about the property of orphans, honest measure, just speech, and the fulfilment of covenants (6:152).
