The recitation ends with the declaration that this is God's path and that its followers are commanded to hold to it (6:153).

The sūrah of the Distinguisher includes among the qualities of the servants of the Most Compassionate that they do not bear false witness and that they pass by falsehood with dignity (25:72), and the sūrah of the Pilgrimage includes the avoidance of false testimony among the commands given to the community.

The Book's statements about the actual prohibition are given in the verses that follow, where the Prophet is told to recite what God has forbidden: the association of others with Him, the failure to honour parents, the killing of children, indecency, the taking of life, the property of orphans, dishonest measure, and the breaking of covenants (6:151–152).

---

## Verse 6:151

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Come! Let me recite to you what your Lord has forbidden to you: do not associate others with Him ˹in worship˺. ˹Do not fail to˺ honour your parents. Do not kill your children for fear of poverty. We provide for you and for them. Do not come near indecencies, openly or secretly. Do not take a ˹human˺ life—made sacred by Allah—except with ˹legal˺ right. This is what He has commanded you, so perhaps you will understand

**The Command About Parents**

Do not fail to honour your parents. The command follows immediately after the prohibition of association, and the Book's pairing of the two appears repeatedly. The sūrah of the Night Journey gives the two commands in sequence and describes the treatment of parents in old age, telling the believer not to repel them and to address them with words of honour (17:23–24). The sūrah of Luqmān places the command about parents after the warning against association; and the sūrah of the Sand Dunes states that God has commanded people to honour their parents and describes the mother's hardship (46:15).

The Book's treatment of the obligation includes the actions and the speech by which it is fulfilled. The sūrah of the Night Journey describes the prayer for mercy upon them and the humility with which they are to be treated (17:24). The sūrah of Luqmān states that the obligation holds even when the parents are idolaters, with the qualification that the believer does not obey them in their association of partners with God (31:15). The verse in front of us states the command in its shortest form, and the fuller passages supply its content.

**The Command About Children**

Do not kill your children for fear of poverty. We provide for you and for them. The command takes up the practice the sūrah condemned in its account of the audience's religion, where the killing of children was described as the work of the evil associates (6:137) and where those who did it were declared lost (6:140). The verse in front of us states the prohibition and gives the reason, which addresses the fear that motivated the practice.

The Book's parallel passage in the sūrah of the Night Journey gives the same command with the reason that God provides for them (17:31), and the sūrah of the Cow includes the killing of children among the offences of the children of Israel. The sentence in front of us supplies the remedy for the fear the Book identifies: the provision is guaranteed for the parents and the children together, and the practice rested on an assumption about the future that the community had no grounds for making.

**The Command About Indecency and Life**

Do not come near indecencies, openly or secretly. Do not take a human life, made sacred by Allah, except with legal right. The two commands address the conduct of the individual and the safety of the community, and the Book attaches the same prohibitions to the covenant. The sūrah of the Heights records the Prophet's declaration that his Lord has forbidden open and secret indecencies, sinfulness, unjust aggression, and the association of others with God (7:33), and the sūrah of the Night Journey gives the command against taking life with the provision about the rights of the heirs (17:33).

The Book's phrase about the sacredness of life appears in the sūrah of the Night Journey and in the sūrah of the Distinguisher, where the servants of the Most Compassionate are described as those who do not invoke any other god, do not take a life except with right, and do not commit adultery (25:68). The verse in front of us states the two prohibitions in the same order, and the exception for legal right is the same in each passage.

The next verse continues the recitation with the commands about the wealth of the orphan, the giving of full measure, the requirement of justice in speech, and the fulfilment of the covenant with God, and it closes with the same formula addressed to mindfulness (6:152). The two verses together form the sūrah's statement of what the covenant requires, and the verse that follows them identifies the whole as the straight path (6:153).

The Book has a parallel passage in the sūrah of the Night Journey, where God lists the commands given to the children of Israel and to the Prophet's community (17:22–39).

---

## Verse 6:152

> And do not come near the wealth of the orphan—unless intending to enhance it—until they attain maturity. Give full measure and weigh with justice. We never require of any soul more than what it can afford. Whenever you speak, maintain justice—even regarding a close relative. And fulfil your covenant with Allah. This is what He has commanded you, so perhaps you will be mindful

**The Wealth of the Orphan**

And do not come near the wealth of the orphan, unless intending to enhance it, until they attain maturity. The command continues the recitation, and it protects the property of children who have no one to defend them. The exception is precise: a guardian may handle the property if he intends to improve it, and he must hand it over when the child reaches the age at which he can manage it.

In the sūrah of the Women, the guardians are instructed to test the orphans until they reach marriageable age and then to release their property to them (4:6), and it warns against consuming it. The same command appears in the sūrah of the Night Journey in words close to those of this verse, with the addition of the command to fulfil every covenant (17:34).

**The Giving of Full Measure**

Give full measure and weigh with justice. The command governs the conduct of trade, which was the foundation of the audience's economy, and the Book attaches the same requirement to its accounts of the earlier communities.

**The Limit of Obligation**

We never require of any soul more than what it can afford. The sentence follows the commands and states the measure of the obligation, and the Book uses it at the end of passages that lay down requirements. The long passage of legislation in the sūrah of the Cow closes with the declaration that God does not require of any soul more than it can afford, in a verse that also records the community's prayer for forgiveness and mercy (2:286). The sūrah of the Divorce tells the person of limited resources to provide according to what God has given him (65:7).

**Justice in Speech**

Whenever you speak, maintain justice, even about a close relative. The command governs testimony and speech, and the Book attaches the same requirement to the community's dealings with one another and with their opponents. In the sūrah of the Women, the believers are told to stand firm for justice as witnesses for God even if it is against themselves, their parents, or their close relatives (4:135). The sūrah of the Table commands them to bear true testimony and not to let hatred of a people drive them into injustice (5:8).

In the sūrah of the Night Journey, the believer is commanded to give in full when he measures and to weigh with an even balance, which is described as fairest and best in the end (17:35). The sūrah of the Most Compassionate commands the community to weigh with justice and not to give short measure (55:9); and the sūrah of the Defrauders opens with a condemnation of those who take full measure for themselves and give short measure to others (83:1–3).

The same command stands in the same position in the list of the sūrah of the Night Journey (17:34), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God loves those who honour their trusts and are mindful of Him (3:76).

---

## Verse 6:153

> Indeed, that is My Path—perfectly straight. So follow it and do not follow other ways, for they will lead you away from His Way. This is what He has commanded you, so perhaps you will be conscious ˹of Allah˺.”

**The Commands Gathered Into a Path**

Indeed, that is My Path, perfectly straight. The sentence gathers the commands of the two preceding verses into a single image, and the demonstrative reaches back over the whole recitation. What the sūrah has listed — the worship of God alone, the honouring of parents, the protection of children, the avoidance of indecency, the sanctity of life, the care of orphans, honest measure, just speech. The keeping of covenants — is presented as one road rather than as a set of separate rules.

The image has been prepared throughout the chapter. The sūrah asked the Prophet to say that his Lord has guided him to a straight path in the verse that will appear shortly after this one (6:161). Its earlier passage on guidance described the path of the Lord as straight and stated that the signs had been made clear to those who are mindful (6:126). The Book's use of the image at the opening of the Qur'an, where the community prays for guidance upon the straight path (1:6), gives the metaphor its place in the community's daily worship.

**The Command to Follow and the Warning**

In the sūrah of the Family of Imran, the community is commanded to hold firmly to the rope of God and not to be divided (3:103), and the sūrah of the Romans describes those who have divided their faith and split into sects, each rejoicing in what they have (30:32). The sūrah of the Cattle itself states that the Prophet bears no responsibility for those who have divided their religion into sects (6:159), and the verse in front of us supplies the command that keeps the community from that condition.

The verse closes the passage that began with the demand for witnesses and the recitation of what God has forbidden (6:150–152). The sūrah's survey of the audience's system is finished, its argument against the audience's excuses is complete, and the requirements of the covenant have been stated. What remains in the chapter is the closing section, which returns to the Book itself and to the testimony that the earlier scriptures bear to it.

The verse that follows this one turns to Moses and the Scripture given to him, describing it as complete for those who do good, detailed in everything, and a guide and a mercy (6:154). The transition from the path to the earlier scripture is deliberate: the way that the community is commanded to follow is the same way that the earlier books marked out.

The verses that follow describe the blessing of the Book that has been revealed and answer the excuses of the people who claimed that the scriptures had been given only to the communities before them (6:155–157). The verse in front of us supplies the image that unites the sūrah's commandments, and the reader who takes it up is placed on the road at the end of the argument.

---

## Verse 6:154

> Additionally, We gave Moses the Scripture, completing the favour upon those who do good, detailing everything, and as a guide and a mercy, so perhaps they would be certain of the meeting with their Lord

**The Scripture Given to Moses**

Additionally, We gave Moses the Scripture. The verse turns from the path of the community to the revelation given to the earlier prophet, and it describes that scripture in a series of phrases. The Book refers to the giving of Moses' scripture in many places: the sūrah of the Cow says that God gave Moses the Scripture and the criterion (2:53), the sūrah of the Believers says that He gave Moses the Scripture so that his people might be guided (23:49). The sūrah of the Story describes the book given to Moses after the destruction of the earlier generations as an insight, a guide, and a mercy (28:43).

**Detailing Everything**

Detailing everything. The phrase states the comprehensiveness of the guidance given, and the Book uses the same description for its own revelation. The sūrah of Fussilat calls the Book one whose verses are perfectly explained, a Qur'an in Arabic for people who know (41:3), and the sūrah of the Cattle states in its own account of the Book that God has explained what He has forbidden (6:119). The phrase in this verse belongs to that family of statements, and it presents the earlier scripture as complete for the purposes it was given to serve.

The verse follows the recitation of the covenant and the identification of the commands as the straight path (6:151–153), and it precedes the passage that describes the Book given to the Prophet and answers the excuses of the people who claimed that revelation had passed them by (6:155–157). The two revelations are placed side by side, and the effect is to present the Qur'an as the continuation of a provision that the sūrah's audience already knew.

The Book's use of the earlier scriptures in its arguments appears throughout the sūrah. The sūrah has appealed to the Book of Moses as evidence that God reveals to human beings (6:91), it has described the earlier communities' reactions to their scriptures (6:146), and it has stated that the people who were given the earlier books recognise the Prophet's message. What this verse supplies is the description of the earlier scripture itself.

The next verse declares that the Book revealed to the Prophet is blessed and commands the community to follow it and be mindful so that they may be shown mercy (6:155). The sentence follows the account of the earlier scripture and presents the new revelation as the same kind of gift, and the verses that follow state the excuses that the Book removes.

The passage that continues from here describes the people who claimed that the scriptures had been given only to two groups before them and that they had not studied them, and it states that the clear proof, the guide, and the mercy have now come to them (6:156–157). The verse in front of us prepares that argument, and it does so by describing the earlier scripture in the terms that the new revelation will claim for itself.

---

## Verse 6:155

> This is a blessed Book We have revealed. So follow it and be mindful ˹of Allah˺, so you may be shown mercy

**The Blessed Book**

This is a blessed Book We have revealed. The sentence declares the Book that the community has received, and it uses the same description the sūrah applied earlier in the chapter when it said that the Book sent down to the Prophet is blessed and confirms what came before it (6:92). The description appears again in the sūrah of the Prophets, where the revelation is called a blessed reminder that has been sent down. In the sūrah of Ṣād, where God calls the Book revealed to the Prophet blessed and states its purpose: that people may contemplate its verses and that the people of understanding may be reminded (38:29).

The word carries the sense of benefit and increase, and the Book's use of it in these passages attaches it to the guidance the revelation provides. The verse in front of us states that the Book is blessed and then draws the practical consequence in its command.

**The Command to Follow**

So follow it and be mindful of Allah. The sentence attaches two requirements to the description: the community is to follow what the Book contains, and it is to be mindful of God in doing so. The sūrah has used the same pair in its account of the covenant, where the commands were gathered into a path with the instruction to follow and the appeal to consciousness of God (6:153), and the verse in front of us repeats the arrangement for the Book itself.

The Book's instructions to follow what has been revealed appear at several points in this sūrah. The Prophet was told to follow what was revealed to him from his Lord (6:106), and the sūrah of the Cattle states that the community is commanded to follow the Book and to be mindful. The two commands in the sentence are the practical form of the description that precedes them.

**The Mercy That Is the Purpose**

So you may be shown mercy. The sentence states the purpose of the command, and the Book attaches mercy to the guidance of the revelation throughout. The sūrah has described the Book given to Moses as a guide and a mercy (6:154), and the next verse will describe the revelation that has come to the audience as a clear proof, a guide, and a mercy (6:157). The phrase in front of us states the outcome the Book's guidance is meant to produce.

The Book's accounts of the earlier revelations use the same language. The sūrah of the Story calls the book of Moses an insight, a guide, and a mercy, and the sūrah of the Bee describes the Book sent down as guidance, mercy, and good news for those who submit (16:89). The verse in front of us belongs to that family of statements, and it places the community that receives the Qur'an within the same provision.

**Why the Verse Is Stated After the Earlier Scripture**

The sūrah has argued for that continuity from its earlier verses, describing the Book as confirming what came before it and stating that God has not neglected anything in the Record (6:38). The verse in front of us supplies the command that follows from the description, and the passage that follows states the excuses that the Book's arrival removes.

---

## Verse 6:156

> You ˹pagans˺ can no longer say, “Scriptures were only revealed to two groups before us and we were unaware of their teachings.”

**The Two Groups Named**

The Book refers to the earlier communities in several passages, and it acknowledges the scriptures they were given. The sūrah of the Cow mentions the believers, the Jews, the Christians. The Sabians, and states that those who believe in God and the Last Day and do right will have their reward (2:62); the sūrah of the Table gives the same statement (5:69); and the sūrah of the Cattle itself has described the Book given to Moses and the guidance it contained (6:154). The verse in front of us refers to those communities as the recipients of the earlier revelations.

The audience's position in Makkah was not that of a people without contact with those communities. The Book appeals repeatedly to the knowledge of the People of the Book and describes their recognition of the Prophet's message (6:20; 2:146), and its argument in the passage on revelation relied on the presence of the earlier scriptures in the region. The claim that the audience had been unaware of those teachings was therefore an excuse rather than a description of its situation.

**The Language of the Revelation**

The Book's answer to the excuse is supplied by the revelation's own language, and the sūrahs describe the Qur'an as an Arabic scripture in several places. The sūrah of Joseph says that God sent the revelation down as an Arabic Qur'an so that the people might understand (12:2). The sūrah of the Ornament says that it has been made an Arabic Qur'an so that the audience might understand (43:3); and the sūrah of the Poets describes the revelation as coming in a clear Arabic tongue (26:195). The verse in front of us states that the excuse of unfamiliarity no longer applies.

The Book's treatment of the charge that the Prophet was taught by others uses the same argument. The sūrah of the Bee records the accusation and answers that the person the accusers have in mind speaks a foreign tongue while the revelation is in eloquent Arabic (16:103). The verses in front of us belong to that line of argument, and they state that the Book's arrival in the audience's own language settles the question.

**What the Verse Says About the Earlier Scriptures**

The sūrah's argument about the earlier revelations runs through the chapter. It appealed to the Book of Moses as evidence that God reveals to human beings (6:91), it described the restrictions imposed upon the Jewish communities (6:146), and it stated that the people who were given the earlier books recognise the Prophet's message. The verse in front of us states the conclusion the audience is to draw from all of it.

The next verse completes the passage with the declaration that there has now come from the Lord a clear proof, a guide, and a mercy, and with the question of who does greater wrong than those who reject the signs of God and turn away from them (6:157).

The sūrah's closing section continues from there with the warning about the signs whose arrival is awaited and with the statement that belief will not benefit those who did not believe before (6:158).

---

## Verse 6:157

> Nor can you say, “If only the Scriptures had been revealed to us, we would have been better guided than they.” Now there has come to you from your Lord a clear proof—a guide and mercy. Who then does more wrong than those who deny Allah’s revelations and turn away from them? We will reward those who turn away from Our revelations with a dreadful punishment for turning away

**The Clear Proof, the Guide, and the Mercy**

Now there has come to you from your Lord a clear proof — a guide and mercy. The sentence states what the audience has received, and the three terms it uses describe the revelation in the language the sūrah has already applied to the earlier scriptures. The Book of Moses was called a guide and a mercy (6:154), and the Book revealed to the Prophet was called blessed in the verse before this one (6:155). What the sentence states is that the proof has come from the Lord and that it carries both guidance and mercy.

The Book describes its own revelation in the same terms elsewhere. The sūrah of the Table opens its address to the People of the Book with the announcement that a light and a clear Book have come from God (5:15), and the sūrah of the Story calls the book given to Moses an insight, a guide, and a mercy. It belongs to that family of statements, and it places the Qur'an within the same provision that the earlier communities had received.

**The Question About the Greatest Wrong**

Who then does more wrong than those who deny Allah's revelations and turn away from them? The question is the sūrah's repeated formula, and it appears in the passages about fabricating lies against God (6:21), about claiming to have received revelation (6:93), and about inventing dietary rules (6:144). The form in front of us attaches the formula to the denial of the signs and to the act of turning away from them.

The Book uses the same question in other sūrahs. The sūrah of the Adoration asks who does more wrong than the person who is reminded of the revelations of his Lord and then turns away from them. It states that God will take retribution from the guilty (32:22); the sūrah of the Cave asks the same question about those who turn away from the signs and forget what their hands have sent ahead (18:57). The offence is stated here in the context of the Book that has just come.

**The Punishment Attached to Turning Away**

We will reward those who turn away from Our revelations with a dreadful punishment for turning away. The sentence states the consequence, and it attaches the punishment to the act by which the offence is committed. The Book's account of recompense follows the conduct it answers, and the sūrah of Consultation states that the reward of an evil deed is its equivalent (42:40).

The Book's statements about turning away from the signs run through the sūrah. It has described the people who turn away from the signs as unable to see them (6:46), it has described the condition of those whose hearts are turned aside after their first refusal (6:110), and it has stated that the evil of the people who turn away will be answered. The arrangement is stated here in its final form, and the phrase for turning away names the offence twice in a single sentence.

The next verse asks whether the audience is waiting for the coming of the angels, or for the Lord Himself, or for some of the major signs. It states that on the day the signs arrive the belief of those who did not believe earlier will not benefit them, before instructing the Prophet to say that both sides are waiting (6:158). The passage continues the sūrah's treatment of the demand for signs, and it states the point at which the opportunity closes.

The sūrah then moves to the verses about the divisions among the communities and about the reward of deeds (6:159–160), and it closes with the Prophet's declaration that God has guided him to the straight path (6:161). This sentence stands at the beginning of that closing movement, and the reader who has followed the argument now has the whole case before him.

---

## Verse 6:158

> Are they awaiting the coming of the angels, or your Lord ˹Himself˺, or some of your Lord’s ˹major˺ signs? On the Day your Lord’s signs arrive, belief will not benefit those who did not believe earlier or those who did no good through their faith. Say, “Keep waiting! We too are waiting.”

**The Three Things Awaited**

Are they awaiting the coming of the angels, or your Lord Himself, or some of your Lord's major signs? The verse names the events the audience treated as the conditions for their belief, and the three items correspond to the demands the sūrah has recorded. The demand for the angels was stated in the earlier passage about the signs (6:8), and the demand that God come to them was recorded among the challenges the audience made (6:34 context); the major signs are the events that precede the judgement.

The Book records the same kind of waiting in other sūrahs. The sūrah of the Cow asks whether the people are waiting for God to come to them in the shade of clouds along with the angels. It states that the matter would then be settled (2:210). The sūrah of the Bee asks whether they are awaiting the coming of the angels or the command of the Lord. It notes that the earlier communities did the same (16:33); and the sūrah of Muhammad asks whether they are only waiting for the Hour to take them by surprise once its signs have come (47:18). The verse in front of us states the audience's position in the form of a question.

**The Closing of the Opportunity**

On the Day your Lord's signs arrive, belief will not benefit those who did not believe earlier or those who did no good through their faith. The sentence states that the arrival of the signs will not produce the outcome the audience expected, and the Book attaches the same rule to the moment of death. The sūrah of the Women says that repentance is not accepted from those who persist in sin until death approaches and they then declare their belief (4:18), and the sūrah of Yūnus records the cry of Pharaoh when he was drowning and the answer that his declaration came too late (10:90–91).

**What the Verse Says About the Demand for Signs**

The verse closes the sūrah's long treatment of the demand for signs, which began with the oaths sworn by the audience (6:109) and ran through the statement that even the angels and the dead would not convince them (6:111) and the account of the enemies appointed to every prophet (6:112). The verse in front of us states what the audience is waiting for and what the arrival of those things will and will not accomplish.

The Book's answer to the demand has been consistent throughout the passage. The signs are with God (6:109), the hearts of those who refuse are turned aside (6:110), and the request for a demonstration that would compel belief is answered by the statement that belief is accepted only while it is free. The sentence in front of us supplies the final element of that answer, and the passage that follows turns to the divisions among the communities.

The verse follows the account of the Book's arrival, the removal of the audience's excuse, and the statement of the punishment for turning away (6:155–157), and it precedes the verses about the divisions among the people of the earlier books (6:159) and the reward of deeds (6:160). The sūrah's closing section therefore moves from the Book to the response to it, and from the response to the reckoning that follows.

---

## Verse 6:159

> Indeed, you ˹O Prophet˺ are not responsible whatsoever for those who have divided their faith and split into sects. Their judgment rests only with Allah. And He will inform them of what they used to do

**The Divisions Among the Communities**

Indeed, you are not responsible whatsoever for those who have divided their faith and split into sects. The verse turns from the audience's waiting to the condition of the communities that had received the earlier scriptures, and it states that the Prophet bears no responsibility for them. The sūrah has been addressing a city in which the divisions of the earlier communities were known, and the sentence places those divisions outside the messenger's charge.

The Book's warnings against division appear in several passages. In the sūrah of the Family of Imran, the community is told not to be like those who split into sects and differed after clear proofs had come to them. The verse states that they will have a great punishment (3:105). The sūrah of the Romans describes those who divided their faith and split into sects, each rejoicing in what they have (30:32); and the sūrah of Consultation states that the people of the earlier books did not split into sects out of mutual envy until knowledge had come to them (42:14). The sūrah of the Cattle itself warned against following the other ways that branch off from the path (6:153), and the sentence here describes the condition of the people who took those ways.

**The Limit of the Prophet's Responsibility**

The phrase not responsible whatsoever repeats the limit the sūrah has set in several places. The Prophet was told that he is neither keeper nor maintainer over the people who deny his message (6:107), that he is not a keeper over them (6:104), and that his duty is to warn and to remind. The sentence in front of us applies the limit to a particular case, and it does so in the strongest form the language allows.

The Book gives the same assurance to the Prophet in its accounts of the earlier messengers. In the sūrah of the Bee, he is told to invite people to the way of the Lord with wisdom and good instruction. The verse states that his Lord knows best who has strayed and who is guided (16:125), and the sūrah of the Overwhelming Event states that he is only a reminder and not a controller over the people (88:21–22). The verse in front of us belongs to that family of statements, and the divisions it describes were the work of the communities themselves.

**The Judgment Left With God**

Their judgment rests only with Allah. The sentence states where the matter is settled, and the Book uses the same referral in its accounts of the disputes among the people of the earlier scriptures. In the sūrah of the Pilgrimage, God states that He will judge between the people on the day of judgement about their differences (22:69), and the sūrah of the Cow states that God will judge between them about what they differed about (2:113). The verse in front of us states the referral about the divisions described in its first sentence.

The verse follows the passage about the Book's arrival and the response that will not benefit those who waited (6:155–158), and it precedes the statement about the reward of the good deed and the punishment of the evil deed (6:160). The sūrah's closing section therefore moves from the Book to the divisions, and from the divisions to the accountability of the individual.

The verses that follow describe the reward that is multiplied and the punishment that is not, and the passage ends with the declaration of the Prophet about the path of his Lord (6:161). The verse in front of us supplies the contrast between the communities that divided and the individual who will stand before God with his own deed, and the arrangement gives the reader both warnings at once.

---

## Verse 6:160

> Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded tenfold. But whoever comes with a bad deed will be punished for only one. None will be wronged

**The Reward of the Good Deed**

Whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded tenfold. The verse states the measure of the reward, and the Book's accounts of recompense attach a multiplication to the good deed in several places. The sūrah of the Cow compares the spending of wealth in the cause of God to a grain that sprouts into seven ears, each ear containing a hundred grains. It states that God multiplies as He wills for whom He wills (2:261). The sūrah of the Women says that God never wrongs anyone by an atom's weight and that He multiplies the good deed and gives a great reward (4:40); and the sūrah of the Stories says that whoever comes with a good deed will be rewarded with what is better (28:84).

The multiplication is the Book's statement about the generosity of the reward, and it is stated alongside the exactness of the punishment. Both measures are given here in a single sentence, and the arrangement presents the accounting as more generous than it is strict.

**The Punishment of the Bad Deed**

But whoever comes with a bad deed will be punished for only one. The sentence states the limit of the punishment, and the Book's statements about justice repeat the same principle. The sūrah of the Women states that God wrongs no one by an atom's weight (4:40); the sūrah of Fussilat says that whoever does good does so for his own benefit and whoever does evil does so to his own loss. That the Lord is never unjust to His servants (41:46); and the sūrah of the Cave describes the record of deeds presented on the day of judgement with the declaration that the Lord wrongs no one (18:49).

The Book's presentation of the punishment as measured and limited belongs to its account of God's justice. The multiplication attached to the good deed is not paralleled on the other side, and the sentence in front of us states the difference explicitly. The reader is left with the two measures stated side by side.

The verse stands between the statement about the divisions of the communities (6:159) and the declaration of the Prophet about the path to which he has been guided (6:161). The three verses form the sūrah's closing movement: the schisms are left to God, the individual's deed is measured, and the Prophet declares the guidance he has received and the worship he performs.

The verses that follow the declaration include the Prophet's statement that his prayer, his worship, his life, and his death belong to God (6:162), the declaration that God has no partner (6:163), and the final verse about the ranks in which people have been placed (6:165). What this sentence states is the measure that underlies the whole closing passage, and the reader is left with the accounting that the sūrah has been describing since its opening verses.

The next verse gives the Prophet's declaration of the guidance he has received and of the tradition he follows (6:161). The declaration states what the Prophet has received, and it answers the sūrah's account of the audience's religion with a statement of the religion he follows.

---

## Verse 6:161

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Surely my Lord has guided me to the Straight Path, a perfect way, the faith of Abraham, the upright, who was not one of the polytheists.”

**The Declaration of Guidance**

Say, Surely my Lord has guided me to the Straight Path. The verse gives the Prophet a declaration to make, and it returns to the image the sūrah has used for the religion it presents. The path was named when the commands of the covenant were gathered into a single road (6:153), and the Prophet's own position on it is stated here. The declaration is made in the first person, and it places the Prophet among the people who have been guided rather than above them.

The Book's use of the image at the opening of the Qur'an gives it its place in the community's worship, since the prayer for guidance upon the straight path stands at the head of the Book (1:6). The declaration attributed to Jesus in the sūrah of the Family of Imran closes with the statement that this is the straight path (3:51), and the sūrah of the Consultation says that the revelation is a light by which God guides whom He wills to a straight path (42:52). The declaration in front of us belongs to that family of statements.

**The Faith of Abraham**

The faith of Abraham, the upright, who was not one of the polytheists. The verse identifies the tradition the Prophet follows, and the Book attaches the same identification to its commands in several places. The sūrah of the Bee states that God revealed to the Prophet to follow the faith of Abraham, the upright, who was not one of the polytheists (16:123); the sūrah of the Family of Imran commands the same following in the words Allah has declared the truth. Follow the Way of Abraham (3:95); and the sūrah of the Cow answers the claim of the Jews and Christians to exclusive guidance with the declaration that the community follows the faith of Abraham (2:135).

In the sūrah of the Women, the question is asked who is better in faith than the person who submits himself to God, does good, and follows the Way of Abraham, and the verse states that God took Abraham as a friend (4:125). The sūrah of the Bee describes Abraham as a model of excellence, devoted to God, perfectly upright, and not a polytheist, and it adds that he was grateful for God's favours. The sūrah has already told the story of Abraham's argument with his people. The account runs from his questioning of his father and his people about the idols (6:74) to his declaration that he has turned his face toward the One who originated the heavens and the earth and that he is not one of the polytheists (6:79).

**The Tag Attached to Abraham**

The phrase who was not one of the polytheists appears wherever the Book identifies Abraham's faith, and its repetition is deliberate. The sūrah of the Bee attaches it to the description of Abraham as a model of excellence (16:120), the sūrah of the Family of Imran attaches it to the command to follow his way (3:95). The sūrah of the Cow attaches it to the declaration that the community follows his faith (2:135). The phrase states what the following of Abraham excludes.

The verse begins the three-verse declaration that closes the sūrah's teaching about the Prophet's own religion. The declaration states the guidance he has received (6:161), the dedication of his worship, life, and death (6:162), and his disavowal of any partner along with his being the first to submit (6:163). The three verses belong together, and they answer the account of the audience's religion that the sūrah has given.

The chapter's final verses then return to the theme of individual accountability and to the ranks in which people have been placed on the earth (6:164–165). The declaration in front of us supplies the content of the Prophet's own position, and the verses that follow state the terms on which all people are judged.

---

## Verse 6:162

> Say, “Surely my prayer, my worship, my life, and my death are all for Allah—Lord of all worlds

**The Four Terms of the Sentence**

The first term is the prayer, which the sūrah has emphasised throughout its instructions to the community (6:72; 6:92), and the second is the offering or act of worship, which the Book uses for the sacrifice performed in the name of God. The sūrah's dietary passages have dealt with the invocation of names over animals (6:118; 6:121), and the declaration places the practice of sacrifice under the same dedication as the prayer.

**Sacrifice and the Practice of the Audience**

The mention of the offering speaks directly to the audience's religious practice, which the sūrah has described in its passages about the shares assigned to God and to the idols (6:136) and about the animals that were not slaughtered in the Name of God (6:138). The declaration answers those practices by placing the sacrifice where it belongs, and the sūrah of the Abundance states the same requirement in its command to pray and sacrifice to the Lord alone (108:2).

The Book's treatment of the offering elsewhere states what the act is for. The sūrah of the Pilgrimage says that neither the meat nor the blood of the sacrifice reaches God, but that the piety of the person who offers it reaches Him (22:37), and the verse in front of us places the practice inside a declaration that also includes the worshipper's life and death. The four terms together show that the offering is not a transaction but a statement of the direction of a person's existence.

**The Meaning of Sincere Devotion**

The Book's instructions to the Prophet elsewhere command the same sincerity in explicit terms. The sūrah of the Troops tells him to say that he is commanded to worship God, being sincerely devoted to Him alone. To be the first of those who submit (39:11–12); the sūrah of the Clear Proof describes the command given to the people of the earlier books as the worship of God alone with sincere devotion in all uprightness (98:5). The declaration in front of us expresses the same intention, and it does so by naming the things that must be devoted.

The verse that follows adds the denial of any partner and the statement that the Prophet has been commanded and is the first to submit (6:163), and the sūrah then returns to the accountability of every person and to the ranks in which people have been placed (6:164–165). The declaration in front of us supplies the middle term of that closing arrangement, and the reader is left with the direction the revelation asks of him.

The next verse completes the declaration with the statement that God has no partner, that the Prophet is commanded, and that he is the first to submit (6:163). The three elements answer the audience's association of partners with God, state the ground of the Prophet's conduct, and identify the religion by its character of submission.

The sūrah's closing verses then take up the question of accountability from the side of every person, stating that the Lord of everything is the one to whom people return and that differences will be settled before Him (6:164). The verse in front of us supplies the dedication that the message requires, and the verses that follow state the terms on which the dedication is judged.

---

## Verse 6:163

> He has no partner. So I am commanded, and so I am the first to submit.”

**The Command and the Response**

So I am commanded. The sentence states that the Prophet's declaration is not a personal choice but an instruction he has received, and the Book's commands to him elsewhere use the same form. The sūrah of the Troops tells him to say that he is commanded to worship God, being sincerely devoted to Him alone. That he is commanded to be the first of those who submit (39:11–12); the sūrah of the Cattle itself has placed a series of commands before him in its earlier verses, from following what is revealed (6:106) to declaring that God's argument is conclusive (6:149).

**The First to Submit**

The Book's word for submission describes the surrender of the person to God's command, and the religion itself is given that name in other passages. The sūrah of the Family of Imran states that God's only way is Islam (3:19), and the sūrah of the Table records God's declaration that He has chosen submission as the religion of the community (5:3). The Prophet's statement that he is the first of those who submit places him at the head of the people who accept that religion.

The Book's use of the phrase elsewhere appears in the commands given to the Prophet. The sūrah of the Troops joins the command to be the first of those who submit to the command of sincere worship (39:12), and the sūrah of the Cattle's opening verses record the Prophet's declaration that he is commanded to be the first of those who submit. The precedence is a precedence in time and in commitment, and the community that follows him is described in the Book as the people who submit.

**Submission as the Religion's Name**

The name the verse gives the religion is the one the Book uses for the response of the person who accepts the message. The sūrah of the Cow describes Abraham and Ishmael praying that their descendants be a community submitting to God (2:128 context), and the sūrah of the Family of Imran states that whoever follows a religion other than submission will not have it accepted of him (3:85). The Book's use of the term covers the conduct as well as the confession, since the person who submits is the one who acts on what has been sent down.

The next verse gives the Prophet the question about whether he should seek a lord other than God while God is the Lord of everything. It states that no one reaps except what he sows, that no soul bears another's burden, and that all people will return to their Lord and be informed of their differences (6:164). The passage turns from the Prophet's declaration to the accountability of the audience, and the two themes are joined by the statement about the Lord of everything.

The sūrah then closes with the verse about the successors placed on the earth and the ranks in which they have been set, and with the declaration that the Lord is swift in punishment and All-Forgiving, Most Merciful (6:165). The verse in front of us completes the Prophet's declaration, and the reader is left with the message in its final form: one Lord, one worship, and a submission that the messenger himself has embraced.

The Book's statement of God's unity elsewhere gives the same negation in the sūrah of Sincerity, where God is declared one and indivisible and where the denial of offspring follows (112:1–3).

---

## Verse 6:164

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Should I seek a lord other than Allah while He is the Lord of everything?” No one will reap except what they sow. No soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another. Then to your Lord is your return, and He will inform you of your differences

**The Question About Another Lord**

Say, Should I seek a lord other than Allah while He is the Lord of everything? The verse gives the Prophet a question to put to the audience, and the question returns to the ground on which the whole sūrah has stood. The Book's opening command to the Prophet in this sūrah was to declare that he would not take any guardian other than the One who originated the heavens and the earth (6:14), and the sentence in front of us states the same position in the form of a question.

The Book uses the question in other sūrahs as well. In the sūrah of the Troops, the audience is asked whether it is urging the Prophet to worship anyone other than God. It is addressed as ignorant (39:64); the sūrah of the Forgiver records the Prophet's declaration that he has been forbidden to worship what they worship besides God since the clear proofs have come to him.

**No One Reaps Except What He Sows**

No one will reap except what they sow. The sentence states the arrangement of the reckoning, and the Book attaches the same principle to the accountability of every person. The sūrah of the Star declares that each person will have only what he endeavoured toward (53:39), and the sūrah of Fussilat says that whoever does good does so for his own benefit and whoever does evil does so to his own loss (41:46). The sūrah of the Cattle itself has stated the two measures of reward and punishment in the verse before this passage (6:160).

**No Soul Bears Another's Burden**

No soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another. The sentence states the same principle in the language of carrying, and the Book repeats it in several places. It is stated in the sūrah of the Star that no soul burdened with sin will bear the burden of another (53:38). The sūrah of Fāṭir says the same and adds that a soul crying out for help with its burden will not be relieved, even by a close relative (35:18). The sūrah of the Troops states that no bearer of burdens bears another's (39:7); and the sūrah of the Night Journey gives the same statement in its account of the responsibility of every person (17:15).

The Book's rejection of substitution extends to the intercession that the audience expected from its idols. The sūrah has already stated that no protector or intercessor besides God will help the people who are warned (6:51) and that the intercessors the audience claimed will be absent on the day of judgment (6:94). The sentence in front of us states the principle that underlies those passages, and the sūrah of the Cow's warning about the day when no soul will be of help to another adds the same list of things that will not be accepted (2:48).

**The Return and the Settlement of Differences**

Then to your Lord is your return, and He will inform you of your differences. The sentence closes the verse with the statement of the outcome, and the Book uses the same formula in its accounts of the disputes among the people of the earlier books. The sūrah of the Table states that God will inform people of what they differed about (5:48), the sūrah of the Pilgrimage says that God will judge between them on the day of judgement about their differences (22:69). The sūrah of the Troops states that their differences will be settled before their Lord.

The sūrah's own treatment of the divisions among the communities appears in the previous passage, where the judgment of those who split into sects is left with God (6:159). The sentence in front of us completes that arrangement by stating that the differences which occupied the people of the earlier books will be presented and explained, and it leaves the community with the revelation it has received.

---

## Verse 6:165

> He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth and elevated some of you in rank over others, so He may test you with what He has given you. Surely your Lord is swift in punishment, but He is certainly All-Forgiving, Most Merciful

**The Successors Placed on the Earth**

He is the One Who has placed you as successors on earth. The verse opens the sūrah's final statement with the theme of succession, and the Book uses the same description in several places. The sūrah of Fāṭir states that God has placed people as successors on the earth and that whoever disbelieves bears the burden of his own disbelief (35:39). The sūrah of the Cow records God's announcement to the angels that He would place a successive authority on the earth (2:30); and the sūrah of the Cattle itself told the Prophet earlier that God could do away with the audience and replace them with whoever He wills (6:133). The sentence in front of us states the arrangement in the form of a description of the audience's position.

**The Elevation in Ranks**

And elevated some of you in rank over others. The sentence states the differences that exist among people in the present life, and the Book attaches the same fact to the distribution of livelihood in several passages. According to the sūrah of the Ornament, God has distributed people's livelihood in this world and raised some of them in rank above others so that they may serve one another, and the passage asks whether it is the audience who distribute the mercy of their Lord (43:32). The sūrah of the Cattle itself states at its close that God has raised some above others in rank, and it has already stated that the ranks of the Hereafter are assigned according to deeds (6:132).

**The Test in What Has Been Given**

So He may test you with what He has given you. The clause states the purpose for which people have been placed on the earth and given their differences, and the Book attaches testing to the gifts of the present life in several passages. In the sūrah of the Kingdom, God states that He created death and life to test which of the people is best in deeds (67:2). The sūrah of the Cave says that God has made what is on the earth an adornment for it to test which of them is best in deeds (18:7); and the sūrah of the Cattle says that God has tested some people by means of others (6:53). The sentence in front of us states the purpose in the plainest terms.

**Swift in Punishment**

Surely your Lord is swift in punishment. The attribute opens the sūrah's closing declaration, and the Book attaches it to the warnings about the communities that persisted in denial. God's declaration in the sūrah of the Heights is that He would send against the people of the earlier books those who would make them suffer terribly until the day of judgment, and that verse closes with the statement that the Lord is swift in punishment and All-Forgiving, Most Merciful (7:167). The sūrah of the Thunder states the same pair in its answer to those who ask for the torment to be hastened before the grace (13:6), and the sūrah of Fussilat names the Lord of forgiveness and painful punishment (41:43).

Within the sūrah of the Heights, the two names stand together at the end of its warning (7:167). The sūrah of the Troops tells those who have exceeded the limits against their own souls not to despair of the mercy of God, for He is the All-Forgiving, the Most Merciful (39:53); the Book's habit of placing mercy alongside severity is one of its most consistent features.

---
